\section{Proof of Theorem~{\thmMopFunctionalForms} (DMOP-$\alpha$ Functional Forms)}


\label{appendix:functional}


Theorem~{\thmMopFunctionalForms} follows immediately as a consequence of the following results, Lemmas \ref{lem:mop-1-formula} and \ref{lem:mop-0-formula}.
Here, an $A$ superscript is used to denote ancestral trajectories, so that $x_{1:n,j}^{A, P,\theta}$ contains the history of the prediction particle $x_{n,j}^{P,\theta}$ at observation times $\seq{1}{n}$.
Similarly, $g_{i,j}^{A,P,\theta}$ is the ancestral weight of $x_{n,j}^{P,\theta}$ at observation time $i$.

\begin{lem}
\label{lem:mop-1-formula}
Write $\nabla_\theta \hat\ell^\alpha(\theta)$ for the DMOP-$\alpha$ gradient estimate (or, equivalently, the gradient of MOP-$\alpha$ when $\theta=\phi$).
Consider the case where we use the after-resampling conditional likelihood estimate so that $\hat\lik(\theta) = \prod_{n=1}^N L_n^{A, \theta, \alpha}$.
When $\alpha=1$,
    \begin{equation}
        \nabla_\theta \hat{\ell}^1(\theta) 
        = \frac{1}{J}\sum_{j=1}^J \nabla_\theta \log f_{Y_{1:N}|X_{1:N}}\big(y_{1:N}^* | x_{1:n,j}^{A, F,\theta};\theta\big),
    \end{equation}
    yielding the bootstrap filter estimator of \cite{poyiadjis11} and \cite{scibior21}.
\end{lem}

\iffalse
\begin{proof}[Proof.]
Fix a coordinate $i$ and write
\begin{equation}
Z_i^J=\partial_{\theta_i}\hat\ell^\alpha(\theta),
\qquad
z_i^\alpha=\partial_{\theta_i}\ell^\alpha(\theta).
\label{eq:dmop-direct-error-coordinate-definitions}
\end{equation}
Equation~(\ref{eq:conditional-score-path-decomposition}) and
Equation~(\ref{eq:bounded-realized-measurement-score}) give
\begin{align}
|Z_i^J|
&\leq
\sum_{n=1}^N
\left\{
G'(\theta)+2G'(\theta)\sum_{r=1}^{n-1}\alpha^r
\right\}                                                   \notag\\
&\leq
NG'(\theta)\left\{1+\frac{2}{1-\alpha}\right\}<\infty.
\label{eq:dmop-direct-error-square-integrability}
\end{align}
Thus all random variables used below are square integrable.

We first separate the error created by the time-$n$ multinomial
resampling.  For the prediction paths at time $n$, write
\begin{equation}
p_{n,a}
=
\frac{g_{n,a}^{\phi}}{\sum_{b=1}^Jg_{n,b}^{\phi}},
\qquad a=1,\ldots,J,
\label{eq:dmop-direct-time-n-resampling-probability}
\end{equation}
and put
\begin{align}
R_{n,i}^J
&=
\frac1J\sum_{j=1}^J
\left[
\sum_{s=1}^n\alpha^{n-s}h_{s,i}(\xi_{n,k_j}^P)
-\sum_{a=1}^Jp_{n,a}
\sum_{s=1}^n\alpha^{n-s}h_{s,i}(\xi_{n,a}^P)
\right].
\label{eq:aggregated-terminal-resampling-error}
\end{align}
Conditionally on the prediction paths, the $J$ summands in
Equation~(\ref{eq:aggregated-terminal-resampling-error}) are independent
and centered.  Moreover,
\begin{align}
\operatorname{osc}\left(
\sum_{s=1}^n\alpha^{n-s}h_{s,i}
\right)
&\leq
2G'(\theta)\sum_{s=1}^n\alpha^{n-s}                         \notag\\
&\leq
\frac{2G'(\theta)}{1-\alpha}.
\label{eq:aggregated-terminal-function-oscillation}
\end{align}
It follows that
\begin{align}
\E\left\{(R_{n,i}^J)^2\mid\xi_{n,1:J}^P\right\}
&=
\frac1J
\Var\left\{
\left.
\sum_{s=1}^n\alpha^{n-s}h_{s,i}(\xi_{n,k_1}^P)
\right|\xi_{n,1:J}^P
\right\}                                                   \notag\\
&\leq
\frac{G'(\theta)^2}{J(1-\alpha)^2},
\label{eq:aggregated-terminal-resampling-second-moment}
\end{align}
where the last line uses
$\Var(Y)\leq\operatorname{osc}(Y)^2/4$.

We next derive the local perturbation bound for the predictable part.
Fix $s\leq t\leq n$ and retain the manuscript's $h_{s,i}$.  Define
\begin{align}
q_{t,n}^{1}
&=Q_{t,n}(1),                                                \notag\\
q_{t,n}^{0}
&=
\begin{cases}
Q_{t,n-1}(1),&t<n,\\
1,&t=n,
\end{cases}
&
F_{t,n}
&=\frac{q_{t,n}^{1}}{q_{t,n}^{0}},
\label{eq:dmop-direct-local-future-functions}
\end{align}
and, for a probability measure $\mu$ on prediction paths through time
$t$,
\begin{equation}
C_{t,n}(\mu)
=
\frac{\mu(h_{s,i}q_{t,n}^{1})}{\mu(q_{t,n}^{1})}
-
\frac{\mu(h_{s,i}q_{t,n}^{0})}{\mu(q_{t,n}^{0})}.
\label{eq:dmop-direct-continuation-functional}
\end{equation}
At $t=n$, this is precisely the predictable part of the one-lag
difference:
\begin{align}
C_{n,n}\{m(\xi_n^P)\}
&=
\sum_{a=1}^Jp_{n,a}h_{s,i}(\xi_{n,a}^P)
-m(\xi_n^P)(h_{s,i}),                                      \notag\\
C_{n,n}(\eta_n)
&=
\begin{cases}
\rho_n(h_{s,i})-\rho_{n-1}(h_{s,i}),&s<n,\\
\rho_n(h_{n,i})-\eta_n(h_{n,i}),&s=n.
\end{cases}
\label{eq:dmop-direct-continuation-endpoints}
\end{align}
When several values of $s$ occur in the same display below, write
$C_{t,n}^{(s)}$ for the same functional with the displayed $h_{s,i}$;
the superscript records no additional object.  The conditional-score
decomposition gives the exact identities
\begin{align}
Z_i^J
&=
\sum_{n=1}^{N}R_{n,i}^J
+\sum_{n=1}^{N}m(\xi_n^P)(h_{n,i})
+\sum_{n=1}^{N}\sum_{s=1}^{n}
\alpha^{n-s}C_{n,n}^{(s)}\{m(\xi_n^P)\},
\label{eq:dmop-direct-exact-predictor-decomposition}
\\
z_i^\alpha
&=
\sum_{n=1}^{N}\eta_n(h_{n,i})
+\sum_{n=1}^{N}\sum_{s=1}^{n}
\alpha^{n-s}C_{n,n}^{(s)}(\eta_n).
\label{eq:dmop-direct-exact-population-decomposition}
\end{align}
Indeed, for $s<n$, the $s$th summand of $R_{n,i}^J$ plus
$\alpha^{n-s}C_{n,n}^{(s)}\{m(\xi_n^P)\}$ is
\begin{align}
\alpha^{n-s}
\left\{
m(\xi_n)(h_{s,i})-m(\xi_{n-1})(h_{s,i})
\right\},
\end{align}
while for $s=n$ the three terms are
\begin{align}
&\left[
m(\xi_n)(h_{n,i})
-\sum_{a=1}^Jp_{n,a}h_{n,i}(\xi_{n,a}^P)
\right]
+m(\xi_n^P)(h_{n,i})                                      \notag\\
&\qquad+
\left[
\sum_{a=1}^Jp_{n,a}h_{n,i}(\xi_{n,a}^P)
-m(\xi_n^P)(h_{n,i})
\right]
=m(\xi_n)(h_{n,i}).
\label{eq:dmop-direct-current-predictor-cancellation}
\end{align}
This proves Equation~
(\ref{eq:dmop-direct-exact-predictor-decomposition}); the population
identity follows by the same cancellation.
The recursion for $Q_{t,n}$ and preservation of $h_{s,i}$ after time
$s$ give, for $s<t\leq n$,
\begin{equation}
C_{t,n}\{\Phi_t(\mu)\}=C_{t-1,n}(\mu).
\label{eq:dmop-direct-continuation-consistency}
\end{equation}
Indeed, for $l\in\{0,1\}$,
\begin{align}
\frac{\Phi_t(\mu)(h_{s,i}q_{t,n}^{l})}
{\Phi_t(\mu)(q_{t,n}^{l})}
&=
\frac{\mu\{G_{t-1}M_t(h_{s,i}q_{t,n}^{l})\}}
{\mu\{G_{t-1}M_t(q_{t,n}^{l})\}}                          \notag\\
&=
\frac{\mu\{h_{s,i}G_{t-1}M_t(q_{t,n}^{l})\}}
{\mu\{G_{t-1}M_t(q_{t,n}^{l})\}}                          \notag\\
&=
\frac{\mu(h_{s,i}q_{t-1,n}^{l})}
{\mu(q_{t-1,n}^{l})}.
\end{align}
Subtracting the cases $l=1$ and $l=0$ proves
Equation~(\ref{eq:dmop-direct-continuation-consistency}).

Assumptions {\refAssumpBoundedProcess} and
{\refAssumpBoundedMeasurement} imply, exactly as in Equations~
(\ref{eq:weak-bias-uniform-normalizer-ratio}) and
(\ref{eq:weak-bias-normalized-semigroup-contraction}), that
\begin{align}
\sup_{x,x'}\frac{q_{t,n}^{0}(x)}{q_{t,n}^{0}(x')}
&\leq C,                                                     \notag\\
\underbar G\leq F_{t,n}(x)&\leq\bar G,                    \notag\\
\operatorname{osc}(F_{t,n})
&\leq C\varrho^{n-t}.
\label{eq:dmop-direct-future-function-contraction}
\end{align}
Normalize $q_{t,n}^{0}$ so that $\mu(q_{t,n}^{0})=1$ and write
\begin{equation}
\bar\mu(f)=\mu(q_{t,n}^{0}f).
\end{equation}
Then
\begin{equation}
C_{t,n}(\mu)
=
\frac{\bar\mu(h_{s,i}F_{t,n})}{\bar\mu(F_{t,n})}
-\bar\mu(h_{s,i}).
\label{eq:dmop-direct-continuation-covariance-form}
\end{equation}
For constants $h_0$ and $F_0$ in the respective ranges of $h_{s,i}$
and $F_{t,n}$,
\begin{align}
&\bar\mu(h_{s,i}F_{t,n})
-\bar\mu(h_{s,i})\bar\mu(F_{t,n})                         \notag\\
&\quad=
\bar\mu\{(h_{s,i}-h_0)(F_{t,n}-F_0)\}
-\bar\mu(h_{s,i}-h_0)\bar\mu(F_{t,n}-F_0).
\label{eq:dmop-direct-local-covariance-identity}
\end{align}
Equations~(\ref{eq:bounded-realized-measurement-score}) and
(\ref{eq:dmop-direct-future-function-contraction}) therefore give
\begin{equation}
|C_{t,n}(\mu)|
\leq
CG'(\theta)\varrho^{n-t}.
\label{eq:dmop-direct-local-continuation-size}
\end{equation}

The same cancellation controls the derivative and the quadratic
remainder of the normalized update.  Put
\begin{equation}
\mathcal R_l(\mu)
=\frac{\mu(h_{s,i}q_{t,n}^{l})}{\mu(q_{t,n}^{l})},
\qquad l\in\{0,1\}.
\end{equation}
Direct subtraction of two ratios gives
\begin{align}
\mathcal R_l(\nu)-\mathcal R_l(\mu)
&=(\nu-\mu)D_\mu\mathcal R_l
-
\frac{(\nu-\mu)(q_{t,n}^{l})}
{\nu(q_{t,n}^{l})}
(\nu-\mu)D_\mu\mathcal R_l,
\label{eq:dmop-direct-exact-ratio-expansion}
\\
D_\mu\mathcal R_l(x)
&=
\frac{q_{t,n}^{l}(x)}{\mu(q_{t,n}^{l})}
\{h_{s,i}(x)-\mathcal R_l(\mu)\}.
\label{eq:dmop-direct-ratio-derivative}
\end{align}
Subtracting the case $l=0$ from $l=1$, using
$q_{t,n}^{1}=q_{t,n}^{0}F_{t,n}$, and applying
Equation~(\ref{eq:dmop-direct-local-covariance-identity}) yield
\begin{align}
\operatorname{osc}
\{D_\mu\mathcal R_1-D_\mu\mathcal R_0\}
&\leq
C\operatorname{osc}(h_{s,i})
\operatorname{osc}(F_{t,n})                               \notag\\
&\leq
CG'(\theta)\varrho^{n-t}.
\label{eq:dmop-direct-local-derivative-oscillation}
\end{align}
The denominators in
Equation~(\ref{eq:dmop-direct-exact-ratio-expansion}) are bounded away
from zero.  To spell out the remainder subtraction, put
\begin{equation}
A_l=\frac{(\nu-\mu)(q_{t,n}^{l})}{\nu(q_{t,n}^{l})},
\qquad
B_l=(\nu-\mu)D_\mu\mathcal R_l,
\qquad l\in\{0,1\}.
\end{equation}
The difference of the two exact ratio remainders is
\begin{align}
-A_1B_1+A_0B_0
&=-(A_1-A_0)B_1-A_0(B_1-B_0).
\label{eq:dmop-direct-two-remainder-products}
\end{align}
For $l\in\{0,1\}$, define
\begin{equation}
a_l=\frac{q_{t,n}^{l}}{\mu(q_{t,n}^{l})}.
\label{eq:dmop-direct-local-normalized-denominators}
\end{equation}
Then $\mu(a_l)=1$, and direct algebra gives
\begin{align}
A_l
&=\frac{(\nu-\mu)(a_l)}{\nu(a_l)}
=1-\frac1{\nu(a_l)},                                      \notag\\
A_1-A_0
&=
\frac1{\nu(a_0)}-\frac1{\nu(a_1)}                         \notag\\
&=
\frac{\nu(a_1-a_0)}{\nu(a_0)\nu(a_1)}                    \notag\\
&=
\frac{(\nu-\mu)(a_1-a_0)}{\nu(a_0)\nu(a_1)}.
\label{eq:dmop-direct-local-A-difference}
\end{align}
The normalization preceding
Equation~(\ref{eq:dmop-direct-continuation-covariance-form}) and the
identity $q_{t,n}^{1}=q_{t,n}^{0}F_{t,n}$ give
\begin{equation}
a_1-a_0
=
q_{t,n}^{0}
\left\{
\frac{F_{t,n}}{\bar\mu(F_{t,n})}-1
\right\}.
\label{eq:dmop-direct-local-normalizer-difference}
\end{equation}
Since $\bar\mu(F_{t,n})$ lies between the infimum and supremum of
$F_{t,n}$, Equations~
(\ref{eq:dmop-direct-future-function-contraction}) and
(\ref{eq:dmop-direct-local-normalizer-difference}) imply
\begin{equation}
\operatorname{osc}(a_1-a_0)
\leq C\operatorname{osc}(F_{t,n})
\leq C\varrho^{n-t}.
\label{eq:dmop-direct-local-normalizer-difference-bound}
\end{equation}
The same uniform ratio bounds give
$\inf_l\nu(a_l)\geq C^{-1}$ and
$\operatorname{osc}(a_0)\leq C$.  Moreover,
\begin{align}
B_1-B_0
&=(\nu-\mu)
\{D_\mu\mathcal R_1-D_\mu\mathcal R_0\},                 \notag\\
B_1
&=(\nu-\mu)D_\mu\mathcal R_1,
\end{align}
where Equations~(\ref{eq:dmop-direct-ratio-derivative}) and
(\ref{eq:dmop-direct-local-derivative-oscillation}) give
\begin{align}
\operatorname{osc}(D_\mu\mathcal R_1)
&\leq CG'(\theta),                                        \notag\\
\operatorname{osc}
\{D_\mu\mathcal R_1-D_\mu\mathcal R_0\}
&\leq CG'(\theta)\varrho^{n-t}.
\label{eq:dmop-direct-local-B-factor-bounds}
\end{align}
Substitution into
Equation~(\ref{eq:dmop-direct-two-remainder-products}) now gives, without
discarding either empirical factor,
\begin{align}
|-A_1B_1+A_0B_0|
&\leq
C|(\nu-\mu)(a_1-a_0)|
|(\nu-\mu)D_\mu\mathcal R_1|                              \notag\\
&\quad+
C|(\nu-\mu)(a_0)|
|(\nu-\mu)
\{D_\mu\mathcal R_1-D_\mu\mathcal R_0\}|.
\label{eq:dmop-direct-local-remainder-four-factors}
\end{align}
Centering and dividing each function in
Equation~(\ref{eq:dmop-direct-local-remainder-four-factors}) by its
displayed positive oscillation gives the following bound.  If a displayed
oscillation is zero, its corresponding empirical integral is identically
zero and that product vanishes.
\begin{align}
&\left|
C_{t,n}(\nu)-C_{t,n}(\mu)
-(\nu-\mu)
\{D_\mu\mathcal R_1-D_\mu\mathcal R_0\}
\right|                                                     \notag\\
&\quad\leq
CG'(\theta)\varrho^{n-t}
\sum_{r=1}^{2}
\left| (\nu-\mu)(f_{1,r})\right|
\left| (\nu-\mu)(f_{2,r})\right|,
\label{eq:dmop-direct-local-quadratic-remainder}
\end{align}
where all $f_{1,r}$ and $f_{2,r}$ have
oscillations at most one; their additive centering constants do not
change any $(\nu-\mu)$ integral.  The constants depend only on Assumptions
{\refAssumpBoundedProcess} and {\refAssumpBoundedMeasurement}.
This is the specialization of Equations~(3.1)--(3.4) and
Appendix~A.3 of \citet{delMoral11} to the difference of the two ratios
in Equation~(\ref{eq:dmop-direct-continuation-functional}).

We now write the full local telescope before summing any norms.  Put
\begin{equation}
\eta_t^J=m(\xi_t^P),
\qquad
\mu_t^J=
\begin{cases}
\eta_1,&t=1,\\
\Phi_t(\eta_{t-1}^J),&t\geq2.
\end{cases}
\label{eq:dmop-direct-local-predictor-law}
\end{equation}
In this display, $\Phi_t$ denotes the canonical path-space lift of the
same predictor update: the potential is evaluated at the last location,
multinomial selection chooses a complete path, and $M_t$ appends the new
location.  Its last-coordinate marginal is the predictor update already
defined in Section~\ref{appendix:feynman}.
Thus, conditionally on the preceding prediction cloud, the $J$ particles
forming $\eta_t^J$ are independent with common law $\mu_t^J$.  For the
current predictor term in
Equation~(\ref{eq:dmop-direct-exact-predictor-decomposition}), define
\begin{equation}
H_{t,n}(\mu)=\Phi_{t,n}(\mu)(h_{n,i}),
\qquad 1\leq t\leq n,
\label{eq:dmop-direct-current-predictor-continuation}
\end{equation}
where $\Phi_{n,n}$ is the identity.  For $t<s$, extend the already
defined continuation functional by
\begin{equation}
C_{t,n}^{(s)}(\mu)
=C_{s,n}^{(s)}\{\Phi_{t,s}(\mu)\}.
\label{eq:dmop-direct-backward-continuation-definition}
\end{equation}
Equations~(\ref{eq:dmop-direct-continuation-consistency}) and
(\ref{eq:weak-bias-composition-identity}) then give the exact telescopes
\begin{align}
&H_{n,n}(\eta_n^J)-H_{n,n}(\eta_n)                         \notag\\
&\quad=
\sum_{t=1}^{n}
\left[
H_{t,n}(\eta_t^J)-H_{t,n}(\mu_t^J)
\right],
\label{eq:dmop-direct-current-predictor-telescope}
\\
&C_{n,n}^{(s)}(\eta_n^J)-C_{n,n}^{(s)}(\eta_n)             \notag\\
&\quad=
\sum_{t=1}^{n}
\left[
C_{t,n}^{(s)}(\eta_t^J)-C_{t,n}^{(s)}(\mu_t^J)
\right].
\label{eq:dmop-direct-continuation-full-telescope}
\end{align}
For example, the second term in the $t$th summand of
Equation~(\ref{eq:dmop-direct-continuation-full-telescope}) is
$C_{t-1,n}^{(s)}(\eta_{t-1}^J)$ for $t\geq2$, so consecutive terms
cancel.  At $t=1$, $\mu_1^J=\eta_1$, and the remaining endpoints are
exactly those displayed on the left.  The same verification proves
Equation~(\ref{eq:dmop-direct-current-predictor-telescope}).

We now derive the required future-current bounds from Assumption~
{\refAssumpBoundedProcess}; no contraction condition on the pair in
Equation~(\ref{eq:location-weight-gradient-pair-recursion}) is needed.
For $t\leq n$, define the predictor semigroup
\begin{equation}
\widetilde Q_{t,n}(f)
=
\begin{cases}
f,&t=n,\\
Q_{t,n-1}\{M_n(f)\},&t<n.
\end{cases}
\label{eq:dmop-direct-predictor-semigroup}
\end{equation}
Thus, directly from the definition of $\Phi_{t,n}$,
\begin{equation}
\Phi_{t,n}(\mu)(f)
=
\frac{\mu\widetilde Q_{t,n}(f)}
{\mu\widetilde Q_{t,n}(1)}.
\label{eq:dmop-direct-predictor-semigroup-ratio}
\end{equation}
For $t\leq r<n$, put
\begin{equation}
\widetilde P_{r,r+1}^{(n)}(x,A)
=
\frac{
\displaystyle\int_A
M_{r+1}(x,du)\widetilde Q_{r+1,n}(1)(u)
}{
\displaystyle\int
M_{r+1}(x,du)\widetilde Q_{r+1,n}(1)(u)
},
\label{eq:dmop-direct-predictor-forward-kernel}
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}
\widetilde P_{t,n}^{(n)}
=
\widetilde P_{t,t+1}^{(n)}
\widetilde P_{t+1,t+2}^{(n)}\cdots
\widetilde P_{n-1,n}^{(n)},
\qquad
\widetilde P_{n,n}^{(n)}=I.
\label{eq:dmop-direct-normalized-forward-product}
\end{equation}
The same calculation as Equation~
(\ref{eq:weak-bias-forward-minorization}), now with
$\widetilde Q_{r+1,n}(1)$ in place of $Q_{r+1,n}(1)$, gives
\begin{equation}
\widetilde P_{r,r+1}^{(n)}(x,A)
\geq
\frac{\underbar M}{\bar M}
\frac{
\displaystyle\int_A\widetilde Q_{r+1,n}(1)(u),du
}{
\displaystyle\int\widetilde Q_{r+1,n}(1)(u),du
}.
\label{eq:dmop-direct-predictor-forward-minorization}
\end{equation}
Consequently, every kernel in Equation~
(\ref{eq:dmop-direct-predictor-forward-kernel}) has Dobrushin coefficient
at most $\varrho$, and
\begin{equation}
\frac{\widetilde Q_{t,n}(f)}{\widetilde Q_{t,n}(1)}
=
\widetilde P_{t,n}^{(n)}f.
\label{eq:dmop-direct-predictor-kernel-factorization}
\end{equation}
It follows that
\begin{align}
\operatorname{osc}\{\widetilde P_{t,n}^{(n)}h_{n,i}\}
&\leq
\varrho^{n-t}\operatorname{osc}(h_{n,i})                  \notag\\
&\leq
2G'(\theta)\varrho^{n-t}.
\label{eq:dmop-direct-current-score-forward-contraction}
\end{align}
The first variation of $H_{t,n}$ is
\begin{align}
D_\mu H_{t,n}(x)
&=
\frac{\widetilde Q_{t,n}(1)(x)}
{\mu\widetilde Q_{t,n}(1)}
\left\{
\widetilde P_{t,n}^{(n)}h_{n,i}(x)-H_{t,n}(\mu)
\right\}.
\label{eq:dmop-direct-current-score-first-variation}
\end{align}
Indeed, multiplying the right side by $(\nu-\mu)$ and using
$(\nu-\mu)(1)=0$ gives the linear term obtained by direct subtraction of
$\nu\widetilde Q_{t,n}(h_{n,i})/
\nu\widetilde Q_{t,n}(1)$ and
$\mu\widetilde Q_{t,n}(h_{n,i})/
\mu\widetilde Q_{t,n}(1)$.  Since
$\widetilde Q_{t,n}(1)=Q_{t,n-1}(1)$ for $t<n$, Equation~
(\ref{eq:weak-bias-uniform-normalizer-ratio}) gives
\begin{equation}
0<
\frac{\widetilde Q_{t,n}(1)(x)}
{\mu\widetilde Q_{t,n}(1)}
\leq C.
\label{eq:dmop-direct-current-score-normalizer-bound}
\end{equation}
The expression in braces in Equation~
(\ref{eq:dmop-direct-current-score-first-variation}) is centered inside
the range of $\widetilde P_{t,n}^{(n)}h_{n,i}$.  Therefore Equations~
(\ref{eq:dmop-direct-current-score-forward-contraction}) and
(\ref{eq:dmop-direct-current-score-normalizer-bound}) imply
\begin{equation}
\operatorname{osc}\{D_\mu H_{t,n}\}
\leq
CG'(\theta)\varrho^{n-t}.
\label{eq:dmop-direct-current-score-derivative-bound}
\end{equation}

For $t<s\leq n$, put $\mu_s=\Phi_{t,s}(\mu)$ and
\begin{equation}
d_{s,n}=D_{\mu_s}C_{s,n}^{(s)}.
\end{equation}
Because $h_{s,i}=h_{s,i}(X_s)$ is current and
$q_{s,n}^{0},q_{s,n}^{1}$ are functions of the current location at time
$s$, Equation~(\ref{eq:dmop-direct-ratio-derivative}) shows that
$d_{s,n}$ is also a function of that current location.
Equation~(\ref{eq:dmop-direct-local-derivative-oscillation}), applied at
time $s$, gives
\begin{equation}
\operatorname{osc}(d_{s,n})
\leq
CG'(\theta)\varrho^{n-s}.
\label{eq:dmop-direct-created-score-derivative-bound}
\end{equation}
Differentiating the composition in Equation~
(\ref{eq:dmop-direct-backward-continuation-definition}) gives
\begin{align}
D_\mu C_{t,n}^{(s)}(x)
&=
\frac{\widetilde Q_{t,s}(1)(x)}
{\mu\widetilde Q_{t,s}(1)}
\left\{
\widetilde P_{t,s}^{(s)}d_{s,n}(x)-\mu_s(d_{s,n})
\right\}.
\label{eq:dmop-direct-future-score-chain-rule}
\end{align}
Applying the Dobrushin contraction obtained from Equations~
(\ref{eq:dmop-direct-predictor-forward-minorization}) and
(\ref{eq:dmop-direct-normalized-forward-product}) to $d_{s,n}$, and then
using Equation~(\ref{eq:dmop-direct-created-score-derivative-bound}),
gives
\begin{align}
\operatorname{osc}\{\widetilde P_{t,s}^{(s)}d_{s,n}\}
&\leq
\varrho^{s-t}\operatorname{osc}(d_{s,n})                  \notag\\
&\leq
CG'(\theta)\varrho^{s-t}\varrho^{n-s}                    \notag\\
&=
CG'(\theta)\varrho^{n-t}.
\label{eq:dmop-direct-future-score-two-contractions}
\end{align}
Using the normalizer bound once more in Equation~
(\ref{eq:dmop-direct-future-score-chain-rule}) proves
\begin{align}
\operatorname{osc}\{D_\mu H_{t,n}\}
&\leq CG'(\theta)\varrho^{n-t},                             \notag\\
\operatorname{osc}\{D_\mu C_{t,n}^{(s)}\}
&\leq CG'(\theta)\varrho^{n-t},
\qquad t<s\leq n.
\label{eq:dmop-direct-future-current-derivative-bounds}
\end{align}
For the corresponding quadratic remainders, apply the exact ratio
identity in Equation~(\ref{eq:dmop-direct-exact-ratio-expansion}) to
$H_{t,n}$.  In detail, put
\begin{equation}
a_H=\frac{\widetilde Q_{t,n}(1)}
{\mu\widetilde Q_{t,n}(1)},
\qquad
A_H=\frac{(\nu-\mu)(a_H)}{\nu(a_H)},
\qquad
B_H=(\nu-\mu)D_\mu H_{t,n}.
\end{equation}
Direct subtraction of the two ratios gives
\begin{equation}
H_{t,n}(\nu)-H_{t,n}(\mu)
-(\nu-\mu)D_\mu H_{t,n}
=-A_HB_H.
\label{eq:dmop-direct-current-predictor-exact-remainder}
\end{equation}
The uniform normalizer ratio and
Equation~(\ref{eq:dmop-direct-current-score-derivative-bound}) give
\begin{align}
\operatorname{osc}(a_H)&\leq C,                            \notag\\
\operatorname{osc}(D_\mu H_{t,n})
&\leq CG'(\theta)\varrho^{n-t}.
\end{align}
Since $\mu(a_H)=1$ and $\inf\nu(a_H)\geq C^{-1}$,
\begin{align}
&\left|
H_{t,n}(\nu)-H_{t,n}(\mu)
-(\nu-\mu)D_\mu H_{t,n}
\right|                                                     \notag\\
&\quad\leq
C|(\nu-\mu)(a_H)|
|(\nu-\mu)D_\mu H_{t,n}|.
\label{eq:dmop-direct-current-predictor-remainder-bound}
\end{align}
Thus this quadratic product has coefficient
$CG'(\theta)\varrho^{n-t}$ after its two empirical factors are normalized
by their oscillations.

For $t<s$, the propagated ratios are, explicitly,
\begin{equation}
\mathcal R_{l,t,s}(\mu)
=
\frac{
\mu\widetilde Q_{t,s}(h_{s,i}q_{s,n}^{l})
}{
\mu\widetilde Q_{t,s}(q_{s,n}^{l})
},
\qquad l\in\{0,1\},
\label{eq:dmop-direct-propagated-ratios}
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}
C_{t,n}^{(s)}(\mu)
=
\mathcal R_{1,t,s}(\mu)-\mathcal R_{0,t,s}(\mu).
\label{eq:dmop-direct-propagated-ratio-difference}
\end{equation}
Since $q_{s,n}^{1}=q_{s,n}^{0}F_{s,n}$,
\begin{equation}
\frac{
\widetilde Q_{t,s}(q_{s,n}^{1})
}{
\widetilde Q_{t,s}(q_{s,n}^{0})
}
=
\frac{
\widetilde Q_{t,s}(q_{s,n}^{0}F_{s,n})
}{
\widetilde Q_{t,s}(q_{s,n}^{0})
}.
\label{eq:dmop-direct-propagated-future-ratio}
\end{equation}
The right side is a normalized forward kernel from $t$ to $s$ applied
to $F_{s,n}$.  Repeating the minorization calculation in
Equation~(\ref{eq:dmop-direct-predictor-forward-minorization}), with the
terminal function $q_{s,n}^{0}$, gives
\begin{align}
\operatorname{osc}\left\{
\frac{
\widetilde Q_{t,s}(q_{s,n}^{1})
}{
\widetilde Q_{t,s}(q_{s,n}^{0})
}
\right\}
&\leq
\varrho^{s-t}\operatorname{osc}(F_{s,n})                 \notag\\
&\leq
C\varrho^{s-t}\varrho^{n-s}                              \notag\\
&=
C\varrho^{n-t}.
\label{eq:dmop-direct-propagated-future-ratio-contraction}
\end{align}
Direct subtraction of the two ratios in
Equation~(\ref{eq:dmop-direct-propagated-ratios}) gives
\begin{align}
\mathcal R_{l,t,s}(\nu)-\mathcal R_{l,t,s}(\mu)
&=(\nu-\mu)D_\mu\mathcal R_{l,t,s}
-A_{l,t,s}B_{l,t,s},                                      \notag\\
A_{l,t,s}
&=
\frac{
(\nu-\mu)\widetilde Q_{t,s}(q_{s,n}^{l})
}{
\nu\widetilde Q_{t,s}(q_{s,n}^{l})
},                                                        \notag\\
B_{l,t,s}
&=(\nu-\mu)D_\mu\mathcal R_{l,t,s}.
\label{eq:dmop-direct-propagated-exact-ratio-expansion}
\end{align}
Subtracting $l=0$ from $l=1$ yields the exact identity
\begin{align}
&C_{t,n}^{(s)}(\nu)-C_{t,n}^{(s)}(\mu)
-(\nu-\mu)
\{D_\mu\mathcal R_{1,t,s}-D_\mu\mathcal R_{0,t,s}\}
                                                               \notag\\
&\quad=
-(A_{1,t,s}-A_{0,t,s})B_{1,t,s}
-A_{0,t,s}(B_{1,t,s}-B_{0,t,s}).
\label{eq:dmop-direct-propagated-two-remainder-products}
\end{align}
Put
\begin{align}
a_{l,t,s}
&=
\frac{\widetilde Q_{t,s}(q_{s,n}^{l})}
{\mu\widetilde Q_{t,s}(q_{s,n}^{l})},
\qquad l\in\{0,1\},                                      \notag\\
F_{t,s,n}
&=
\frac{\widetilde Q_{t,s}(q_{s,n}^{1})}
{\widetilde Q_{t,s}(q_{s,n}^{0})}.
\label{eq:dmop-direct-propagated-normalized-denominators}
\end{align}
Then $\mu(a_{l,t,s})=1$, and the definition of $F_{t,s,n}$ gives
\begin{equation}
a_{1,t,s}-a_{0,t,s}
=
a_{0,t,s}
\left\{
\frac{F_{t,s,n}}{\mu(a_{0,t,s}F_{t,s,n})}-1
\right\}.
\label{eq:dmop-direct-propagated-normalizer-difference}
\end{equation}
The scalar $\mu(a_{0,t,s}F_{t,s,n})$ lies between the infimum and
supremum of $F_{t,s,n}$.  The uniform normalizer ratios and
Equations~(\ref{eq:dmop-direct-propagated-future-ratio-contraction})--
(\ref{eq:dmop-direct-propagated-normalizer-difference}) therefore give
\begin{align}
\inf_l\nu(a_{l,t,s})&\geq C^{-1},                          \notag\\
\operatorname{osc}(a_{0,t,s})&\leq C,                     \notag\\
\operatorname{osc}(a_{1,t,s}-a_{0,t,s})
&\leq C\varrho^{n-t}.
\label{eq:dmop-direct-propagated-normalizer-factor-bounds}
\end{align}
Exactly as in Equation~(\ref{eq:dmop-direct-local-A-difference}),
\begin{align}
A_{1,t,s}-A_{0,t,s}
&=
\frac{(\nu-\mu)(a_{1,t,s}-a_{0,t,s})}
{\nu(a_{0,t,s})\nu(a_{1,t,s})},                           \notag\\
A_{0,t,s}
&=
\frac{(\nu-\mu)(a_{0,t,s})}{\nu(a_{0,t,s})}.
\label{eq:dmop-direct-propagated-A-factor-identities}
\end{align}
Also,
\begin{align}
B_{1,t,s}-B_{0,t,s}
&=
(\nu-\mu)
\{D_\mu\mathcal R_{1,t,s}-D_\mu\mathcal R_{0,t,s}\},
                                                               \notag\\
B_{1,t,s}
&=(\nu-\mu)D_\mu\mathcal R_{1,t,s}.
\label{eq:dmop-direct-propagated-B-factor-identities}
\end{align}
Equation~(\ref{eq:dmop-direct-future-current-derivative-bounds}) and the
single-ratio formula obtained from
Equation~(\ref{eq:dmop-direct-propagated-ratios}) give
\begin{align}
\operatorname{osc}
\{D_\mu\mathcal R_{1,t,s}-D_\mu\mathcal R_{0,t,s}\}
&\leq CG'(\theta)\varrho^{n-t},                            \notag\\
\operatorname{osc}(D_\mu\mathcal R_{1,t,s})
&\leq CG'(\theta).
\label{eq:dmop-direct-propagated-B-factor-bounds}
\end{align}
Substitution of Equations~
(\ref{eq:dmop-direct-propagated-normalizer-factor-bounds})--
(\ref{eq:dmop-direct-propagated-B-factor-bounds}) into
Equation~(\ref{eq:dmop-direct-propagated-two-remainder-products}) gives
\begin{align}
&\left|
C_{t,n}^{(s)}(\nu)-C_{t,n}^{(s)}(\mu)
-(\nu-\mu)
\{D_\mu\mathcal R_{1,t,s}-D_\mu\mathcal R_{0,t,s}\}
\right|                                                     \notag\\
&\quad\leq
C|(\nu-\mu)(a_{1,t,s}-a_{0,t,s})|
|(\nu-\mu)D_\mu\mathcal R_{1,t,s}|                        \notag\\
&\qquad+
C|(\nu-\mu)(a_{0,t,s})|
|(\nu-\mu)
\{D_\mu\mathcal R_{1,t,s}-D_\mu\mathcal R_{0,t,s}\}|.
\label{eq:dmop-direct-propagated-quadratic-remainder}
\end{align}
The first product in
Equation~(\ref{eq:dmop-direct-propagated-quadratic-remainder}) has
oscillation product at most $CG'(\theta)\varrho^{n-t}$; the second has
the same bound.  Centering and dividing the four functions by their
displayed oscillation bounds therefore places the common coefficient
$CG'(\theta)\varrho^{n-t}$ outside two products of empirical errors of
functions having oscillation at most one.  This is the required
coefficient for the quadratic remainder when $t<s$.  Together with
Equation~(\ref{eq:dmop-direct-local-quadratic-remainder}) for $s\leq t$
and the direct ratio expansion for $H_{t,n}$, all quadratic remainders
therefore have the coefficients displayed in
Equation~(\ref{eq:dmop-direct-future-current-derivative-bounds}).

Equation~(\ref{eq:location-weight-gradient-pair-recursion}) shows why no
additional mixing coefficient is required for the accumulated weight
gradient: the previously accumulated component is multiplied by
$\alpha$, while every new
input is a bounded function $h_s(X_s)$ of the mixing location.
The proof now sums those explicit coefficients before taking norms.
After collecting all score contributions at their local sampling time
$t$, those with $s\leq t\leq n$ have total coefficient
\begin{align}
&CG'(\theta)
\sum_{s=1}^{t}\sum_{n=t}^{N}
\alpha^{n-s}\varrho^{n-t}                                  \notag\\
&\quad=
CG'(\theta)
\left(\sum_{a=0}^{t-1}\alpha^a\right)
\left(\sum_{b=0}^{N-t}(\alpha\varrho)^b\right)             \notag\\
&\quad\leq
\frac{CG'(\theta)}{(1-\alpha)(1-\alpha\varrho)}.
\label{eq:dmop-direct-past-score-coefficient-sum}
\end{align}
Those with $t<s\leq n$ have total coefficient
\begin{align}
&CG'(\theta)
\sum_{s=t+1}^{N}\sum_{n=s}^{N}
\alpha^{n-s}\varrho^{s-t}\varrho^{n-s}                    \notag\\
&\quad\leq
CG'(\theta)
\left(\sum_{a=1}^{\infty}\varrho^a\right)
\left(\sum_{b=0}^{\infty}(\alpha\varrho)^b\right)          \notag\\
&\quad=
\frac{CG'(\theta)\varrho}
{(1-\varrho)(1-\alpha\varrho)}.
\label{eq:dmop-direct-future-score-coefficient-sum}
\end{align}
The current predictor terms in
Equation~(\ref{eq:dmop-direct-current-predictor-telescope}) contribute
\begin{equation}
CG'(\theta)\sum_{n=t}^{N}\varrho^{n-t}
\leq\frac{CG'(\theta)}{1-\varrho}.
\label{eq:dmop-direct-current-predictor-coefficient-sum}
\end{equation}
Since $1-\alpha\varrho\geq1-\varrho$, the oscillation of the
aggregated first-order local function, and the coefficient of its
quadratic remainder, are at most
\begin{equation}
\frac{CG'(\theta)}{1-\alpha}.
\label{eq:dmop-direct-aggregated-local-oscillation}
\end{equation}

The same bound can be seen directly on the current location--weight-gradient
pair.  For this calculation only, write
\begin{equation}
w_{t,i}
=
\left.\partial_{\theta_i}\log w_t^{F,\theta}\right|_{\theta=\phi}.
\end{equation}
Consider two pairs $(x,w_{t,i})$ and $(x',w'_{t,i})$ at time $t$.
For each terminal time $t+r$, couple the normalized forward location
kernels in Equation~(\ref{eq:dmop-direct-normalized-forward-product}).
Equation~(\ref{eq:weak-bias-forward-minorization}) permits the coupling
to be chosen so that, at every intermediate time $t+q$,
\begin{equation}
\Pr\{X_{t+q}\ne X'_{t+q}\}
\leq
\varrho^q.
\label{eq:dmop-direct-location-coupling}
\end{equation}
Iteration of Equation~(\ref{eq:location-weight-gradient-pair-recursion})
gives
\begin{align}
w_{t+r,i}
&=
\alpha^r w_{t,i}
+\sum_{q=1}^{r}\alpha^{r-q}h_{t+q,i}(X_{t+q}),             \notag\\
w'_{t+r,i}
&=
\alpha^r w'_{t,i}
+\sum_{q=1}^{r}\alpha^{r-q}h_{t+q,i}(X'_{t+q}).
\label{eq:dmop-direct-coupled-weight-gradient-iteration}
\end{align}
Equations~(\ref{eq:bounded-realized-measurement-score}),
(\ref{eq:dmop-direct-location-coupling}), and
(\ref{eq:dmop-direct-coupled-weight-gradient-iteration}) imply
\begin{align}
\E|w_{t+r,i}-w'_{t+r,i}|
&\leq
\alpha^r|w_{t,i}-w'_{t,i}|
+2G'(\theta)\sum_{q=1}^{r}\alpha^{r-q}\varrho^q.
\label{eq:dmop-direct-coupled-weight-gradient-bound}
\end{align}
The remaining convolution is the finite sum
\begin{equation}
\sum_{q=1}^{r}\alpha^{r-q}\varrho^q
=
\begin{cases}
\displaystyle
\frac{\varrho(\alpha^r-\varrho^r)}{\alpha-\varrho},
&\alpha\ne\varrho,\\[2mm]
r\alpha^r,&\alpha=\varrho.
\end{cases}
\label{eq:dmop-direct-pair-forgetting-convolution}
\end{equation}
Hence the coupled pair forgets its initial condition in the bounded
location--absolute-weight metric used in
Equation~(\ref{eq:dmop-direct-coupled-weight-gradient-bound}), at the
geometric rate $\max(\alpha,\varrho)^r$ apart from the factor $r$ when
$\alpha=\varrho$.  This does not assert total-variation contraction of
the pair: deterministic differences in the second coordinate can remain
mutually singular even while their absolute distance contracts.
Let $L=N-t$.  The finite identities
\begin{align}
(1-\alpha)\sum_{r=0}^{L-1}\alpha^r+\alpha^L&=1,
\label{eq:dmop-direct-initial-weight-coefficient-one}
\\
(1-\alpha)\sum_{r=q}^{L-1}\alpha^{r-q}
+\alpha^{L-q}&=1,
\qquad 1\leq q\leq L,
\label{eq:dmop-direct-future-input-coefficient-one}
\end{align}
are exactly the coefficients in the full-score summation-by-parts identity
(\ref{eq:full-score-summation-by-parts}).  Multiply Equation~
(\ref{eq:dmop-direct-coupled-weight-gradient-bound}) by $1-\alpha$ for
$r<L$, retain coefficient one for $r=L$, and sum.  Interchanging the two
finite sums and using Equations~
(\ref{eq:dmop-direct-initial-weight-coefficient-one})--
(\ref{eq:dmop-direct-future-input-coefficient-one}) gives
\begin{align}
&(1-\alpha)\sum_{r=0}^{L-1}
\E|w_{t+r,i}-w'_{t+r,i}|
+\E|w_{t+L,i}-w'_{t+L,i}|                                \notag\\
&\quad\leq
\left|w_{t,i}-w'_{t,i}\right|
+2G'(\theta)\sum_{q=1}^{L}\varrho^q                      \notag\\
&\quad\leq
\frac{2G'(\theta)}{1-\alpha}
+\frac{2G'(\theta)\varrho}{1-\varrho}                   \notag\\
&\quad\leq
\frac{CG'(\theta)}{1-\alpha},
\label{eq:dmop-direct-pair-full-score-continuation-bound}
\end{align}
where Equation~(\ref{eq:unrolled-filtered-weight}) gives
$|w_{t,i}-w'_{t,i}|\leq2G'(\theta)/(1-\alpha)$.
Thus the pair need not be total-variation mixing: location coupling and
the affine contraction of its weight-gradient component give precisely
the score-continuation bound used in Equation~
(\ref{eq:dmop-direct-aggregated-local-oscillation}).

We next define the two local terms rather than citing a fixed-terminal
formula for their sum.  For the signed empirical error
$\eta_t^J-\mu_t^J$, put
\begin{align}
\Gamma_{t,i}^J
&=(\eta_t^J-\mu_t^J)
\left[
\sum_{n=t}^{N}D_{\mu_t^J}H_{t,n}
+\sum_{n=t}^{N}\sum_{s=1}^{n}
\alpha^{n-s}D_{\mu_t^J}C_{t,n}^{(s)}
\right],
\label{eq:dmop-direct-centered-local-field-definition}
\\
B_{t,i}^J
&=
\sum_{n=t}^{N}
\left[
H_{t,n}(\eta_t^J)-H_{t,n}(\mu_t^J)
-(\eta_t^J-\mu_t^J)D_{\mu_t^J}H_{t,n}
\right]                                                     \notag\\
&\quad+
\sum_{n=t}^{N}\sum_{s=1}^{n}\alpha^{n-s}
\left[
C_{t,n}^{(s)}(\eta_t^J)-C_{t,n}^{(s)}(\mu_t^J)
-(\eta_t^J-\mu_t^J)D_{\mu_t^J}C_{t,n}^{(s)}
\right].
\label{eq:dmop-direct-local-remainder-definition}
\end{align}
Substitute the telescopes
Equations~(\ref{eq:dmop-direct-current-predictor-telescope}) and
(\ref{eq:dmop-direct-continuation-full-telescope}) into the difference
of Equations~(\ref{eq:dmop-direct-exact-predictor-decomposition}) and
(\ref{eq:dmop-direct-exact-population-decomposition}), and then use
Equations~(\ref{eq:dmop-direct-centered-local-field-definition}) and
(\ref{eq:dmop-direct-local-remainder-definition}).  Term-by-term
collection gives the exact identity
\begin{equation}
Z_i^J-z_i^\alpha
=
\sum_{n=1}^{N}R_{n,i}^J
+\sum_{t=1}^{N}\Gamma_{t,i}^J
+\sum_{t=1}^{N}B_{t,i}^J,
\label{eq:dmop-direct-local-error-decomposition}
\end{equation}
which is the local perturbation construction in Definition~3.1 and
Appendix~A.3 of \citet{delMoral11}, specialized before the sums are
taken.  Conditional independence of the $J$ local particle draws,
Equations~(\ref{eq:dmop-direct-local-quadratic-remainder}) and
(\ref{eq:dmop-direct-aggregated-local-oscillation}), and
\begin{align}
\E\left[
\left.
\left\{\frac1J\sum_{j=1}^J
\big(f(X_j)-\mu(f)\big)\right\}^2
\right|\mu
\right]
&=\frac{\mu\{(f-\mu f)^2\}}J
\leq\frac{\operatorname{osc}(f)^2}{4J},                   \notag\\
\left\|
\left(\frac1J\sum_{j=1}^J\{f_1(X_j)-\mu(f_1)\}\right)
\left(\frac1J\sum_{j=1}^J\{f_2(X_j)-\mu(f_2)\}\right)
\right\|_{L^2\mid\mu}
&\leq
\frac{C\operatorname{osc}(f_1)\operatorname{osc}(f_2)}J
\label{eq:dmop-direct-local-empirical-moments}
\end{align}
give
\begin{align}
\E(\Gamma_{t,i}^J\mid\mathcal F_{t-1})&=0,                 \notag\\
\E\{(\Gamma_{t,i}^J)^2\mid\mathcal F_{t-1}\}
&\leq
\frac{CG'(\theta)^2}{J(1-\alpha)^2},                       \notag\\
\|B_{t,i}^J\|_{L^2}
&\leq
\frac{CG'(\theta)}{J(1-\alpha)}.
\label{eq:dmop-direct-local-field-bounds}
\end{align}
Here $\mathcal F_{t-1}$ contains all particle variables preceding the
time-$t$ local draws.  For completeness, if
$Y_j=f(X_j)-\mu(f)$, conditional independence and $\mu(Y_j)=0$ give
\begin{align}
&\E\left[
\left.
\left(\frac1J\sum_{j=1}^JY_j\right)^4
\right|\mu
\right]                                                     \notag\\
&\quad=
\frac1{J^4}
\left\{
\sum_{j=1}^J\mu(Y_j^4)
+6\sum_{1\leq j<k\leq J}\mu(Y_j^2)\mu(Y_k^2)
\right\}                                                    \notag\\
&\quad\leq
\frac{J+3J(J-1)}{J^4}\operatorname{osc}(f)^4              \notag\\
&\quad\leq
\frac{3}{J^2}\operatorname{osc}(f)^4.
\label{eq:dmop-direct-local-fourth-moment}
\end{align}
The second inequality in
Equation~(\ref{eq:dmop-direct-local-empirical-moments}) follows by
Cauchy--Schwarz applied to two instances of
Equation~(\ref{eq:dmop-direct-local-fourth-moment}).

Within each of the two families, the variables $R_{n,i}^J$ and
$\Gamma_{t,i}^J$ are martingale differences in chronological order.
The two families need not be mutually orthogonal because the selection
at time $n$ also enters the next prediction cloud.  Applying
$\|A+B\|_{L^2}^2\leq2\|A\|_{L^2}^2+2\|B\|_{L^2}^2$ first, and then
martingale orthogonality separately within each family, gives
\begin{align}
\left\|
\sum_{n=1}^{N}R_{n,i}^J
+\sum_{t=1}^{N}\Gamma_{t,i}^J
\right\|_{L^2}^2
&\leq
2\sum_{n=1}^{N}\|R_{n,i}^J\|_{L^2}^2
+2\sum_{t=1}^{N}\|\Gamma_{t,i}^J\|_{L^2}^2               \notag\\
&\leq
\frac{CNG'(\theta)^2}{J(1-\alpha)^2}.
\label{eq:dmop-direct-centered-local-sum}
\end{align}
For the quadratic terms, Minkowski's inequality gives
\begin{align}
\left\|\sum_{t=1}^{N}B_{t,i}^J\right\|_{L^2}
&\leq
\sum_{t=1}^{N}\|B_{t,i}^J\|_{L^2}                         \notag\\
&\leq
\frac{CNG'(\theta)}{J(1-\alpha)}.
\label{eq:dmop-direct-quadratic-sum}
\end{align}
Combining Equations~(\ref{eq:dmop-direct-local-error-decomposition}),
(\ref{eq:dmop-direct-centered-local-sum}), and
(\ref{eq:dmop-direct-quadratic-sum}), and using
$(a+b)^2\leq2a^2+2b^2$, yields
\begin{equation}
\E|Z_i^J-z_i^\alpha|^2
\leq
CG'(\theta)^2
\left\{
\frac{N}{J(1-\alpha)^2}
+\frac{N^2}{J^2(1-\alpha)^2}
\right\}.
\label{eq:dmop-direct-coordinate-particle-error}
\end{equation}
Summing over $i=1,\ldots,p$ proves
Equation~(\ref{eq:refined-dmop-alpha-direct-particle-error}).  Since
$z_i^\alpha$ is deterministic,
\begin{equation}
\Var(Z_i^J)
=\inf_{a\in\mathbb R}\E|Z_i^J-a|^2
\leq\E|Z_i^J-z_i^\alpha|^2.
\end{equation}
Summing this inequality over the coordinates proves
Equation~(\ref{eq:refined-dmop-alpha-variance}).  If $J\geq N$, then
$N^2/J^2\leq N/J$, which proves
Equation~(\ref{eq:refined-dmop-alpha-variance-j-geq-n}).
\end{proof}

\fi
\begin{proof}[Proof.]
    Consider the case of MOP-$\alpha$ when $\alpha=1$ and $\theta=\phi$. We then have a nice telescoping product property for the after-resampling likelihood estimate:
\begin{eqnarray}
    \hat{\lik}^1(\theta) &:=& \prod_{n=1}^N L_n^{A, \theta, \alpha} = \prod_{n=1}^N L_n^\phi \cdot \frac{\sum_{j=1}^J w_{n,j}^{F,\theta}}{\sum_{j=1}^J w_{n,j}^{P,\theta}}= \prod_{n=1}^N L_n^\phi \cdot \frac{\sum_{j=1}^J w_{n,j}^{F,\theta}}{\sum_{j=1}^J w_{n-1,j}^{F,\theta}} \nonumber
\\
&=& \left(\frac{1}{J}\sum_{j=1}^J w_{N,j}^{F,\theta}\right) \prod_{n=1}^N L_n^\phi,
\end{eqnarray}
where the third equality follows from the choice of $\alpha=1$, and the fourth equality is the resulting telescoping property. 
The log-derivative identity lets us decompose the score estimate as
\begin{equation}\label{eq:log-derivative-identity}
\nabla_\theta \hat\ell^1(\theta) = \frac{\nabla_\theta \hat\lik^1(\theta)}{\hat\lik^1(\theta)} = \frac{\nabla_\theta\left(\frac{1}{J}\sum_{j=1}^J w_{N,j}^{F,\theta}\right) \prod_{n=1}^N L_n^\phi}{\prod_{n=1}^N L_n^\phi} =  \frac{1}{J}\sum_{j=1}^J \nabla_\theta w_{N,j}^{F,\theta}.
\end{equation}
From (\ref{eq:log-derivative-identity}), we see that the derivative of the log-likelihood estimate is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:eq:log-derivative2}
    \nabla_\theta \hat{\ell}^1(\theta) := \frac{1}{J}\sum_{j=1}^J \nabla_\theta w_{N,j}^{F,\theta}.
\end{equation}
We proceed to decompose (\ref{eq:eq:log-derivative2}).
First, observe that as $\alpha=1$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:lemma-s1-ratio}
w_{n,j}^{P,\theta} = w_{n-1,j}^{F,\theta}\frac{g_{n,j}^\theta}{g_{n,j}^\phi} = \prod_{i=1}^n \frac{g_{i,j}^{A,P,\theta}}{g_{i,j}^{A,P,\phi}},
\end{equation}
Note that the right hand side of (\ref{eq:lemma-s1-ratio}) is the cumulative product of measurement density ratios over the ancestral trajectory of the $j$-th prediction particle at timestep $n$.
We then use the log-derivative identity again, yielding the following expression for the gradient of the log-weights as the sum of the log measurement densities over the ancestral trajectory,
\begin{eqnarray}
 \frac{\nabla_\theta w_{n,j}^{P,\theta}}{w_{n,j}^{P,\theta}} = \nabla_\theta \log w_{n,j}^{P,\theta} &=& \nabla_\theta \log \left(\prod_{i=1}^n \frac{g_{i,j}^{A,P,\theta}}{g_{i,j}^{A,P,\phi}}\right) 
 \\
 &=& \nabla_\theta \sum_{i=1}^n \left(\log g_{i,j}^{A,P,\theta} - \log g_{i,j}^{A,P,\phi}\right)
 \\
 &=& \sum_{i=1}^n \nabla_\theta \log g_{i,j}^{A,P,\theta}.
\end{eqnarray}
This is equal to the gradient of the logarithm of the conditional density of the observed measurements given the ancestral trajectory of the $j$-th prediction particle up to timestep $n$,
\begin{eqnarray}  
\nabla_\theta \sum_{n=1}^N \log g_{n,j}^{A,P,\theta} &=& \nabla_\theta \log\left(\prod_{n=1}^N g_{n,j}^{A,P,\theta}\right) 
\\
&=&  \nabla_\theta \log\left(\prod_{n=1}^N f_{Y_n|X_n}\big(y_n^* | x_{n,j}^{A, P,\theta};\theta\big)\right)
\\
&=& \nabla_\theta \log f_{Y_{1:N}|X_{1:N}}\big(y_{1:N}^* | x_{1:n,j}^{A, P,\theta};\theta\big).
\end{eqnarray}
Multiplying both sides of the expression by $w_{N,j}^{P,\theta} $ yields an expression for the gradient of the weights at timestep $N$,
\begin{equation}
\nabla_\theta w_{N,j}^{P,\theta} = w_{N,j}^{P,\theta} \sum_{n=1}^N \nabla_\theta \log g_{n,j}^{A,P,\theta} = w_{N,j}^{P,\theta} \nabla_\theta \log f_{Y_{1:N}|X_{1:N}}\big(y_{1:N}^* | x_{1:n,j}^{A, P,\theta};\theta\big).    
\end{equation}
Substituting the above identity into the log-likelihood decomposition obtained earlier in Equation \ref{eq:log-derivative-identity} yields
\begin{eqnarray}
    \nabla_\theta \hat{\ell}^1(\theta) &=& \frac{1}{J}\sum_{j=1}^J \nabla_\theta w_{N,j}^{F,\theta} =\frac{1}{J}\sum_{j=1}^J \nabla_\theta w_{N,k_j}^{P,\theta}
    \nonumber \\
    &=& \frac{1}{J}\sum_{j=1}^J w_{N,k_j}^{P,\theta} \nabla_\theta \log f_{Y_{1:N}|X_{1:N}}\big(y_{1:N}^* | x_{1:n,k_j}^{A, P,\theta};\theta \big).
\end{eqnarray}
Finally, observing that $\theta=\phi$ implies $w_{N,j}^{F,\theta}=1$, we obtain 
\begin{equation}
    \nabla_\theta \hat{\ell}^1(\theta) = \frac{1}{J}\sum_{j=1}^J \nabla_\theta \log f_{Y_{1:N}|X_{1:N}}\big(y_{1:N}^* | x_{1:n,j}^{A, F,\theta};\theta\big).
\end{equation}
This has the same undiscounted path-space score target as the estimators of
\cite{poyiadjis11, scibior21} for the bootstrap filter.  The
reparameterization score contribution need not equal their complete-data
likelihood-ratio score contribution particle by particle.
\end{proof}

Note that the variance of the DMOP-$\alpha$ log-likelihood derivative estimate scales poorly with $N$ when $\theta\neq\phi$. 
This can be seen by observing that
\begin{eqnarray}
    \nabla_\theta \hat{\ell}^1(\theta) &=& \frac{1}{J}\sum_{j=1}^J \nabla_\theta w_{N,j}^{F,\theta} =\frac{1}{J}\sum_{j=1}^J \nabla_\theta w_{N,k_j}^{P,\theta}
\nonumber \\
&=& \frac{1}{J}\sum_{j=1}^J w_{N,k_j}^{P,\theta} \nabla_\theta \log f_{Y_{1:N}|X_{1:N}}\big(y_{1:N}^* | x_{1:n,k_j}^{A, P,\theta}; \theta \big).
\end{eqnarray}
When $\theta\neq\phi$, we see that $w_{N,k_j}^{P,\theta} = O(c^N)$. 
When $\theta=\phi$, a generic normalized path-functional inequality gives
the coarse upper bound $O(N^4/J)$ after rescaling to the full
unnormalized score.  Section~\ref{appendix:sharper-biasvar} gives an exact
constant-weight calculation with order $N^2/J$ at $\alpha=1$ and explains
why this example does not by itself provide a general finite-$J$ upper
bound.

\begin{lem}
\label{lem:mop-0-formula}
Write $\nabla_\theta \hat\ell^\alpha(\theta)$ for the DMOP-$\alpha$ gradient estimate (or, equivalently, the gradient of MOP-$\alpha$ when $\theta=\phi$).
Consider the case where we use the after-resampling conditional likelihood estimate so that $\hat\lik(\theta) = \prod_{n=1}^N L_n^{A, \theta, \alpha}$.
When $\alpha=0$,
    \begin{equation}
        \nabla_\theta \hat\ell^0(\theta) 
        = \frac{1}{J} \sum_{n=1}^N \sum_{j=1}^J \nabla_\theta \log\left(f_{Y_n|X_{n}}\big(y_n^*|x_{n,j}^{F, \theta}; \theta\big)\right),
    \end{equation}
    yielding the estimate of \cite{naesseth18} when applied to the bootstrap filter. 
\end{lem}
\begin{proof}[Proof.]
First, write
\begin{equation}
s_{n,j} = \frac{f_{Y_n|X_n}\big(y_n^*|x_{n,j}^{P, \theta};\theta\big)}{f_{Y_n|X_n}\big(y_n^*|x_{n,j}^{P, \phi};\theta\big)}
\end{equation}
as shorthand for the measurement density ratios. 
Observe that, when $\alpha=0,$, the likelihood estimate becomes
\begin{eqnarray}
% \nonumber
    \hat{\lik}^0(\theta) := \prod_{n=1}^N L_n^{A, \theta, \alpha} &=& \prod_{n=1}^N L_n^\phi \cdot \frac{\sum_{j=1}^J w_{n,j}^{F,\theta}}{\sum_{j=1}^J w_{n,j}^{P,\theta}} 
    \\
    &=& \prod_{n=1}^N L_n^\phi \cdot \frac{1}{J}\sum_{j=1}^J s_{n,j} 
    \\
    &=& \prod_{n=1}^N L_n^\phi \cdot \frac{1}{J}\sum_{j=1}^J \frac{f_{Y_n|X_n}\big(y_n^*|x_{n,j}^{P, \theta};\theta\big)}{f_{Y_n|X_n}\big(y_n^*|x_{n,j}^{P, \phi};\theta\big)}.
\end{eqnarray}
We lose the nice telescoping property observed in the MOP-$1$ case, but this expression still yields something useful. 
This is because its gradient when $\theta=\phi$ is therefore 
\begin{align}
    \nabla_\theta \hat{\ell}^0(\theta) &:= \sum_{n=1}^N \nabla_\theta \log\left(L_n^\phi \frac{1}{J} \sum_{j=1}^J s_{n,j}\right) \\
    &= \sum_{n=1}^N \frac{\nabla_\theta \left(L_n^\phi \frac{1}{J} \sum_{j=1}^J s_{n,j}\right)}{\left(L_n^\phi \frac{1}{J} \sum_{j=1}^J s_{n,j}\right)} \\
    &= \sum_{n=1}^N \frac{\sum_{j=1}^J \nabla_\theta s_{n,j}}{\sum_{j=1}^J s_{n,j}} \\
    &= \sum_{n=1}^N \frac{1}{J} \sum_{j=1}^J \frac{\nabla_\theta f_{Y_n|X_{n}}\big(y_n^*|x_{n,j}^{F, \theta}; \theta\big)}{f_{Y_n|X_{n}}(y_n^*|x_{n,j}^{F, \phi}; \phi)} \\
    &= \frac{1}{J} \sum_{n=1}^N \sum_{j=1}^J \nabla_\theta \log\left(f_{Y_n|X_{n}}\big(y_n^*|x_{n,j}^{F, \theta}; \theta\big)\right),
\end{align}
where we use the log-derivative trick in the second equality, observe that $\sum_{j=1}^J s_{n,j} = J$ when $\theta=\phi$ in the fourth equality, and use the log-derivative trick again while noting that $\theta=\phi$ in the fifth equality. This yields the desired result.
\end{proof}



\section{Proof of Theorem~{\thmMopConvergence} (Optimization Convergence Analysis)}
\label{appendix:convergence}

All derivatives in this section are evaluated at the common filtering and
evaluation parameter $\theta=\phi$.  Let $Z_n$ contain the complete ancestral
path through time $n$, the parameter-independent simulator variables, and the
first- and second-order gradients propagated by automatic differentiation.  Set
\begin{align}
G_n^\theta(Z_n)
  &=f_{Y_n\mid X_n}(y_n^*\mid X_n^\theta;\theta),
&W_\theta(Z_N)&=\prod_{n=1}^N G_n^\theta(Z_n),                 \label{eq:s2-weight}\\
\psi_\theta(Z_N)
  &=\nabla_\theta^{\rm tot}\log W_\theta(Z_N),
&\Xi_\theta(Z_N)&=\nabla_\theta^{2,{\rm tot}}
                         \log W_\theta(Z_N).                  \label{eq:s2-score-curvature}
\end{align}
The superscript ``tot'' means that the derivative passes through the
reparameterized simulated path while the base random numbers and the discrete
resampling indices are held fixed.

Write $\rho_N^J=J^{-1}\sum_{j=1}^J\delta_{Z_{N,j}}$ for the empirical measure
of the after-resampling genealogical particles, and define the normalized
Feynman--Kac path measure by
\begin{equation}
\rho_N(F)=\frac{\E\{W_\theta(Z_N)F(Z_N)\}}
                 {\E\{W_\theta(Z_N)\}}.
\label{eq:s2-rho-definition}
\end{equation}
Assumptions~{\refAssumpBoundedMeasurement} and
{\refAssumpDifferentiableSimulator} give
$|\partial_{\theta_i}W_\theta|\leq N\bar G^NG'(\theta)$, so dominated
convergence and the log-derivative identity yield
\begin{equation}
\nabla_\theta\ell(\theta)=\rho_N(\psi_\theta),
\qquad
\nabla_\theta\hat\ell_J^{A,1}(\theta)=\rho_N^J(\psi_\theta).
\label{eq:s2-gradient-identities}
\end{equation}
The second equality is Lemma~\ref{lem:mop-1-formula}; importantly, it is one
terminal empirical average of the complete path score, not a sum of empirical
filtering expectations at the individual observation times.

For completeness, multinomial resampling gives the required genetic
Feynman--Kac particle system explicitly.  For semigroup indexing, let
$\mathsf Z_0$ be the augmented prediction path at observation $1$; for
$q=1,\ldots,N-1$, let $\mathsf Z_q$ be the prediction path at observation
$q+1$; and let $\mathsf Z_N$ be the terminal path after the final resampling.
For $0\leq q\leq N$, write
$\rho_q^J=J^{-1}\sum_{j=1}^J\delta_{\mathsf Z_{q,j}}$, where the
$\mathsf Z_{0,j}$ are independent draws from the unweighted prediction law of
$\mathsf Z_0$ and $\mathsf Z_{N,j}=Z_{N,j}$.
Write $V_q^\theta(\mathsf Z_q)=G_{q+1}^\theta(\mathsf Z_q)$ for
$q=0,\ldots,N-1$.  Let the locally indexed augmented kernel
$\mathcal M_{q+1}^\theta$ append the next independent simulator innovation and
its gradients for $0\leq q\leq N-2$, and put
$\mathcal M_N^\theta=\operatorname{Id}$ for the final selection.  The one-step
population map is
\begin{equation}
\Phi_{q+1}^\theta(\mu)(dz')
=\frac{\int\mu(dz)V_q^\theta(z)\mathcal M_{q+1}^\theta(z,dz')}
       {\mu(V_q^\theta)},\qquad 0\leq q<N.                    \label{eq:s2-fk-update}
\end{equation}
Conditionally on the current cloud, the $J$ children are independent with
common law $\Phi_{q+1}^\theta(\rho_q^J)$.  The final observation is handled by
the $q=N-1$ transition, namely multinomial selection with potential
$V_{N-1}^\theta=G_N^\theta$ followed by the identity mutation.  Thus
$\rho_N^J$ is exactly the terminal empirical measure of this augmented
historical system.  Systematic resampling does not have this
conditional-independence property and is not covered by the calculation.

We next write out the particle concentration constant used below.  For
$0\leq q\leq N$, let
\begin{align}
Q_{q,N}^\theta F(z_q)
 &=\E\left[
   F(\mathsf Z_N)\prod_{r=q}^{N-1}V_r^\theta(\mathsf Z_r)
   \,\middle|\,\mathsf Z_q=z_q
   \right],                                                     \label{eq:s2-q-semigroup}\\
P_{q,N}^\theta F
 &=\frac{Q_{q,N}^\theta F}{Q_{q,N}^\theta1},\qquad
g_{q,N}=\sup_{z,z'}
 \frac{Q_{q,N}^\theta1(z)}{Q_{q,N}^\theta1(z')},\qquad
b_{q,N}=\sup_{\operatorname{osc}(F)\leq1}
 \operatorname{osc}(P_{q,N}^\theta F),                         \label{eq:s2-semigroup-constants}
\end{align}
where $Q_{N,N}^\theta F=F$ and
$\operatorname{osc}(F)=\sup_{z,z'}|F(z)-F(z')|$.  Thus
$0\leq b_{q,N}\leq1$.  With
\begin{equation}
R_G=\frac{\bar G}{\underbar G},\qquad
S_N=\sum_{q=0}^Ng_{q,N}^3b_{q,N},\qquad K_N=32S_N,\qquad
K_N^{\rm pf}=32\sum_{r=0}^NR_G^{3r},                           \label{eq:s2-explicit-constant}
\end{equation}
the potential bounds give, term by term,
\begin{equation}
\underbar G^{N-q}\leq Q_{q,N}^\theta1(z_q)
 \leq\bar G^{N-q},\qquad
g_{q,N}\leq R_G^{N-q},\qquad K_N\leq K_N^{\rm pf}.             \label{eq:s2-explicit-constant-bound}
\end{equation}
In particular, the constant used in the theorem is the closed-form quantity
\begin{equation}
K_N^{\rm pf}=
\begin{cases}
32(N+1),&R_G=1,\\[1mm]
\displaystyle 32\frac{R_G^{3(N+1)}-1}{R_G^3-1},&R_G>1.
\end{cases}                                                     \label{eq:s2-explicit-constant-closed}
\end{equation}
Here is the constant calculation behind the tail bound used below.  Let
$r_N^{\rm DM}$ and $\beta_N^{\rm DM}$ denote the second- and first-order
coefficients in Theorem~1.2 of \citet{delMoral11}.  The Feynman--Kac bounds in
their Appendix~A.3 give
\begin{equation}
r_N^{\rm DM}\leq4S_N,
\qquad
(\beta_N^{\rm DM})^2
 \leq4\sum_{q=0}^Ng_{q,N}^2b_{q,N}^2
 \leq4S_N,                                                       \label{eq:s2-dm-coefficient-bounds}
\end{equation}
where the last inequality uses $g_{q,N}\geq1$ and $0\leq b_{q,N}\leq1$.
If $S_N=0$, the particle error is identically zero.  Hence suppose $S_N>0$.
For $u\in(0,1/2]$, set $x=Ju^2/(32S_N)$.  If $x\leq\log6$, then
$6e^{-x}\geq1$ and the desired probability bound is trivial.  If
$x>\log6$, use
$h^{-1}(x)\leq2x+2\sqrt x$ for
$h(t)=\{t-\log(1+t)\}/2$ and
$1+2x+2\sqrt x\leq8x$ to obtain
\begin{align*}
\frac{r_N^{\rm DM}}J\{1+h^{-1}(x)\}
&\leq\frac{u^2}{8x}\{1+2x+2\sqrt x\}
 \leq u^2\leq\frac u2,\\
\beta_N^{\rm DM}\sqrt{\frac{2x}{J}}
&\leq2\sqrt{S_N}\sqrt{\frac{2x}{J}}=\frac u2.
\end{align*}
Theorem~1.2 applied to both $F$ and $-F$, after centering and scaling $F$ to
oscillation one, therefore gives the explicit Feynman--Kac corollary
\begin{equation}
\Pr\left(
 |(\rho_N^J-\rho_N)(F)|>\operatorname{osc}(F)u
 \right)
\leq6\exp\left(-\frac{Ju^2}{K_N}\right)
\leq6\exp\left(-\frac{Ju^2}{K_N^{\rm pf}}\right).             \label{eq:s2-scalar-concentration}
\end{equation}
for every $F$ with $\operatorname{osc}(F)>0$ and every $u\in(0,1/2]$.
If $F$ is constant, $(\rho_N^J-\rho_N)(F)=0$ identically.
Equations~\ref{eq:s2-explicit-constant}--
\ref{eq:s2-scalar-concentration} replace all unnamed model-dependent
concentration constants.

\begin{lem}[Explicit concentration of the DMOP-$1$ gradient]
\label{lemma:grad_bound}
Assume multinomial resampling and let
\begin{equation}
A_N(\theta)=\max_{1\leq i\leq p}
 \operatorname{osc}(\psi_{\theta,i}),
\qquad
u_g(J,\delta)=
 \sqrt{\frac{K_N^{\rm pf}}{J}\log\frac{6p}{\delta}}.
\label{eq:s2-gradient-constants}
\end{equation}
If $J\geq4K_N^{\rm pf}\log(6p/\delta)$, then, with probability at least
$1-\delta$,
\begin{equation}
\|\nabla_\theta\hat\ell_J^{A,1}(\theta)
      -\nabla_\theta\ell(\theta)\|_2
\leq A_N(\theta)\sqrt p\,u_g(J,\delta).                        \label{eq:s2-gradient-concentration}
\end{equation}
Moreover, Assumption~{\refAssumpBoundedMeasurement} gives
$A_N(\theta)\leq2NG'(\theta)$.  Consequently, the explicit condition
\begin{equation}
J\geq K_N^{\rm pf}\log\frac{6p}{\delta}
 \max\left\{4,\frac{4pN^2G'(\theta)^2}{\epsilon^2}\right\}     \label{eq:s2-gradient-particle-count}
\end{equation}
implies that the left side of \eqref{eq:s2-gradient-concentration} is at most
$\epsilon$ with probability at least $1-\delta$.
\end{lem}

\begin{proof}
The lower bound on $J$ makes $u_g(J,\delta)\leq1/2$.  Apply
\eqref{eq:s2-scalar-concentration} to each normalized coordinate
$\psi_{\theta,i}/A_N(\theta)$; a constant coordinate has zero error and needs
no normalization.  A union bound over the $p$ coordinates gives
\begin{equation*}
\max_{1\leq i\leq p}
 |(\rho_N^J-\rho_N)(\psi_{\theta,i})|
 \leq A_N(\theta)u_g(J,\delta)
\end{equation*}
with probability at least $1-\delta$.  Now use
$\|v\|_2\leq\sqrt p\|v\|_\infty$ and
\eqref{eq:s2-gradient-identities}.  Finally,
$|\psi_{\theta,i}|\leq NG'(\theta)$, so
$A_N(\theta)\leq2NG'(\theta)$; substituting this inequality and solving
$2NG'(\theta)\sqrt p\,u_g(J,\delta)\leq\epsilon$ gives
\eqref{eq:s2-gradient-particle-count}.
\end{proof}

The next result treats the actual particle Hessian.  It retains the
outer-product and empirical-mean terms created by differentiating the logarithm
of the terminal empirical average.

\begin{lem}[Explicit concentration of the DMOP-$1$ particle Hessian]
\label{lemma:hess_bound}
Suppose in addition that Assumption~{\refAssumpBoundedMeasurement} gives the
entrywise bound
\(
|\Xi_{\theta,ab}(Z_N)|\leq NG''(\theta)
\)
for $1\leq a,b\leq p$.  Define
\begin{equation}
d_p=\frac{p(p+3)}2,\qquad
H_N(\theta)=2p\{NG''(\theta)+3N^2G'(\theta)^2\},\qquad
u_H(J,\delta)=
 \sqrt{\frac{K_N^{\rm pf}}{J}\log\frac{6d_p}{\delta}}.         \label{eq:s2-hessian-constants}
\end{equation}
If $J\geq4K_N^{\rm pf}\log(6d_p/\delta)$, then, with probability at least
$1-\delta$,
\begin{equation}
\|\nabla_\theta^2\hat\ell_J^{A,1}(\theta)
      -\nabla_\theta^2\ell(\theta)\|_{\rm op}
\leq H_N(\theta)u_H(J,\delta).                                 \label{eq:s2-hessian-concentration}
\end{equation}
Fix $0<\kappa<\gamma$.  If
\begin{equation}
\gamma I_p\preceq-\nabla_\theta^2\ell(\theta)\preceq\Gamma I_p
\quad\hbox{and}\quad
J\geq K_N^{\rm pf}\log\frac{6d_p}{\delta}
 \max\left\{4,\frac{H_N(\theta)^2}{\kappa^2}\right\},       \label{eq:s2-hessian-particle-count}
\end{equation}
then, with probability at least $1-\delta$,
\begin{equation}
(\gamma-\kappa)I_p
 \preceq-\nabla_\theta^2\hat\ell_J^{A,1}(\theta)
 \preceq(\Gamma+\kappa)I_p.                                  \label{eq:s2-hessian-eigenvalues}
\end{equation}
In particular, the raw particle curvature is then positive definite and
invertible.
\end{lem}

\begin{proof}
For fixed reference-filter random numbers and resampling indices, the
telescoping identity in the proof of Lemma~\ref{lem:mop-1-formula} gives
\begin{equation*}
\hat\ell_J^{A,1}(\theta)
=C_\phi+\log\left\{\frac1J\sum_{j=1}^J\exp(a_j(\theta))\right\},
\qquad a_j(\theta)=\log w_{N,j}^{F,\theta},\qquad a_j(\phi)=0,
\end{equation*}
where $C_\phi$ is constant in $\theta$.  Twice differentiating this log-sum-exp at
$\theta=\phi$ gives the exact finite-particle identity
\begin{equation}
\nabla_\theta^2\hat\ell_J^{A,1}
=\rho_N^J(\Xi_\theta+\psi_\theta\psi_\theta^T)
 -\rho_N^J(\psi_\theta)\rho_N^J(\psi_\theta)^T.                \label{eq:s2-empirical-hessian-identity}
\end{equation}
Assumption~{\refAssumpBoundedMeasurement} also gives the entrywise dominating
bound
\begin{equation*}
|\partial_{\theta_a\theta_b}^2W_\theta|
=W_\theta|\Xi_{\theta,ab}+\psi_{\theta,a}\psi_{\theta,b}|
\leq\bar G^N\{NG''(\theta)+N^2G'(\theta)^2\}.
\end{equation*}
Thus differentiation through second order may pass under the expectation, and
differentiating $\log\E W_\theta$ gives the population identity
\begin{equation}
\nabla_\theta^2\ell
=\rho_N(\Xi_\theta+\psi_\theta\psi_\theta^T)
 -\rho_N(\psi_\theta)\rho_N(\psi_\theta)^T.                   \label{eq:s2-population-hessian-identity}
\end{equation}

Let $A_1=2NG'(\theta)$ and
$A_2=2\{NG''(\theta)+N^2G'(\theta)^2\}$.  The $p$ scalar functions
$\psi_a$ have oscillation at most $A_1$, and the symmetric entries of
$\Xi+\psi\psi^T$ have oscillation at most $A_2$.  Apply
\eqref{eq:s2-scalar-concentration} jointly to these functions, using
$d_p=p+p(p+1)/2$ scalar events.  On the resulting event,
\begin{align*}
\|(\rho_N^J-\rho_N)(\psi)\|_2
 &\leq A_1\sqrt p\,u_H(J,\delta),\\
\|(\rho_N^J-\rho_N)(\Xi+\psi\psi^T)\|_{\rm op}
 &\leq pA_2u_H(J,\delta).
\end{align*}
Both $\|\rho_N^J(\psi)\|_2$ and $\|\rho_N(\psi)\|_2$ are at most
$\sqrt p\,N G'(\theta)$.  Therefore
\begin{align*}
&\|\rho_N^J(\psi)\rho_N^J(\psi)^T
       -\rho_N(\psi)\rho_N(\psi)^T\|_{\rm op}\\
&\hspace{18mm}\leq
2\sqrt p\,N G'(\theta)\,
 \|(\rho_N^J-\rho_N)(\psi)\|_2
\leq4pN^2G'(\theta)^2u_H(J,\delta).
\end{align*}
Combining the last three displays with
\eqref{eq:s2-empirical-hessian-identity} and
\eqref{eq:s2-population-hessian-identity} proves
\eqref{eq:s2-hessian-concentration}.  Condition
\eqref{eq:s2-hessian-particle-count} makes its right side at most $\kappa$;
Weyl's eigenvalue inequality then gives
\eqref{eq:s2-hessian-eigenvalues}.
\end{proof}

For the optimization theorem, no stochastic Hessian claim is needed if the
curvature is regularized deterministically.  If
$\widetilde B=Q\operatorname{diag}(\lambda_1,\ldots,\lambda_p)Q^T$ is an
orthogonal eigendecomposition of any symmetric raw estimate, define
\begin{equation}
B=Q\operatorname{diag}([\lambda_1]_c^C,\ldots,[\lambda_p]_c^C)Q^T,
\qquad [x]_c^C=\min\{C,\max\{c,x\}\}.                          \label{eq:s2-spectral-clipping}
\end{equation}
For every $v\in\mathbb R^p$, direct expansion in the eigenbasis gives
\begin{equation}
c\|v\|_2^2\leq v^TBv\leq C\|v\|_2^2,
\qquad
C^{-1}\|v\|_2^2\leq v^TB^{-1}v\leq c^{-1}\|v\|_2^2.         \label{eq:s2-preconditioner-bounds}
\end{equation}
Thus $cI_p\preceq B\preceq CI_p$ and
$C^{-1}I_p\preceq B^{-1}\preceq c^{-1}I_p$.  Lemma~
\ref{lemma:hess_bound} shows that the unclipped particle Hessian has these
properties with $c=\gamma-\kappa$ and $C=\Gamma+\kappa$ under
\eqref{eq:s2-hessian-particle-count}; \eqref{eq:s2-spectral-clipping}
guarantees the chosen $c,C$ bounds for every realization.

\begin{proof}[Proof of Theorem~{\thmMopConvergence}.]
Let $\tau$ and $\mathcal E_M$ be the stopping time and simultaneous gradient
event defined in Theorem~{\thmMopConvergence}.  If $\mathcal F_m$ is the
history before the $m$th particle-filter call, then $\theta_m$ and
$\{m\leq\min(\tau,M-1)\}$ are $\mathcal F_m$-measurable.  Lemma~
\ref{lemma:grad_bound}, with failure probability $\delta/M$, gives
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\Pr(\mathcal E_M^c\mid\theta_0)
&\leq\sum_{m=0}^{M-1}
 \E\left[\mathbf1_{\{m\leq\min(\tau,M-1)\}}
 \Pr\left(
 \|g_m-\nabla f(\theta_m)\|_2>\epsilon\mid\mathcal F_m
 \right)\,\middle|\,\theta_0\right]\\
&\leq\sum_{m=0}^{M-1}\frac{\delta}{M}=\delta.
\end{aligned}                                                   \label{eq:s2-all-gradient-events}
\end{equation}
This calculation is valid at the adaptive iterates because each call uses a
fresh particle-randomness block.

Work on $\mathcal E_M$ and write
$p_m=-B_m^{-1}g_m$.  If $\|g_m\|_2\leq\sigma\epsilon$, then
\begin{equation}
\|\nabla f(\theta_m)\|_2
 \leq\|g_m\|_2+\epsilon\leq(\sigma+1)\epsilon.                 \label{eq:s2-stopping-gradient}
\end{equation}
Strong convexity implies
$f(\theta)-f(\theta^*)\leq\|\nabla f(\theta)\|_2^2/(2\gamma)$;
therefore
\begin{equation}
f(\theta_m)-f(\theta^*)
\leq\frac{(\sigma+1)^2\epsilon^2}{2\gamma}.                   \label{eq:s2-stopping-neighborhood}
\end{equation}

Suppose instead that $\|g_m\|_2>\sigma\epsilon$.  Since
$g_m=-B_mp_m$ and $cI_p\preceq B_m\preceq CI_p$,
\begin{equation}
c\|p_m\|_2\leq\|g_m\|_2\leq C\|p_m\|_2,
\qquad
\epsilon\|p_m\|_2<\frac{C}{\sigma}\|p_m\|_2^2.              \label{eq:s2-noise-direction-bound}
\end{equation}
The smoothness inequality and the gradient-error event give
\begin{align}
f(\theta_m+\eta p_m)-f(\theta_m)
&\leq\eta\nabla f(\theta_m)^Tp_m
      +\frac{\Gamma\eta^2}{2}\|p_m\|_2^2\notag\\
&\leq-\eta p_m^TB_mp_m
      +\eta\epsilon\|p_m\|_2
      +\frac{\Gamma\eta^2}{2}\|p_m\|_2^2.                    \label{eq:s2-descent-start}
\end{align}
The choices of $\sigma$ and $\eta$ imply
\begin{equation*}
\frac{C}{\sigma}\|p_m\|_2^2
 \leq\frac{c(1-\beta)}2\|p_m\|_2^2,
\qquad
\frac{\Gamma\eta}{2}\|p_m\|_2^2
 \leq\frac{c(1-\beta)}2\|p_m\|_2^2.
\end{equation*}
Using $p_m^TB_mp_m\geq c\|p_m\|_2^2$ in
\eqref{eq:s2-descent-start} now gives
\begin{equation}
f(\theta_{m+1})-f(\theta_m)
\leq-\eta\beta p_m^TB_mp_m.                                  \label{eq:s2-descent}
\end{equation}

On a nonstopping iteration,
\begin{equation}
\|\nabla f(\theta_m)\|_2
 \leq\|g_m\|_2+\epsilon
 <\frac{\sigma+1}{\sigma}\|g_m\|_2.                          \label{eq:s2-gradient-comparison}
\end{equation}
Consequently, \eqref{eq:s2-preconditioner-bounds} and strong convexity give
\begin{samepage}
\begin{align}
p_m^TB_mp_m
 &=g_m^TB_m^{-1}g_m\notag\\
 &\geq\frac1C\left(\frac{\sigma}{\sigma+1}\right)^2
       \|\nabla f(\theta_m)\|_2^2\notag\\
 &\geq\frac{2\gamma}{C}
       \left(\frac{\sigma}{\sigma+1}\right)^2
       \{f(\theta_m)-f(\theta^*)\}.                           \label{eq:s2-gap-direction}
\end{align}
\end{samepage}
Combining \eqref{eq:s2-descent} and \eqref{eq:s2-gap-direction} proves
\begin{equation}
f(\theta_{m+1})-f(\theta^*)
\leq q\{f(\theta_m)-f(\theta^*)\},
\qquad
q=1-\frac{2\eta\beta\gamma}{C}
       \left(\frac{\sigma}{\sigma+1}\right)^2.               \label{eq:s2-linear-rate}
\end{equation}
The step-size bound also verifies the claimed range of the contraction:
\begin{equation*}
0<1-q
<2\beta(1-\beta)\frac{c\gamma}{C\Gamma}
\leq\frac12,
\qquad\text{so}\qquad \frac12<q<1.
\end{equation*}
Iteration gives the $q^m$ conclusion in the theorem, while
\eqref{eq:s2-stopping-neighborhood} gives its stopping conclusion after
substituting $f=-\ell$.
\end{proof}

The result is deliberately restricted to DMOP-$1$.  For $\alpha<1$, the
population limit is a discounted gradient.  A theorem for that case must add a
finite-sample bound for the discount bias relative to the exact score; choosing
$\alpha$ close to one does not by itself provide such a bound.




\section{Feynman-Kac Models and Monte Carlo Approximations}
\label{appendix:feynman}




In this section, we introduce the Feynman-Kac convention of \cite{delMoral04} that has since become commonplace \citep{karjalainen23} for the analysis of the particle filter.
The mathematical formalization and notation introduced here will be adopted in the remainder of the analysis, in order to prove Theorems~{\thmMopTargeting}, \thmMopGradConsistency, and \thmMopBiasvar.
Let $(\eta_n)_{n=1}^N, (\pi_n)_{n=1}^N, (\rho_n)_{n=1}^N$ be sequences of probability measures on the state space $\gX$.
This is the sequence of prediction distributions $f_{X_{n}|Y_{1:n-1}}$, filtering distributions $f_{X_{n}|Y_{1:n}}$, and posterior distributions $f_{X_{1:n}|Y_{1:n}}$ that we seek to approximate with the particle filter.
For any measurable bounded functional $h$, we adopt the following functional-analytic notation, borrowed from \cite{delMoral04, chopin20, karjalainen23}.
We choose our specific choice of notation and definitions to be in line with that of \cite{karjalainen23}. 


\paragraph{\bf Markov kernels and the process model:} A Markov kernel $M$ with source $\gX_1$ and target $\gX_2$ is a map $M: \gX_1 \times \mathcal{B}(\gX_2) \to [0,1]$ such that for every set $A \in \mathcal{B}(\gX_2)$ and every point $x \in \gX_1$, the map $x \mapsto M(x, A)$ is a measurable function of $x$, and the map $A \mapsto M(x,A)$ is a probability measure on $\gX_2$. The quantity $M(x,A)$ can be thought of as the probability of transitioning to the set $A$ given that we are at the point $x$. If this yields a density, this then corresponds to the process density $f_{X_{2}|X_1}$ conditional on $x$ and integrated over $A$.  

\paragraph{\bf Markov kernels and measures:} For any measure $\eta$, any Markov kernel $M$ on $\gX$, any point $x \in \gX$ and any measurable subset $A \subseteq \gX$, let 
\begin{align}
    \eta(h) &= \int h \, d\eta = \int h(x) \eta(dx), \\(\eta M)(A) &= \int \eta(dx)M(x,A), \\
    (Mh)(x) &= \int M(x, dy) h(y).
\end{align}

\paragraph{\bf Compositions of Markov kernels:} The composition of a Markov kernel $M_1$ with another Markov kernel $M_2$ is another Markov kernel, given by 
\begin{equation}
 (M_1M_2)(x, A) = \int M_1(x, dy) M_2(y, A).
\end{equation}

\paragraph{\bf Total variation distance:} The total variation distance between two measures $\mu$ and $\nu$ on $\gX$ is
\begin{equation}
\|\mu-\nu\|_{\mathrm{TV}}=\sup _{\|h\|_{\infty} \leq 1 / 2}|\mu(\phi)-\nu(\phi)|=\sup _{\operatorname{osc}(h) \leq 1}|\mu(h)-\nu(h)|.    
\end{equation}

\paragraph{\bf Dobrushin contraction:} The Dobrushin contraction coefficient $\beta_{\text{TV}}$ of a Markov kernel $M$ is given by
\begin{equation}
\beta_{\mathrm{TV}}(M)=\sup _{x, y \in \gX}\big\|M(x, \cdot)-M(y, \cdot)\big\|_{\mathrm{TV}}=\sup _{\mu, \nu \in \mathcal{P}, \mu \neq \nu} \frac{\|\mu M-\nu M\|_{\mathrm{TV}}}{\|\mu-\nu\|_{\mathrm{TV}}}.    
\end{equation}

\paragraph{\bf Potential functions and the measurement model:} A potential function $G : \gX \to [0,\infty)$ is a non-negative function of an element of the state space $x \in \gX$. 
In our case, this corresponds to the measurement model, and in our previous notation is written as $g_{n,j} = f_{Y_n|X_n}\big(y_n^*|x_{n,j}^F;\theta\big) = G_n(x_{n,j}^F)$, where we suppress the dependence on $\theta$ for notational simplicity. 
Note that $G_n(\cdot) = f_{Y_n|X_n}(y_n^*|\;\cdot\;)$ is the conditional density of the observed measurement at time $n$, where we condition on the filtering particle $x_{n,j}^F$ as an element of the state space. 

\paragraph{\bf Feynman-Kac models:} A Feynman-Kac model on $\gX$ is a tuple $(\pi_0, (M_n)_{n=1}^N, (G_n)_{n=1}^N)$ of an initial probability measure on the state space $\pi_0$, a sequence of transition kernels $(M_n)_{n=1}^N$, and a sequence of potential functions $(G_n)_{n=1}^N$. In the notation used in the main text, this corresponds to the starting distribution $f_{X_0}$, the sequence of transition densities $f_{X_{n}|X_{n-1}}$, and the measurement densities $f_{Y_n|X_n}$. This induces a set of mappings from the set of probability measures on $\gX$ to itself, $\mathcal{P}(\gX) \to \mathcal{P}(\gX)$, as follows:
\begin{itemize}
    \item The update from the prediction to the filtering distributions is given by 
    \begin{equation}
    \pi_n(dx) = \Psi_n(\eta_n)(dx) = \frac{G_n(x)\cdot\eta_n(dx)}{\eta_n(G_n)}.
    \end{equation}
    \item The map from the prediction distribution at timestep $n$ to timestep $n+1$ is given by 
    \begin{equation}
 \Phi_{n+1}(\eta_n) = \Psi_n(\eta_n) M_{n+1}.       
    \end{equation}
    \item The composition of maps between prediction distributions yields the map from the prediction distribution at time $k$ to the prediction distribution at time $n$ where $k \leq n$,
    \begin{equation}
 \Phi_{k,n} = \Phi_n \circ ... \circ \Phi_{k+1}.       
    \end{equation}
\end{itemize}
\paragraph{\bf The particle filter:} The particle filter then yields a Monte Carlo approximation to the above Feynman-Kac model, via a sequence of mixture Dirac measures. When one resamples at every timestep, the prediction measure at timestep $n$ is then given by 
\begin{equation}
\eta_n^J = \frac{1}{J}\sum_{j=1}^J \delta_{x_{n,j}^P},
\end{equation}
and the filtering measure at timestep $n$ is given by
\begin{equation}
\pi_n^J = \frac{\sum_{j=1}^J g_{n,j} \delta_{x_{n,j}^P}}{\sum_{j=1}^J g_{n,j}} \approx \frac{1}{J} \sum_{j=1}^J \delta_{x_{n,j}^F}.
\end{equation}
In a slight abuse of notation, we will identify $x_{n, 1:J}^P \equiv \eta_n^J$, and $x_{n, 1:J}^F \equiv \pi_n^J$. 
As in \cite{karjalainen23}, one can view this as an inhomogenous Markov process evolving on $\gX^{J}$. The corresponding Markov transition kernel is then 
\begin{equation}
\textbf{M}_n(x_{n-1, 1:J}^P, \cdot) = \left(\Phi_{n}\left(\eta_{n-1}^J\right)\right)^{\otimes J} = \left(\Phi_{n}\left(\frac{1}{J}\sum_{j=1}^J \delta_{x_{n-1,j}^P}\right)\right)^{\otimes J},
\end{equation}
and the composition of Markov kernels on particles from timestep $n$ to timestep $n+k$ is written 
\begin{equation}
\textbf{M}_{n, n+k} = \textbf{M}_{n+1}\cdots \textbf{M}_{n+k}.
\end{equation}
One may wonder why \cite{karjalainen23} require this process to evolve on $\gX^{J}$. This is because at every timestep $n$, we in fact draw $X_{n, j}^P | \{X_{n-1, 1:J}^P = x_{n-1, 1:J}^P\} \sim \textbf{M}_n(x_{n-1, 1:J}^P, \cdot) = \eta_0^{\otimes J} \textbf{M}_{0,n}$ for $j=1,...,J$. 

\paragraph{\bf Forgetting of the particle filter:} 
The above formalization yields a result from \cite{karjalainen23} on the forgetting of the particle filter that we require for our analysis of the bias, variance, and error of MOP-$\alpha$.  
That is, \cite{karjalainen23} show that
\begin{equation}
\beta_{\mathrm{TV}}\left(\mathbf{M}_{n, n+k}\right) \leq(1-\epsilon)^{\lfloor k /(O(\log J))\rfloor},
\end{equation}
for some $\epsilon$ dependent on $\bar{G}, \underbar{G}, \bar{M}, \underbar{M}$ in Assumptions~{\refAssumpBoundedMeasurement} and~{\refAssumpBoundedProcess}. As a result, the mixing time of the particle filter is only on the order of $O(\log(J))$ timesteps. 


\vspace{3mm}

Equipped with the above formalisms and results, we are now in a position to provide guarantees on the performance of MOP-$\alpha$ itself. 



\section{Proof of Theorem~{\thmMopTargeting} via a Strong Law of Large Numbers for Triangular Arrays of Particles With Off-Parameter Resampling}
\label{appendix:targeting}



Here, we prove Theorem~{\thmMopTargeting} by deriving more general result, from which it will be clear that MOP-$\alpha$ targets the filtering distribution and is strongly consistent for the likelihood.
We will prove a strong law of large numbers for triangular arrays of particles with off-parameter resampling, meaning that we resample the particles according to an arbitrary resampling rule that is not necessarily in proportion to the target distribution of interest. 
Using weights that encode the cumulative discrepancy between the resampling distribution and the target distribution (instead of resampling to equal weights, as in the basic particle filter) provides a sufficient correction to ensure almost sure convergence.
We now introduce the precise definition of an off-parameter resampled particle filter. 

\begin{defn}[Off-Parameter Resampled Particle Filters]
    \label{defn:off-parameter-filter}
    An off-parameter resampled particle filter is a Monte Carlo approximation to a Feynman-Kac model $\left(\pi_0,\left(M_n\right)_{n=1}^N,\left(G_n\right)_{n=1}^N\right)$, where we inductively define given some $x_{n-1,j}^F$, $w_{n-1,j}^F$ comprising the filtering measure approximation $\pi_n^J$:
    \begin{enumerate}
        \item The prediction particles at timestep $n$, $x_{n,j}^P$, are drawn from $X_{n,j}^P \sim \pi_n^J M_n$, and the prediction weights are given by $w_{n,j}^P = w_{n-1,j}^F$. 
        \item The prediction measure at timestep $n$ is given by 
        \begin{equation}
        \eta_n^J = \frac{\sum_{j=1}^J w_{n,j}^P \, \delta_{x_{n,j}^P}}{\sum_{j=1}^J w_{n,j}^P}.
        \end{equation}
        \item The particles are resampled at every timestep $n$, yielding indices $k_j$ according to some arbitrary probabilities $(p_{n,j})_{j=1}^J$.
        \item The filtering measure at timestep $n$ is given by
        \begin{equation}
        \pi_n^J = \frac{\sum_{j=1}^J \delta_{x_{n,j}^P } w_{n,j}^P \, g_{n,j}}{\sum_{j=1}^J w_{n,j}^P \, g_{n,j}} \approx \frac{\sum_{j=1}^J \delta_{x_{n,k_j}^P }w_{n,k_j}^P \, g_{n,k_j}\big/p_{n,k_j}}{\sum_{j=1}^J w_{n,k_j}^P\, g_{n,k_j}\big/p_{n,k_j}} = \frac{\sum_{j=1}^J w_{n,j}^F \, \delta_{x_{n,j}^F}}{\sum_{j=1}^J w_{n,j}^F}.
        \end{equation}
        \item The prediction weights at the next timestep are given by $w_{n+1,j}^P = w_{n,j}^F =  w_{n,k_j}^P \, g_{n,k_j}\big/p_{n,k_j}$.
    \end{enumerate}
\end{defn}

To prove that the weight correction is sufficient for almost sure convergence, we first introduce what it means for a triangular array of particles to \textit{target} a given target distribution. 

\begin{defn}[Targeting]
    A pair of random vectors $(X, W)$ drawn from some measure $g$ \textbf{targets} another measure $\pi$ if, for any measurable and bounded functional $h$,
\begin{equation}
    E_g\big[h(X) \cdot W\big]=E_\pi\big[h(X)\big].
\end{equation}  
    A particle approximation is a triangular array of pairs of random vectors $(X^J_j, W^J_j), j=1,2, \ldots,J$.
    The particle approximation \textbf{targets} $\pi$ if, for any measurable and bounded functional $h$,
\begin{equation}
    \frac{\sum_{j=1}^J h(X^J_j) \, W^J_j}{\sum_{j=1}^J W^J_j} \stackrel{a.s.}{\to} E_\pi\big[h(X)\big]
\end{equation}
as $J \to \infty$.
\end{defn}
\cite{chopin04} asserted without proof that common particle filter algorithms target the filtering distribution in this sense, while \cite{chopin20} proved a related result assuming bounded densities.
We follow a similar approach to \cite{chopin20}, based on showing strong laws of large numbers for triangular arrays, noting that triangular array strong laws do not generally hold without an additional regularity condition such as boundedness.
 
In order to prove the consistency of our variation on the particle filter, we now present three helper lemmas.
The first follows from standard importance sampling arguments, the second from integrating out the marginal, and the third from Bayes' theorem. 
We state Lemma~\ref{lem:change-measure-proper-weights} assuming multinomial resampling, which is convenient for the proof though other resampling strategies may be preferable in practice.

\begin{lem}[Change of Weight Measure]
    \label{lem:change-measure-proper-weights}
    Suppose that $\{(\tilde X_j^J,U_j^J),j=1,\dots,J\}$ targets $f_X$. Now, let $\{(Y_j^J,V_j^J),j=1,\dots,J\}$ be a multinomial sample with indices $k_j$ drawn from $\{(\tilde X_j^J,U_j^J)\}$ where $(\tilde X_j^J,U_j^J)$ is represented, on average, proportional to $\pi^J_j J$ times. Write
    \begin{equation}
    (Y_j^J,V_j^J) = \big(\tilde X^J_{k_j},U^J_{k_j}\big/\pi^J_{k_j}\big).
    \end{equation} 
    If the importance sampling weights $U_j/\pi_j$ are bounded, then $\{(Y^J_j,V^J_j),j=1,\dots,J\}$ targets $f_X$.
\end{lem}

\begin{proof}[Proof.]
    Note that as the $Y_j^J$ are a subsample from $X_j^J$, $h$ can be a function of $Y$ as well as it is one for $X$. We then expand
    \begin{equation}\frac{\sum_j h(Y_j^J) \, V_j^J}{\sum_j V_j^J} = \frac{\sum_j h(\tilde X_{k_j}^J)\, \frac{U_{k_j}^J}{\pi_{k_j}^J}}{\sum_j \frac{U_{k_j}^J}{\pi_{k_j}^J}}.\end{equation}
    By hypothesis,
    \begin{equation}
    \frac{\sum_j h(\tilde X_j^J)\, U_j^J}{\sum_j U_j^J} \stackrel{a.s.}{\to} \E_{f_X}\big[f(X)\big].
    \end{equation}
    We want to show
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:lemma1:h}
    \frac{\sum_j h(X_{k_j}^J)\, \frac{U_{k_j}^J}{\pi_{k_j}^J}}{\sum_j \frac{U_{k_j}^J}{\pi_{k_j}^J}} - \frac{\sum_j h(\tilde X_j^J)\, U_j^J}{\sum_j U_j^J} \stackrel{a.s.}{\to} 0.
    \end{equation}
    For this, it is sufficient to show that
   \begin{equation} 
   \sum_j h(\tilde X_{k_j}^J)\frac{U_{k_j}^J}{\pi_{k_j}^J}
    -  \sum_j h(\tilde X_j^J)\frac{U_j^J}{\pi_j^J}\pi_j^J \stackrel{a.s.}{\to} 0 
    \end{equation}
    since an application of this result with $h(x)=1$ provides almost sure convergence of the denominator in (\ref{eq:lemma1:h}).
    Write $g(\tilde X_j^J) = h(\tilde X_j^J)\frac{U_{k_j}^J}{\pi_{k_j}^J}$. We therefore need to show that 
    \begin{equation}
    \sum_j Z_j^J := \sum_j \left(g(\tilde X_{k_j}^J) -  g(\tilde X_j^J) \, \pi_j^J \right) \stackrel{a.s.}{\to} 0.
\end{equation}
    Because the functional $h$ and importance sampling weights $u_{k_j}^J/\pi_{k_j}^J$ are bounded, we have that $\E\big[(Z_j^J)^4\big] < \infty$. We can then follow the argument of \cite{chopin20} from this point on, where noting that the $Z_j^J$ are conditionally independent given the $\tilde{X}_j^J$ and $U_j^J$,
    \begin{eqnarray} 
    \E\left[\left(\sum_j Z_j^J\right)^4 \Bigg| (\tilde X_j^J,U_j^J)_{j=1}^J \right] 
    &=& J\, \E\left[(Z_1^J)^4|(\tilde X_j^J,U_j^J)_{j=1}^J\right] 
    \nonumber
    \\
    && \hspace{1mm}+ \hspace{2mm} 3J(J-1)\left(\E\big[(Z_j^J)^2\big|(\tilde X_j^J,U_j^J)_{j=1}^J\big]\right) 
    \\ 
    &\leq& CJ^2,
    \end{eqnarray}
    for some $C>0$. 
    Taking expectations on both sides yields
    \begin{equation}\E\left[\left(\sum_j Z_j^J\right)^4 \right]  \leq CJ^2\end{equation}
    by the tower property. Now by Markov's inequality, 
    \begin{equation}\mathbb{P}\left(\left|\frac{1}{J} \sum_{j=1}^J Z_j^J\right|>\epsilon \right) 
    \leq \frac{1}{\epsilon^4J^4 } 
    \mathbb{E}\left[\left(\sum_{j=1}^J Z_j^J\right)^4\right] \leq \frac{C}{\epsilon^4J^2},\end{equation}
    and as these terms are summable we can apply Borel-Cantelli to conclude that these deviations happen only finitely often for every $\epsilon>0,$ giving us the almost-sure convergence for   
    \begin{equation} 
    \sum_j h(X_{k_j}^J)\, \frac{u_{k_j}^J}{\pi_{k_j}^J}
    -  \sum_j h(X_j^J)\, \frac{u_j^J}{\pi_j^J}\, \pi_j^J \stackrel{a.s.}{\to} 0.
    \end{equation} 
    Similarly, we also have that
    \begin{equation} 
    \sum_j \frac{u_j^J}{\pi_j^J}\pi_j^J
    - \sum_j \frac{u_{k_j}^J}{\pi_{k_j}^J} \stackrel{a.s.}{\to} 0,
    \end{equation}
    and the result is proved. 

    
    
   % Let $h$ be an integrable function. 
    %\begin{align*}
    %    \E\left[\sum_{j=1}^J h(Y_j) v_j\right] 
    %    &= \sum_{j=1}^J \E\left[h(X_{a(j)}) \frac{u_{a(j)}}{\pi_{a(j)}}\right] \\
    %    &= \sum_{j=1}^J \E\left[h(X_{j}) u_{j}\right],
    %\end{align*}
    %and similarly for the denominator. Now, the numerator and denominator of the reweighted quantity have the same expectation as the numerator and denominator of the original quantity, so they must converge to $ E_\pi(h(X))$ almost surely as well. 
\end{proof}


\noindent \textbf{Remark:} Note that Lemma \ref{lem:change-measure-proper-weights} permits $\pi_{1:J}$ to depend on $\{(X_j,u_j)\}$ as long as the resampling is carried out independently of $\{(X_j,u_j)\}$, conditional on $\pi_{1:J}$.

\begin{lem}[Particle Marginals]
    \label{lem:marginal-proper-weights}
    Suppose that $\{(\tilde X_j^J,U_j^J),j=1,\dots,J\}$ targets $f_X$.
