\begin{thm}[MSE and variance bounds for DMOP-$\alpha$]
\label{thm:dmop-alpha-truncation-mse}
Suppose the observations are fixed, $J\geq2$, $\alpha\in[0,1)$,
$\theta=\phi$, Assumptions {\refAssumpBoundedProcess},
{\refAssumpBoundedMeasurement}, and
{\refAssumpDifferentiableSimulator} hold, and multinomial resampling is
used.  The bounded measurement-gradient input in Assumption~
{\refAssumpBoundedMeasurement} is understood to be the current function
$h_n(X_n)$ in
Equation~(\ref{eq:location-weight-gradient-pair-recursion}). Then
\begin{align}
\Var\left\{\nabla_\theta\hat\ell^\alpha(\theta)\right\}
&\lesssim
pG'(\theta)^2
\left(\frac NJ+\frac{N^2}{J^2}\right)
\frac{1+\alpha\varrho}
{(1-\alpha^2)(1-\alpha\varrho)},
\label{eq:verified-theorem-five-variance}
\\
\E\left\|
\nabla_\theta\hat\ell^\alpha(\theta)
-\nabla_\theta\ell(\theta)
\right\|_2^2
&\lesssim
pG'(\theta)^2
\left[
\left(\frac NJ+\frac{N^2}{J^2}\right)
\frac{1+\alpha\varrho}
{(1-\alpha^2)(1-\alpha\varrho)}
+N^2
\left\{
\frac{\varrho(1-\alpha)}
{(1-\varrho)(1-\alpha\varrho)}
\right\}^2
\right].
\label{eq:verified-theorem-five-mse}
\end{align}
The population component of the fixed-observation bias satisfies
\begin{equation}
\left\|
\nabla_\theta\ell^\alpha(\theta)
-\nabla_\theta\ell(\theta)
\right\|_2
\leq
CN\sqrt p\,G'(\theta)
\frac{\varrho(1-\alpha)}
{(1-\varrho)(1-\alpha\varrho)}.
\label{eq:verified-theorem-five-population-bias}
\end{equation}
If $J\geq N$, the $N^2/J^2$ term in
Equation~(\ref{eq:verified-theorem-five-variance}) is absorbed by the
$N/J$ term, so
\begin{equation}
\Var\left\{\nabla_\theta\hat\ell^\alpha(\theta)\right\}
\lesssim
\frac{NpG'(\theta)^2}{J}
\frac{1+\alpha\varrho}
{(1-\alpha^2)(1-\alpha\varrho)}.
\label{eq:verified-theorem-five-variance-j-geq-n}
\end{equation}
Section~\ref{appendix:sharper-biasvar} gives the complete proof and an
exact constant-weight calculation that checks the $N$ and $J$ dependence
and explains the singularity of the closed $\alpha<1$ bound as
$\alpha\uparrow1$.
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}[Proof.]
Lemma~\ref{lem:dmop-alpha-variance} proves Equation~
(\ref{eq:verified-theorem-five-variance}).  Theorem~
\ref{thm:refined-dmop-alpha-mse} proves Equation~
(\ref{eq:verified-theorem-five-mse}) and Equation~
(\ref{eq:verified-theorem-five-population-bias}).
\end{proof}

\section{Sharper Bias-Variance Analysis}
\label{appendix:sharper-biasvar}

We now establish the estimates used in
Theorem~\ref{thm:dmop-alpha-truncation-mse}. Write
$\nabla_\theta\ell_n^\alpha(\theta)$ for the $n$th
conditional score produced by the deterministic differentiated population
recursion. At $\theta=\phi$, its $\alpha=1$ version is the exact
conditional score $\nabla_\theta\ell_n(\theta)$.  The current DMOP object
needed below is the pair consisting of the particle location and the gradient
of its log weight.  The location follows the ordinary particle
transition to which Assumption~{\refAssumpBoundedProcess} applies.  Along
each selected lineage, the second component obeys the existing recursion
\begin{equation}
\left.\nabla_\theta\log w_{n,j}^{F,\theta}\right|_{\theta=\phi}
=
\alpha
\left.\nabla_\theta\log w_{n-1,k_j}^{F,\theta}\right|_{\theta=\phi}
+h_n(x_{n,j}^{F,\phi}).
\label{eq:location-weight-gradient-pair-recursion}
\end{equation}
Here $h_n$ is the bounded current measurement-gradient function of the
particle location.  This is the derivative at $\theta=\phi$ of the
log-space recursion
\begin{align}
\log w_{n,j}^{P,\theta}
&=\alpha\log w_{n-1,j}^{F,\theta},                         \notag\\
\log w_{n,j}^{F,\theta}
&=\alpha\log w_{n-1,k_j}^{F,\theta}
+\log g_{n,k_j}^{\theta}-\log g_{n,k_j}^{\phi}.
\label{eq:log-weight-recursion}
\end{align}
At $\theta=\phi$, the log weights are zero but their gradients need not be.
We do not assume that the location--log-weight-gradient pair is mixing in
total variation.  Instead, the proof expands the log-weight gradient
into its single-time $h_n$ contributions, applies location
forgetting to those contributions, and sums their powers of $\alpha$.
Throughout this section, the observations are fixed. Expectations and
variances are over the simulator and particle-filter random variables,
conditional on those observations.

We use Assumptions {\refAssumpBoundedMeasurement} and
{\refAssumpDifferentiableSimulator} in the precise pathwise sense already
required by the differentiated MOP construction. For fixed simulator random
numbers and fixed resampling indices, Assumption
{\refAssumpDifferentiableSimulator} says that the recursively simulated
state path and the composed log measurement densities are continuously
differentiable in $\theta$ near $\phi$, and that the joint law of the base
simulator random numbers does not depend on $\theta$. The derivative in the
last clause of Assumption {\refAssumpBoundedMeasurement} is the resulting
total derivative:
uniformly in $n,j$ and in a neighborhood of $\phi$, after enlarging
$G'(\theta)$ to bound that neighborhood if necessary,
\begin{equation}
\left\|
\nabla_\theta\log g_{n,j}^{A,F,\theta}
\right\|_\infty
\leq G'(\theta).
\label{eq:bounded-realized-measurement-score}
\end{equation}
These two existing assumptions also justify differentiation under the
finite-horizon simulator expectations used below. Indeed, the product rule
and Equation~(\ref{eq:bounded-realized-measurement-score}) give, pathwise,
\begin{align}
&\left\|
\nabla_\theta\prod_{s=1}^t G_s(X_s)
\right\|_\infty                                                   \notag\\
&\quad=
\left\|
\prod_{s=1}^t G_s(X_s)
\sum_{s=1}^t\nabla_\theta\log G_s(X_s)
\right\|_\infty                                                   \notag\\
&\leq
\prod_{s=1}^t G_s(X_s)
\sum_{s=1}^t
\left\|\nabla_\theta\log G_s(X_s)\right\|_\infty                  \notag\\
&\leq
t\bar G^tG'(\theta).
\label{eq:finite-horizon-product-derivative-bound}
\end{align}
The final expression is deterministic and finite for each fixed $t$. For
each coordinate of $\theta$, the mean-value theorem therefore bounds every
difference quotient about $\phi$ by the same quantity
$t\bar G^tG'(\theta)$. The dominated convergence theorem gives
\begin{equation}
\nabla_\theta
\E_U\left[\prod_{s=1}^tG_s(X_s)\right]
=
\E_U\left[
\nabla_\theta\prod_{s=1}^tG_s(X_s)
\right].
\label{eq:finite-horizon-differentiation-under-expectation}
\end{equation}
Thus the required interchange follows from the clarified Assumptions
{\refAssumpBoundedMeasurement} and
{\refAssumpDifferentiableSimulator}; it is not an additional assumption.
Geometric population forgetting follows from the backward-kernel
contraction under Assumption {\refAssumpBoundedProcess}.  Lemma~
\ref{lem:lagwise-weak-error} gives a uniform weak-error bound for a
current filtering functional.  Its proof is not used for a historical
functional carried by the genealogy; that separate issue is audited
below.

For the path functionals below, evaluated at $\theta=\phi$, let $\rho_t$
be the posterior path measure of the particle locations. Thus, for
every integrable path functional $h$,
\begin{equation}
\rho_t(h)
=
\frac{
\E\left[
h(X_{0:t})
\prod_{s=1}^t G_s(X_s)
\right]
}
{
\E\left[\prod_{s=1}^t G_s(X_s)\right]
}.
\label{eq:canonical-extended-posterior-path-law}
\end{equation}
Here $G_s(X_s)$ is the existing measurement potential.  The accumulated
weight gradient is the second component of the current pair in Equation~
(\ref{eq:location-weight-gradient-pair-recursion}); it is not included in
the location variable $X_s$ or in the transition density appearing in
Assumption~{\refAssumpBoundedProcess}.

Unrolling the existing filtered-weight recursion at \(\theta=\phi\) gives
\begin{equation}
\left.\nabla_\theta\log w_{n,j}^{F,\theta}\right|_{\theta=\phi}
=
\sum_{r=0}^{n-1}\alpha^r
\left.\nabla_\theta\log g_{n-r,j}^{A,F,\theta}\right|_{\theta=\phi}.
\label{eq:unrolled-filtered-weight}
\end{equation}
For a complete ancestral path, write \(h_k\), only in this section, for the
measurement-score contribution
\begin{equation}
h_k
=
\left.\nabla_\theta\log g_k^{A,F,\theta}\right|_{\theta=\phi}
\label{eq:actual-path-score}
\end{equation}
carried on that path. In the path-space notation of
\citet{chanDelMoral18}, the notation used below means explicitly
\begin{equation}
m(\xi_t)
=
\frac1J\sum_{j=1}^{J}\delta_{\xi_{t,j}},
\qquad
m(\xi_t)(h_k)
=
\frac1J\sum_{j=1}^{J}h_k(\xi_{t,j}),
\quad 1\leq k\leq t,
\label{eq:explicit-path-empirical-measure}
\end{equation}
where \(\xi_{t,j}\) is the complete ancestral particle path carried by
particle \(j\) at time \(t\).  Thus \(h_k(\xi_{t,j})\) is the single
time-$k$ measurement-score contribution on that particle, not an
arbitrary functional denoted by $m$.  Equation~
(\ref{eq:canonical-extended-posterior-path-law}) gives its population
counterpart.

We now identify the lag coefficients in the conditional score. At
\(\theta=\phi\), all weights equal one, so differentiating the two sample
averages in the definition of \(L_n^{A,\theta,\alpha}\) gives
\begin{align}
\nabla_\theta\hat\ell_n^\alpha(\theta)
&=
\frac1J\sum_{j=1}^J
\left.\nabla_\theta\log w_{n,j}^{F,\theta}\right|_{\theta=\phi}
-
\frac1J\sum_{j=1}^J
\left.\nabla_\theta\log w_{n,j}^{P,\theta}\right|_{\theta=\phi} \notag\\
&=
\frac1J\sum_{j=1}^J
\left.\nabla_\theta\log w_{n,j}^{F,\theta}\right|_{\theta=\phi}
-
\frac{\alpha}{J}\sum_{j=1}^J
\left.\nabla_\theta\log w_{n-1,j}^{F,\theta}\right|_{\theta=\phi},
\label{eq:conditional-score-weight-difference}
\end{align}
where the second equality uses
\(w_{n,j}^{P,\theta}=(w_{n-1,j}^{F,\theta})^\alpha\). In the
MOP-$\alpha$ recursion, the discounted-weight accumulation and process
propagation preserve the index
\(j\) and occur before the time-\(n\) resampling step. The resampling index
\(k_j\) enters only afterward, through
\begin{equation}
w_{n,j}^{F,\theta}
=
w_{n,k_j}^{P,\theta}
\frac{g_{n,k_j}^{\theta}}{g_{n,k_j}^{\phi}}.
\label{eq:filtered-weight-current-resampling-index}
\end{equation}
Consequently, the second average in
Equation~(\ref{eq:conditional-score-weight-difference}) is pathwise
\begin{equation}
\frac1J\sum_{j=1}^J
\left.\nabla_\theta\log w_{n,j}^{P,\theta}\right|_{\theta=\phi}
=
\frac{\alpha}{J}\sum_{j=1}^J
\left.\nabla_\theta\log w_{n-1,j}^{F,\theta}\right|_{\theta=\phi},
\label{eq:prediction-weight-index-identity}
\end{equation}
which is the empirical average over the time-\((n-1)\) filtered genealogy.
Substituting
Equation~(\ref{eq:unrolled-filtered-weight}) into
Equation~(\ref{eq:conditional-score-weight-difference}) and changing the
index in the second sum give
\begin{align}
\nabla_\theta\hat\ell_n^\alpha(\theta)
&=
\sum_{r=0}^{n-1}\alpha^r m(\xi_n)(h_{n-r})
-\sum_{r=1}^{n-1}\alpha^r m(\xi_{n-1})(h_{n-r}) \notag\\
&=
m(\xi_n)(h_n)
+\sum_{r=1}^{n-1}\alpha^r
\left\{m(\xi_n)(h_{n-r})-m(\xi_{n-1})(h_{n-r})\right\}.
\label{eq:conditional-score-path-decomposition}
\end{align}
Consequently, define
\begin{align}
\Delta_{n,0}^J
&=m(\xi_n)(h_n),
&
\Delta_{n,0}
&=\rho_n(h_n), \notag\\
\Delta_{n,r}^J
&=m(\xi_n)(h_{n-r})-m(\xi_{n-1})(h_{n-r}),
&
\Delta_{n,r}
&=\rho_n(h_{n-r})-\rho_{n-1}(h_{n-r}),
\quad 1\leq r<n.
\label{eq:lag-coefficient-definitions}
\end{align}
We now verify that the right-hand population quantities are the lag
coefficients of the deterministic differentiated recursion. Equations~
(\ref{eq:finite-horizon-differentiation-under-expectation}) and
(\ref{eq:actual-path-score}) give, at $\theta=\phi$,
\begin{align}
&\nabla_\theta
\log\E_U\left[\prod_{s=1}^nG_s(X_s)\right]                       \notag\\
&\quad=
\frac{
\E_U\left[
\prod_{s=1}^nG_s(X_s)\sum_{s=1}^nh_s
\right]
}
{
\E_U\left[\prod_{s=1}^nG_s(X_s)\right]
}                                                               \notag\\
&\quad=
\sum_{s=1}^n\rho_n(h_s).
\label{eq:full-population-score-path-average}
\end{align}
Subtracting the same identity at time $n-1$ gives
\begin{align}
\nabla_\theta\ell_n(\theta)
&=
\sum_{s=1}^n\rho_n(h_s)-\sum_{s=1}^{n-1}\rho_{n-1}(h_s)          \notag\\
&=
\rho_n(h_n)
+\sum_{r=1}^{n-1}
\left\{\rho_n(h_{n-r})-\rho_{n-1}(h_{n-r})\right\}               \notag\\
&=
\sum_{r=0}^{n-1}\Delta_{n,r}.
\label{eq:exact-population-conditional-score-path-decomposition}
\end{align}
Finally, differentiating the two population weighted averages in the
deterministic MOP-$\alpha$ recursion and using
Equation~(\ref{eq:finite-horizon-differentiation-under-expectation}) gives
\begin{align}
\nabla_\theta\ell_n^\alpha(\theta)
&=
\sum_{r=0}^{n-1}\alpha^r\rho_n(h_{n-r})
-\sum_{r=1}^{n-1}\alpha^r\rho_{n-1}(h_{n-r})                   \notag\\
&=
\rho_n(h_n)
+\sum_{r=1}^{n-1}\alpha^r
\left\{\rho_n(h_{n-r})-\rho_{n-1}(h_{n-r})\right\}             \notag\\
&=
\sum_{r=0}^{n-1}\alpha^r\Delta_{n,r}.
\label{eq:population-conditional-score-path-decomposition}
\end{align}

\begin{lem}[Lagwise first-derivative forgetting]
\label{lem:derivative-measure-forgetting}
Suppose $\theta=\phi$. Under Assumptions {\refAssumpBoundedProcess},
{\refAssumpBoundedMeasurement}, and {\refAssumpDifferentiableSimulator},
assume additionally that the bounded measurement-gradient input is the
current function $h_n(X_n)$ in
Equation~(\ref{eq:location-weight-gradient-pair-recursion}).  Then
there are
constants $C<\infty$ and $\varrho\in(0,1)$ such that, for
$0\leq r<n$,
\begin{equation}
\|\Delta_{n,r}\|_2
\leq C\sqrt p\,G'(\theta)\varrho^r.
\label{eq:derivative-measure-forgetting}
\end{equation}
\end{lem}

\begin{proof}[Proof.]
We derive the result from the posterior update and the contraction of the
posterior backward kernels.

\medskip\noindent\emph{Step 1: one-step posterior update.}\quad
First suppose that \(1\leq r<n\), and put \(k=n-r\). Fix a coordinate
\(i\in\{1,\ldots,p\}\), and let \(h_{k,i}\) be the corresponding
coordinate of $h_k$ in Equation~(\ref{eq:actual-path-score}). By
Equation~(\ref{eq:bounded-realized-measurement-score}),
\begin{equation}
|h_{k,i}|
\leq
G'(\theta).
\label{eq:population-forgetting-score-bound}
\end{equation}
Because \(h_{k,i}\) is measurable with respect to the canonical extended
path through time \(k\leq n-1\), Equation~
(\ref{eq:canonical-extended-posterior-path-law}) and conditional expectation
with respect to the new base simulator variable give
\begin{align}
\rho_n(h_{k,i})
&=
\frac{
\E_U\left[
h_{k,i}\prod_{s=1}^{n-1}G_s(X_s)
\E_U\{G_n(X_n)\mid X_{n-1}\}
\right]
}
{
\E_U\left[
\prod_{s=1}^{n-1}G_s(X_s)
\E_U\{G_n(X_n)\mid X_{n-1}\}
\right]
}                                                               \notag\\
&=
\frac{
\rho_{n-1}\{h_{k,i}M_n(G_n)\}
}
{\rho_{n-1}M_n(G_n)},
\label{eq:posterior-path-update-functional}
\end{align}
where the first equality uses the independence of the new base simulator
variable from the path through time \(n-1\). The second equality first uses
the identity below and then divides its numerator and denominator by
\(\E_U\{\prod_{s=1}^{n-1}G_s(X_s)\}\). Using the manuscript's kernel
notation, the required identity is
\begin{equation}
M_n(G_n)(x_{n-1})
=
\int M_n(x_{n-1},dx_n)G_n(x_n).
\label{eq:predictive-potential-functional}
\end{equation}
The bounds on \(G_n\) and the fact that \(M_n\) is a Markov kernel imply
for every \(x_{n-1}\) that
\begin{align}
M_n(G_n)(x_{n-1})
&\geq
\underbar G\int M_n(x_{n-1},dx_n)
=
\underbar G,
\label{eq:predictive-potential-lower}
\\
M_n(G_n)(x_{n-1})
&\leq
\bar G\int M_n(x_{n-1},dx_n)
=
\bar G.
\label{eq:predictive-potential-upper}
\end{align}

\medskip\noindent\emph{Step 2: backward-kernel contraction.}\quad
We next control how much information about the path through time \(k\)
can remain at time \(n-1\). For \(s\geq1\), the posterior backward kernel
from \(X_s\) to \(X_{s-1}\), written only in this proof as \(B_s\), is
\begin{equation}
B_s(x_s,dx_{s-1})
=
\frac{
\pi_{s-1}(dx_{s-1})
f_{X_s\mid X_{s-1}}(x_s\mid x_{s-1};\theta)
}
{\displaystyle
\int
\pi_{s-1}(du)
f_{X_s\mid X_{s-1}}(x_s\mid u;\theta)}.
\label{eq:posterior-backward-kernel}
\end{equation}
Assumption {\refAssumpBoundedProcess} gives the following minorization for
every measurable set \(A\):
\begin{align}
B_s(x_s,A)
&=
\frac{
\displaystyle
\int_A
\pi_{s-1}(dx_{s-1})
f_{X_s\mid X_{s-1}}(x_s\mid x_{s-1};\theta)
}
{\displaystyle
\int
\pi_{s-1}(du)
f_{X_s\mid X_{s-1}}(x_s\mid u;\theta)}                       \notag\\
&\geq
\frac{
\underbar M\,\pi_{s-1}(A)
}
{\bar M\int\pi_{s-1}(du)}                                   \notag\\
&=
\frac{\underbar M}{\bar M}\pi_{s-1}(A).
\label{eq:posterior-backward-minorization}
\end{align}
Put \(\epsilon=\underbar M/\bar M\). If \(\epsilon<1\), Equation~
(\ref{eq:posterior-backward-minorization}) permits the decomposition
\begin{equation}
B_s(x_s,\cdot)
=
\epsilon\pi_{s-1}(\cdot)
+(1-\epsilon)R_s(x_s,\cdot),
\label{eq:posterior-backward-splitting}
\end{equation}
where
\begin{equation}
R_s(x_s,A)
=
\frac{B_s(x_s,A)-\epsilon\pi_{s-1}(A)}{1-\epsilon}
\end{equation}
is a Markov kernel. Hence, for every bounded real function \(f\) and any
\(x_s,x'_s\),
\begin{align}
B_s(f)(x_s)-B_s(f)(x'_s)
&=
(1-\epsilon)
\{R_s(f)(x_s)-R_s(f)(x'_s)\},                                \notag\\
\left|B_s(f)(x_s)-B_s(f)(x'_s)\right|
&\leq
(1-\epsilon)\operatorname{osc}(f).
\label{eq:posterior-backward-one-step-contraction}
\end{align}
If \(\epsilon=1\), Equation~
(\ref{eq:posterior-backward-minorization}) implies
\(B_s(x_s,\cdot)=\pi_{s-1}(\cdot)\), so the left-hand side is zero.
Taking the supremum over \(x_s,x'_s\) gives
\begin{equation}
\operatorname{osc}\{B_s(f)\}
\leq
(1-\epsilon)\operatorname{osc}(f).
\label{eq:posterior-backward-oscillation-contraction}
\end{equation}

\medskip\noindent\emph{Step 3: propagate the time-$k$ contribution.}\quad
The function $h_{k,i}=h_{k,i}(X_k)$ is a bounded current function of the
particle location $X_k$. Repeated conditioning through the backward kernels
therefore gives
\begin{align}
\E_{\rho_{n-1}}\{h_{k,i}(X_k)\mid X_{n-1}=x_{n-1}\}
&=
(B_{n-1}B_{n-2}\cdots B_{k+1}h_{k,i})(x_{n-1}).
\label{eq:iterated-backward-conditioning}
\end{align}
There are \(n-1-k=r-1\) backward kernels in this display. Moreover,
Equation~(\ref{eq:population-forgetting-score-bound}) gives
\begin{align}
\operatorname{osc}(h_{k,i})
&\leq
2\|h_{k,i}\|_\infty                                          \notag\\
&\leq
2G'(\theta).
\label{eq:conditional-past-score-oscillation}
\end{align}
If \(r=1\), Equation~(\ref{eq:iterated-backward-conditioning}) contains no
backward kernel. If \(r\geq2\), applying Equation~
(\ref{eq:posterior-backward-oscillation-contraction}) successively gives
\begin{align}
&\operatorname{osc}\!\left[
\E_{\rho_{n-1}}\{h_{k,i}\mid X_{n-1}=\mathord\cdot\}
\right]                                                       \notag\\
&\qquad\leq
2G'(\theta)
\begin{cases}
1, & r=1,\\
(1-\epsilon)^{r-1}, & r\geq2.
\end{cases}
\label{eq:conditional-past-score-forgetting}
\end{align}

\medskip\noindent\emph{Step 4: covariance identity and geometric bound.}\quad
To finish, abbreviate the conditional expectation on the left of
Equation~(\ref{eq:conditional-past-score-forgetting}) by \(H\), only for
the next calculation. Since the \(X_{n-1}\)-marginal of \(\rho_{n-1}\)
is \(\pi_{n-1}\), the tower property gives
\begin{align}
\rho_{n-1}(h_{k,i})
&=
\pi_{n-1}(H),
\label{eq:tower-past-score}
\\
\rho_{n-1}\{h_{k,i}M_n(G_n)\}
&=
\pi_{n-1}\{H M_n(G_n)\}.
\label{eq:tower-past-score-potential}
\end{align}
Substituting Equations~(\ref{eq:tower-past-score})--
(\ref{eq:tower-past-score-potential}) into
Equation~(\ref{eq:posterior-path-update-functional}) and then subtracting
\(\rho_{n-1}(h_{k,i})\) gives the exact covariance identity
\begin{align}
\Delta_{n,r,i}
&=
\rho_n(h_{k,i})-\rho_{n-1}(h_{k,i})                           \notag\\
&=
\frac{
\pi_{n-1}\{H M_n(G_n)\}
-\pi_{n-1}(H)\pi_{n-1}M_n(G_n)
}
{\pi_{n-1}M_n(G_n)}                                          \notag\\
&=
\frac{
\pi_{n-1}\!\left[
\{H-\pi_{n-1}(H)\}
\{M_n(G_n)-\pi_{n-1}M_n(G_n)\}
\right]
}
{\pi_{n-1}M_n(G_n)}.
\label{eq:lag-coefficient-covariance-identity}
\end{align}
For any probability measure \(\mu\) and bounded real \(f\),
\begin{equation}
|f(x)-\mu(f)|
\leq
\operatorname{osc}(f).
\label{eq:centered-function-oscillation-bound}
\end{equation}
The first positive lag also inherits one contraction from the forward
transition.  To see this directly, fix \(x^\star\).  Assumption
{\refAssumpBoundedProcess} gives, for every measurable set \(A\),
\begin{align}
M_n(x,A)
&=
\int_A
f_{X_n\mid X_{n-1}}(x_n\mid x;\theta)\,dx_n                  \notag\\
&\geq
\underbar M\int_A dx_n                                      \notag\\
&\geq
\frac{\underbar M}{\bar M}
\int_A
f_{X_n\mid X_{n-1}}(x_n\mid x^\star;\theta)\,dx_n           \notag\\
&=
\epsilon M_n(x^\star,A).
\label{eq:forward-transition-minorization}
\end{align}
If \(\epsilon<1\), Equation~
(\ref{eq:forward-transition-minorization}) permits the decomposition
\begin{equation}
M_n(x,\cdot)
=
\epsilon M_n(x^\star,\cdot)
+(1-\epsilon)R_n(x,\cdot),
\label{eq:forward-transition-splitting}
\end{equation}
where \(R_n\) is a Markov kernel.  Therefore, for any \(x,x'\),
\begin{align}
M_n(G_n)(x)-M_n(G_n)(x')
&=
(1-\epsilon)
\{R_n(G_n)(x)-R_n(G_n)(x')\},                              \notag\\
\left|M_n(G_n)(x)-M_n(G_n)(x')\right|
&\leq
(1-\epsilon)\operatorname{osc}(G_n)                        \notag\\
&\leq
(1-\epsilon)(\bar G-\underbar G).
\end{align}
Taking the supremum over \(x,x'\) gives
\begin{equation}
\operatorname{osc}\{M_n(G_n)\}
\leq
(1-\epsilon)(\bar G-\underbar G).
\label{eq:predictive-potential-oscillation}
\end{equation}
If \(\epsilon=1\), Equation~
(\ref{eq:forward-transition-minorization}) implies that \(M_n(x,\cdot)\)
does not depend on \(x\), so the left side of Equation~
(\ref{eq:predictive-potential-oscillation}) is zero and the same bound
holds.
Taking absolute values in Equation~
(\ref{eq:lag-coefficient-covariance-identity}), using
Equations~(\ref{eq:centered-function-oscillation-bound})--
(\ref{eq:predictive-potential-oscillation}), and then using
Equation~(\ref{eq:conditional-past-score-forgetting}), yields
\begin{align}
|\Delta_{n,r,i}|
&\leq
\frac{
\operatorname{osc}(H)
\operatorname{osc}\{M_n(G_n)\}
}
{\underbar G}                                                 \notag\\
&\leq
\frac{2(\bar G-\underbar G)}{\underbar G}
G'(\theta)
\begin{cases}
(1-\epsilon), & r=1,\\
(1-\epsilon)^r, & r\geq2.
\end{cases}
\label{eq:lag-coordinate-geometric-bound}
\end{align}
For both cases, take
\begin{equation}
\varrho=1-\frac{\epsilon}{2}
=1-\frac{\underbar M}{2\bar M}\in(0,1).
\label{eq:population-forgetting-varrho}
\end{equation}
Since $1-\epsilon\leq\varrho$, the two cases in Equation~
(\ref{eq:lag-coordinate-geometric-bound}) are both bounded by
$\varrho^r$.
Summing the squared coordinate bounds and taking square roots give
\begin{align}
\|\Delta_{n,r}\|_2
&=
\left\{\sum_{i=1}^p|\Delta_{n,r,i}|^2\right\}^{1/2}          \notag\\
&\leq
C\sqrt p\,G'(\theta)\varrho^r,
\qquad 1\leq r<n.
\label{eq:lag-vector-geometric-bound}
\end{align}

\medskip\noindent\emph{Step 5: the zero-lag case.}\quad
Finally, when \(r=0\), Equation~
(\ref{eq:lag-coefficient-definitions}) and
Equation~(\ref{eq:bounded-realized-measurement-score}) give
\begin{align}
\|\Delta_{n,0}\|_2
&=
\|\rho_n(h_n)\|_2                                            \notag\\
&\leq
\rho_n(\|h_n\|_2)                                            \notag\\
&\leq
\sqrt p\,G'(\theta)                                         \notag\\
&\leq
C\sqrt p\,G'(\theta)\varrho^0.
\label{eq:current-lag-vector-bound}
\end{align}
Equations~(\ref{eq:lag-vector-geometric-bound}) and
(\ref{eq:current-lag-vector-bound}) prove
Equation~(\ref{eq:derivative-measure-forgetting}).
If \(\epsilon=1\), Equation~
(\ref{eq:lag-coefficient-covariance-identity}) and the zero oscillation in
Equation~(\ref{eq:predictive-potential-oscillation}) show that
\(\Delta_{n,r}=0\) for every \(r\geq1\), so any fixed
\(\varrho\in(0,1)\) proves the same result.  Thus \(\varrho\) depends only
on the model minorization bounds and is independent of \(N,J,\alpha\), and
any lag truncation \(k\).  In particular, the factor \(\varrho^r\) includes
the contraction of the first positive lag; it is not only a tail bound.

\end{proof}

\begin{lem}[Uniform weak bias of current-particle averages]
\label{lem:lagwise-weak-error}
Suppose $\theta=\phi$. Under Assumptions {\refAssumpBoundedProcess},
{\refAssumpBoundedMeasurement}, and
{\refAssumpDifferentiableSimulator}, with multinomial resampling, there is
$C<\infty$ such that, for every bounded real function $f$ of the current
particle location and every $t\geq1$,
\begin{equation}
\left|
\E m(\xi_t)(f)-\rho_t(f)
\right|
\leq
\frac{C}{J}\operatorname{osc}(f).
\label{eq:uniform-current-particle-weak-bias}
\end{equation}
The constant is independent of $t$ and $J$.
Here ``current'' means measurable with respect to the time-$t$ particle
location to which Assumption {\refAssumpBoundedProcess} applies.  In
particular, $h_t=h_t(X_t)$ is current at time $t$.  For $s<t$, however,
$h_s(\xi_{t,j})$ is selected through the ancestry and is not assumed to
be a function of the current location alone.  The lemma is therefore not
applied directly to a historical $h_s$ at a later time.
\end{lem}

\begin{proof}[Proof.]
The proof has four stages: reduce the filtered average to the prediction
cloud, establish contraction of the normalized semigroup, compute the
$J^{-1}$ bias of one particle update, and telescope those updates.

\medskip\noindent\emph{Step 1: prediction-to-filtering reduction.}\quad
The predictor cloud consists of the current particle locations.  A bounded
function of the current location is therefore a current-state functional in
the Feynman--Kac model already defined in Section~\ref{appendix:feynman}.
This statement does not turn an accumulated weight gradient or an
individual ancestral contribution into a function of the current location.
The current-location marginal of
$\rho_t$ is the filtering distribution, and hence
\begin{equation}
\rho_t(f)=\pi_t(f)=\Psi_t(\eta_t)(f).
\label{eq:current-state-posterior-filter-identity}
\end{equation}
Conditionally on the prediction cloud $\eta_t^J$, multinomial resampling
draws the filtering particles independently from $\Psi_t(\eta_t^J)$.
Therefore
\begin{align}
\E\{m(\xi_t)(f)\mid\eta_t^J\}
&=
\frac1J\sum_{j=1}^J
\E\{f(x_{t,j}^F)\mid\eta_t^J\}                              \notag\\
&=
\frac1J\sum_{j=1}^J\Psi_t(\eta_t^J)(f)                     \notag\\
&=
\Psi_t(\eta_t^J)(f).
\label{eq:terminal-resampling-unbiased}
\end{align}

\medskip\noindent\emph{Step 2: normalized-semigroup contraction.}\quad
Take $\Phi_{t,t}$ to be the identity. For $1\leq p\leq t$, define
\begin{align}
Q_{t,t}(f)(x)
&=G_t(x)f(x),                                                \notag\\
Q_{p,t}(f)(x)
&=G_p(x)M_{p+1}\{Q_{p+1,t}(f)\}(x),
\qquad p<t.
\label{eq:weak-bias-semigroup-definition}
\end{align}
The definitions of $\Phi_{p,t}$ and $\Psi_t$ give, for every probability
measure $\mu$,
\begin{equation}
\Psi_t\{\Phi_{p,t}(\mu)\}(f)
=
\frac{\mu Q_{p,t}(f)}{\mu Q_{p,t}(1)}.
\label{eq:normalized-feynman-kac-semigroup}
\end{equation}

For $p<t$,
\begin{equation}
Q_{p,t}(1)(x)
=
G_p(x)\int
f_{X_{p+1}\mid X_p}(u\mid x;\theta)
Q_{p+1,t}(1)(u)\,du.
\label{eq:weak-bias-future-normalizer}
\end{equation}
Thus, for every $x,x'$,
\begin{align}
\frac{Q_{p,t}(1)(x)}{Q_{p,t}(1)(x')}
&\leq
\frac{
\bar G\bar M\int Q_{p+1,t}(1)(u)\,du
}{
\underbar G\underbar M\int Q_{p+1,t}(1)(u)\,du
}                                                        \notag\\
&=
\frac{\bar G\bar M}{\underbar G\underbar M}.
\label{eq:weak-bias-normalizer-ratio}
\end{align}
For $p=t$, the same ratio is at most $\bar G/\underbar G$. Put
\begin{equation}
R=\frac{\bar G\bar M}{\underbar G\underbar M}\geq1.
\label{eq:weak-bias-normalizer-constant}
\end{equation}
Then
\begin{equation}
\sup_{x,x'}
\frac{Q_{p,t}(1)(x)}{Q_{p,t}(1)(x')}
\leq R,
\qquad 1\leq p\leq t.
\label{eq:weak-bias-uniform-normalizer-ratio}
\end{equation}

For $p\leq s<t$, the normalized forward kernel is
\begin{equation}
P_{s,s+1}^{(t)}(x,A)
=
\frac{
\displaystyle\int_A
f_{X_{s+1}\mid X_s}(u\mid x;\theta)
Q_{s+1,t}(1)(u)\,du
}{
\displaystyle\int
f_{X_{s+1}\mid X_s}(u\mid x;\theta)
Q_{s+1,t}(1)(u)\,du
}.
\label{eq:weak-bias-forward-normalized-kernel}
\end{equation}
Define, only within this proof,
\begin{equation}
\nu_{s,t}(A)
=
\frac{
\displaystyle\int_AQ_{s+1,t}(1)(u)\,du
}{
\displaystyle\int Q_{s+1,t}(1)(u)\,du
}.
\label{eq:weak-bias-forward-minorizing-measure}
\end{equation}
Assumption~{\refAssumpBoundedProcess} gives
\begin{align}
P_{s,s+1}^{(t)}(x,A)
&\geq
\frac{
\underbar M\displaystyle\int_AQ_{s+1,t}(1)(u)\,du
}{
\bar M\displaystyle\int Q_{s+1,t}(1)(u)\,du
}                                                        \notag\\
&=
\frac{\underbar M}{\bar M}\nu_{s,t}(A).
\label{eq:weak-bias-forward-minorization}
\end{align}
Consequently, by Equation~
(\ref{eq:population-forgetting-varrho}), the Dobrushin coefficient of
every kernel in Equation~
(\ref{eq:weak-bias-forward-normalized-kernel}) is at most $\varrho$.
The factor $G_s(x)$ cancels from the normalized conditional
expectation, and hence
\begin{equation}
\frac{Q_{p,t}(f)}{Q_{p,t}(1)}
=
P_{p,p+1}^{(t)}P_{p+1,p+2}^{(t)}\cdots
P_{t-1,t}^{(t)}f.
\label{eq:weak-bias-forward-kernel-factorization}
\end{equation}
It follows that
\begin{equation}
\operatorname{osc}
\left\{
\frac{Q_{p,t}(f)}{Q_{p,t}(1)}
\right\}
\leq
\varrho^{t-p}\operatorname{osc}(f).
\label{eq:weak-bias-normalized-semigroup-contraction}
\end{equation}

\medskip\noindent\emph{Step 3: one complete particle update.}\quad
We next bound one complete selection--mutation update. For $p=1$, put
$\mu=\eta_1=\pi_0M_1$. For $p\geq2$, condition on $\eta_{p-1}^J$ and put
\begin{equation}
\mu=\Phi_p(\eta_{p-1}^J).
\label{eq:weak-bias-local-conditional-law}
\end{equation}
In either case, the particles forming $\eta_p^J$ are conditionally
independent with common law $\mu$.  Here $Q_{p,t}$ propagates the local
error to time $t$: $q$ isolates its random normalizer, while $d$ is the
centered empirical fluctuation.  Within this calculation, put
\begin{align}
q
&=
\frac{Q_{p,t}(1)}{\mu Q_{p,t}(1)},                            &
a
&=
\frac{\mu Q_{p,t}(f)}{\mu Q_{p,t}(1)},                        &
d
&=
\frac{Q_{p,t}(f-a)}{\mu Q_{p,t}(1)}.
\label{eq:weak-bias-local-functions}
\end{align}
Then
\begin{equation}
\mu(q)=1,
\qquad
\mu(d)=0,
\label{eq:weak-bias-local-centering}
\end{equation}
and Equation~(\ref{eq:normalized-feynman-kac-semigroup}) gives
\begin{align}
&\Psi_t\{\Phi_{p,t}(\eta_p^J)\}(f)
-\Psi_t\{\Phi_{p,t}(\mu)\}(f)                              \notag\\
&\quad=
\frac{\eta_p^JQ_{p,t}(f)}{\eta_p^JQ_{p,t}(1)}-a             \notag\\
&\quad=
\frac{\eta_p^JQ_{p,t}(f-a)}{\eta_p^JQ_{p,t}(1)}             \notag\\
&\quad=
\frac{\eta_p^J(d)}{\eta_p^J(q)}.
\label{eq:weak-bias-local-ratio}
\end{align}

Equation~(\ref{eq:weak-bias-uniform-normalizer-ratio}) implies
\begin{equation}
R^{-1}\leq q(x)\leq R,
\qquad
\eta_p^J(q)\geq R^{-1},
\qquad
\|q-1\|_\infty\leq R.
\label{eq:weak-bias-q-bounds}
\end{equation}
Writing
\begin{equation}
F=\frac{Q_{p,t}(f)}{Q_{p,t}(1)},
\end{equation}
we have $a=\mu(qF)$ and $\mu(q)=1$. Thus $a$ is an average of
$F$ under the probability measure $q\,d\mu$, so
\begin{equation}
\inf F\leq a\leq\sup F.
\label{eq:weak-bias-a-range}
\end{equation}
Since $d=q(F-a)$, Equations~(\ref{eq:weak-bias-q-bounds}),
(\ref{eq:weak-bias-a-range}), and
(\ref{eq:weak-bias-normalized-semigroup-contraction}) give
\begin{align}
\|d\|_\infty
&\leq
R\operatorname{osc}(F)                                    \notag\\
&\leq
R\varrho^{t-p}\operatorname{osc}(f).
\label{eq:weak-bias-d-bound}
\end{align}

Conditional independence and $\mu(d)=0$ give
\begin{align}
\E\{\eta_p^J(d)\mid\mu\}
&=0,                                                       \notag\\
\E\{\eta_p^J(d)^2\mid\mu\}
&=\frac1J\mu(d^2),                                        \notag\\
\E\{(\eta_p^J(q)-1)^2\mid\mu\}
&=\frac1J\mu\{(q-1)^2\}.
\label{eq:weak-bias-empirical-second-moments}
\end{align}
Consequently,
\begin{align}
&\E\left\{
\left.
\frac{\eta_p^J(d)}{\eta_p^J(q)}
\right|\mu
\right\}                                                    \notag\\
&\quad=
-\E\left\{
\left.
\frac{\eta_p^J(d)\{\eta_p^J(q)-1\}}
{\eta_p^J(q)}
\right|\mu
\right\}.
\label{eq:weak-bias-local-cancellation}
\end{align}
Conditional Cauchy--Schwarz and Equations~
(\ref{eq:weak-bias-q-bounds})--
(\ref{eq:weak-bias-empirical-second-moments}) yield
\begin{align}
&\left|
\E\left\{
\left.
\frac{\eta_p^J(d)}{\eta_p^J(q)}
\right|\mu
\right\}
\right|                                                       \notag\\
&\quad\leq
R
\left[
\E\{\eta_p^J(d)^2\mid\mu\}
\E\{(\eta_p^J(q)-1)^2\mid\mu\}
\right]^{1/2}                                                \notag\\
&\quad=
\frac{R}{J}
\left[
\mu(d^2)\mu\{(q-1)^2\}
\right]^{1/2}                                                \notag\\
&\quad\leq
\frac{C}{J}\varrho^{t-p}\operatorname{osc}(f).
\label{eq:weak-bias-one-complete-update}
\end{align}

\medskip\noindent\emph{Step 4: telescope over update times.}\quad
It remains to write the exact full-update telescope. Since
$\eta_t=\Phi_{1,t}(\eta_1)$,
\begin{align}
&\E\Psi_t(\eta_t^J)(f)-\Psi_t(\eta_t)(f)                    \notag\\
&\quad=
\E\left[
\Psi_t\{\Phi_{1,t}(\eta_1^J)\}(f)
-\Psi_t\{\Phi_{1,t}(\eta_1)\}(f)
\right]                                                     \notag\\
&\qquad+
\sum_{p=2}^t
\E\left[
\Psi_t\{\Phi_{p,t}(\eta_p^J)\}(f)
-\Psi_t\{\Phi_{p,t}(\Phi_p(\eta_{p-1}^J))\}(f)
\right].
\label{eq:weak-bias-full-update-telescope}
\end{align}
Indeed, for $2\leq p\leq t$,
\begin{equation}
\Phi_{p,t}\circ\Phi_p=\Phi_{p-1,t},
\label{eq:weak-bias-composition-identity}
\end{equation}
so consecutive terms cancel. The only terms left are
$\Psi_t(\eta_t^J)(f)$ and $-\Psi_t(\eta_t)(f)$.

Apply Equation~(\ref{eq:weak-bias-one-complete-update}) to every
summand in Equation~(\ref{eq:weak-bias-full-update-telescope}). Then
\begin{align}
\left|
\E\Psi_t(\eta_t^J)(f)-\Psi_t(\eta_t)(f)
\right|
&\leq
\frac{C\operatorname{osc}(f)}{J}
\sum_{p=1}^t\varrho^{t-p}                                  \notag\\
&\leq
\frac{C}{J(1-\varrho)}\operatorname{osc}(f).
\end{align}
Absorb $(1-\varrho)^{-1}$ into $C$ and combine this display with
Equations~(\ref{eq:current-state-posterior-filter-identity}) and
(\ref{eq:terminal-resampling-unbiased}).
\end{proof}

\iffalse
\begin{remark}[Particle bias of the DMOP-$\alpha$ score]
\label{lem:dmop-full-particle-bias}
Suppose $\theta=\phi$ and the model and resampling conditions of Lemma~
\ref{lem:dmop-alpha-variance} hold.  For $\alpha\in[0,1)$,
\begin{align}
\left\|
\E\nabla_\theta\hat\ell_n^\alpha(\theta)
-\nabla_\theta\ell_n^\alpha(\theta)
\right\|_2
&\leq
\frac{C\sqrt p\,G'(\theta)}{J(1-\alpha)},
\label{eq:conditional-score-particle-bias-direct}
\\
\left\|
\E\nabla_\theta\hat\ell^\alpha(\theta)
-\nabla_\theta\ell^\alpha(\theta)
\right\|_2
&\leq
\frac{C\sqrt p\,G'(\theta)}J
\sum_{t=1}^{N}
\left(
\sum_{r=0}^{t-1}\sum_{u=0}^{t-1}
\alpha^{r+u}\varrho^{|r-u|}
\right)^{1/2}                                                \notag\\
&\leq
\frac{CN\sqrt p\,G'(\theta)}J
\left\{
\frac{1+\alpha\varrho}
{(1-\alpha^2)(1-\alpha\varrho)}
\right\}^{1/2}.
\label{eq:full-score-particle-bias-direct}
\end{align}
\end{remark}

\begin{proof}[Proof.]
The local perturbation calculation in the proof of Lemma~
\ref{lem:dmop-alpha-variance} also gives the particle bias without
applying Lemma~\ref{lem:lagwise-weak-error} to a historical function.
We write out the required sums.  Fix a coordinate $i$ and a conditional
score time $n$.  The terminal resampling error in
Equation~(\ref{eq:aggregated-terminal-resampling-error}) has conditional
mean zero, and the first-order local fields in
Equation~(\ref{eq:dmop-direct-local-field-bounds}) have conditional mean
zero.  Hence only the quadratic ratio terms contribute to the bias.

For local sampling times $t$ after creation of $h_{s,i}$, their total
coefficient in the time-$n$ conditional score is bounded by
\begin{align}
&CG'(\theta)
\sum_{t=1}^{n}\sum_{s=1}^{t}
\alpha^{n-s}\varrho^{n-t}                                  \notag\\
&\quad\leq
CG'(\theta)
\left(\sum_{a=0}^{\infty}(\alpha\varrho)^a\right)
\left(\sum_{b=0}^{\infty}\alpha^b\right)                  \notag\\
&\quad=
\frac{CG'(\theta)}{(1-\alpha\varrho)(1-\alpha)}.
\label{eq:conditional-bias-past-local-sum}
\end{align}
Here $a=n-t$ and $b=t-s$.  For local sampling times preceding creation
of $h_{s,i}$, Equation~
(\ref{eq:dmop-direct-future-score-two-contractions}) gives
\begin{align}
&CG'(\theta)
\sum_{t=1}^{n-1}\sum_{s=t+1}^{n}
\alpha^{n-s}\varrho^{s-t}\varrho^{n-s}                    \notag\\
&\quad\leq
CG'(\theta)
\left(\sum_{a=1}^{\infty}\varrho^a\right)
\left(\sum_{b=0}^{\infty}(\alpha\varrho)^b\right)          \notag\\
&\quad=
\frac{CG'(\theta)\varrho}
{(1-\varrho)(1-\alpha\varrho)}.
\label{eq:conditional-bias-future-local-sum}
\end{align}
Here $a=s-t$ and $b=n-s$.  The empirical second-moment identities in
Equation~(\ref{eq:dmop-direct-local-empirical-moments}) make every local
quadratic ratio remainder equal to its displayed coefficient times at
most $C/J$ in absolute expectation.  Since
$1-\alpha\varrho\geq1-\varrho$, Equations~
(\ref{eq:conditional-bias-past-local-sum}) and
(\ref{eq:conditional-bias-future-local-sum}) therefore give
\begin{equation}
\left|
\E\partial_{\theta_i}\hat\ell_n^\alpha(\theta)
-\partial_{\theta_i}\ell_n^\alpha(\theta)
\right|
\leq
\frac{CG'(\theta)}{J(1-\alpha)}.
\label{eq:coordinate-conditional-score-local-bias}
\end{equation}
Taking the Euclidean norm over the $p$ coordinates proves
Equation~(\ref{eq:conditional-score-particle-bias-direct}).  Finally,
\begin{align}
&\left\|
\E\nabla_\theta\hat\ell^\alpha(\theta)
-\nabla_\theta\ell^\alpha(\theta)
\right\|_2                                                 \notag\\
&\quad\leq
\sum_{n=1}^{N}
\left\|
\E\nabla_\theta\hat\ell_n^\alpha(\theta)
-\nabla_\theta\ell_n^\alpha(\theta)
\right\|_2                                                 \notag\\
&\quad\leq
\frac{CN\sqrt p\,G'(\theta)}{J(1-\alpha)},
\end{align}
which proves Equation~(\ref{eq:full-score-particle-bias-direct}).
\end{proof}
\fi

\begin{proof}[Superseded proof.]

\left|
\E m(\xi_n)(h_{n,i})-\rho_n(h_{n,i})

\E\nabla_\theta\hat\ell_n^\alpha(\theta)
-\nabla_\theta\ell_n^\alpha(\theta)
\right\|_2                                                   \notag\\

\begin{lem}[Particle bias of a discounted conditional score]
\label{lem:discounted-dmop-particle-bias}
Suppose $\theta=\phi$. Under the conditions of
Lemma~\ref{lem:lagwise-weak-error}, for
\(\alpha\in[0,1)\),
\begin{equation}
\left\|
\E\nabla_\theta\hat\ell_n^\alpha(\theta)
-\nabla_\theta\ell_n^\alpha(\theta)
\right\|_2
\leq
\frac{C\sqrt p\,G'(\theta)n}{J(1-\alpha)}.
\label{eq:discounted-dmop-particle-bias}
\end{equation}
At \(\alpha=1\), the same argument gives
\begin{equation}
\left\|
\E\nabla_\theta\hat\ell_n^1(\theta)
-\nabla_\theta\ell_n^1(\theta)
\right\|_2
\leq
\frac{C\sqrt p\,G'(\theta)n^2}{J}.
\label{eq:undiscounted-dmop-particle-bias}
\end{equation}
\end{lem}

\begin{proof}[Proof.]
At \(\theta=\phi\), the particles and resampling indices do not depend on
\(\alpha\). Taking expectations in
Equation~(\ref{eq:conditional-score-path-decomposition}), subtracting
Equation~(\ref{eq:population-conditional-score-path-decomposition}), and
using Equation~(\ref{eq:lag-coefficient-definitions}) give
\begin{align}
&\E\nabla_\theta\hat\ell_n^\alpha(\theta)
-\nabla_\theta\ell_n^\alpha(\theta)                            \notag\\
&\qquad=
\sum_{r=0}^{n-1}\alpha^r
\left(\E\Delta_{n,r}^J-\Delta_{n,r}\right).
\label{eq:discounted-bias-lag-decomposition}
\end{align}
Using Lemma~\ref{lem:lagwise-weak-error} and the triangle inequality,
\begin{align}
\left\|
\E\nabla_\theta\hat\ell_n^\alpha(\theta)
-\nabla_\theta\ell_n^\alpha(\theta)
\right\|_2
&\leq
\frac{C\sqrt p\,G'(\theta)n}{J}
\sum_{r=0}^{n-1}\alpha^r                                      \notag\\
&\leq
\frac{C\sqrt p\,G'(\theta)n}{J}
\sum_{r=0}^{\infty}\alpha^r                                   \notag\\
&=
\frac{C\sqrt p\,G'(\theta)n}{J(1-\alpha)},
\end{align}
which proves Equation~(\ref{eq:discounted-dmop-particle-bias}). When
\(\alpha=1\), the same decomposition gives
\begin{align}
\left\|
\E\nabla_\theta\hat\ell_n^1(\theta)
-\nabla_\theta\ell_n^1(\theta)
\right\|_2
&\leq
\sum_{r=0}^{n-1}
\left\|\E\Delta_{n,r}^J-\Delta_{n,r}\right\|_2                 \notag\\
&\leq
n\frac{C\sqrt p\,G'(\theta)n}{J}
=
\frac{C\sqrt p\,G'(\theta)n^2}{J},
\end{align}
which proves Equation~(\ref{eq:undiscounted-dmop-particle-bias}).
\end{proof}
\fi

\begin{lem}[Full-score summation-by-parts identities]
\label{lem:dmop-full-score-summation-by-parts}
Suppose $\theta=\phi$ and $\alpha\in[0,1]$.  Then the particle and
population scores satisfy Equations~
(\ref{eq:full-score-summation-by-parts}) and
(\ref{eq:population-full-score-summation-by-parts}) below.  Moreover,
for every coordinate $i$ and $t\leq N$,
\begin{equation}
\left\|
\sum_{k=1}^{t}\alpha^{t-k}h_{k,i}
\right\|_\infty
\leq
G'(\theta)\sum_{k=1}^{t}\alpha^{t-k}.
\label{eq:discounted-score-supremum-statement}
\end{equation}
\end{lem}

\begin{proof}[Proof.]
\medskip\noindent\emph{Step 1: particle summation by parts.}\quad
We first record the summation-by-parts identity used again in the variance
proof. Sum Equation~(\ref{eq:conditional-score-path-decomposition}) over
$n=1,\ldots,N$ and put $k=n-r$. Since the sums are finite,
\begin{align}
\nabla_\theta\hat\ell^\alpha(\theta)
&=
\sum_{k=1}^{N}
\left[
m(\xi_k)(h_k)
+\sum_{t=k+1}^{N}\alpha^{t-k}
\{m(\xi_t)(h_k)-m(\xi_{t-1})(h_k)\}
\right].
\label{eq:full-score-reindexed-by-contribution}
\end{align}
For each fixed $k$, collecting the coefficient of every
$m(\xi_t)(h_k)$ gives
\begin{align}
&m(\xi_k)(h_k)
+\sum_{t=k+1}^{N}\alpha^{t-k}
\{m(\xi_t)(h_k)-m(\xi_{t-1})(h_k)\}                         \notag\\
&\quad=
(1-\alpha)\sum_{t=k}^{N-1}\alpha^{t-k}m(\xi_t)(h_k)
+\alpha^{N-k}m(\xi_N)(h_k).
\label{eq:one-score-summation-by-parts}
\end{align}
Indeed, the coefficients are $1-\alpha$ at $t=k$,
$\alpha^{t-k}-\alpha^{t+1-k}=(1-\alpha)\alpha^{t-k}$ for
$k<t<N$, and $\alpha^{N-k}$ at $t=N$. Interchanging the finite sums once
more yields
\begin{align}
\nabla_\theta\hat\ell^\alpha(\theta)
&=
(1-\alpha)\sum_{t=1}^{N-1}
m(\xi_t)\left(\sum_{k=1}^{t}\alpha^{t-k}h_k\right)
+m(\xi_N)\left(\sum_{k=1}^{N}\alpha^{N-k}h_k\right).
\label{eq:full-score-summation-by-parts}
\end{align}
\medskip\noindent\emph{Step 2: population identity and uniform bound.}\quad
The same algebra applied to
Equation~(\ref{eq:population-conditional-score-path-decomposition}) gives
\begin{align}
\nabla_\theta\ell^\alpha(\theta)
&=
(1-\alpha)\sum_{t=1}^{N-1}
\rho_t\left(\sum_{k=1}^{t}\alpha^{t-k}h_k\right)
+\rho_N\left(\sum_{k=1}^{N}\alpha^{N-k}h_k\right).
\label{eq:population-full-score-summation-by-parts}
\end{align}
For every coordinate $i$, Equation~
(\ref{eq:bounded-realized-measurement-score}) gives
\begin{equation}
\left\|
\sum_{k=1}^{t}\alpha^{t-k}h_{k,i}
\right\|_\infty
\leq
G'(\theta)\sum_{k=1}^{t}\alpha^{t-k}.
\label{eq:discounted-score-supremum}
\end{equation}

\iffalse
We now apply Lemma~\ref{lem:lagwise-weak-error} directly to the
current-particle averages in Equations~
(\ref{eq:full-score-summation-by-parts}) and
(\ref{eq:population-full-score-summation-by-parts}).  For every
coordinate $i$ and $t\leq N$, Equation~
(\ref{eq:discounted-score-supremum}) and
$\operatorname{osc}(f)\leq2\|f\|_\infty$ give
\begin{align}
\operatorname{osc}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{t}\alpha^{t-k}h_{k,i}\right)
&\leq
2G'(\theta)\sum_{k=1}^{t}\alpha^{t-k}.
\label{eq:full-score-weighted-function-oscillation}
\end{align}
Consequently, Lemma~\ref{lem:lagwise-weak-error} gives
\begin{align}
&\left|
\E m(\xi_t)\left(\sum_{k=1}^{t}\alpha^{t-k}h_{k,i}\right)
-\rho_t\left(\sum_{k=1}^{t}\alpha^{t-k}h_{k,i}\right)
\right|                                                       \notag\\
&\quad\leq
\frac{CG'(\theta)}{J}
\sum_{k=1}^{t}\alpha^{t-k}.
\label{eq:full-score-current-average-particle-bias}
\end{align}
Subtracting Equation~
(\ref{eq:population-full-score-summation-by-parts}) from the expectation
of Equation~(\ref{eq:full-score-summation-by-parts}), applying the

(\ref{eq:full-score-current-average-particle-bias}) give
\begin{align}
&\left|
\E\partial_{\theta_i}\hat\ell^\alpha(\theta)
-\partial_{\theta_i}\ell^\alpha(\theta)
\right|                                                       \notag\\
&\quad\leq
\frac{CG'(\theta)}{J}
\left\{
(1-\alpha)\sum_{t=1}^{N-1}\sum_{k=1}^{t}\alpha^{t-k}
+\sum_{k=1}^{N}\alpha^{N-k}
\right\}                                                     \notag\\
&\quad=
\frac{CNG'(\theta)}{J}.
\label{eq:uniform-full-score-particle-bias-coordinate}
\end{align}
The last equality is exact for every $\alpha\in[0,1]$.  To verify it
without interpreting a ratio at $\alpha=1$, use the finite sums
\begin{align}
&(1-\alpha)\sum_{t=1}^{N-1}\sum_{k=1}^{t}\alpha^{t-k}
+\sum_{k=1}^{N}\alpha^{N-k}                                  \notag\\
&\quad=
\sum_{t=1}^{N-1}(1-\alpha^t)
+\sum_{r=0}^{N-1}\alpha^r                                  \notag\\
&\quad=
(N-1)-\sum_{t=1}^{N-1}\alpha^t
+1+\sum_{r=1}^{N-1}\alpha^r
=N.
\label{eq:full-score-particle-bias-finite-cancellation}
\end{align}
At $\alpha=1$, both sides of the first equality in Equation~
(\ref{eq:full-score-particle-bias-finite-cancellation}) are evaluated as
finite sums, so the equality remains valid.  Squaring Equation~
(\ref{eq:uniform-full-score-particle-bias-coordinate}), summing over
$i=1,\ldots,p$, and taking square roots proves Equation~
(\ref{eq:dmop-full-particle-bias-alpha}).
\fi
Equation~(\ref{eq:discounted-score-supremum}) is identical to
Equation~(\ref{eq:discounted-score-supremum-statement}), completing the
proof.
\end{proof}

\iffalse
\begin{assumptionA6}[Primitive-Innovation Continuation Stability]
Suppose $\theta=\phi$, $\alpha\in[0,1)$, and multinomial resampling is used.
Represent the particle randomness, in chronological order, by independent
primitive blocks $\mathcal U_1,\ldots,\mathcal U_K$, where
\begin{equation}
K=J(2N+1).
\label{eq:number-primitive-particle-blocks}
\end{equation}
These blocks comprise the $J$ initial-state simulator variables and, at each
time $m=1,\ldots,N$, the $J$ propagation simulator variables followed by the
$J$ independent uniforms used for multinomial resampling. Conditional on the
prediction particles, each resampling uniform is mapped through the inverse
categorical distribution defined by the normalized measurement weights.
Thus all state propagation and all resampling in the algorithm are
measurable functions of these independent blocks.

For a coordinate $i$, let
$Z_i=\partial_{\theta_i}\hat\ell^\alpha(\theta)$, put
\begin{equation}
\mathcal H_q^J=\sigma(\mathcal U_1,\ldots,\mathcal U_q),
\qquad
\mathcal H_0^J=\{\varnothing,\Omega\},
\qquad
M_{q,i}=\E(Z_i\mid\mathcal H_q^J),
\label{eq:primitive-particle-filtration}
\end{equation}
and let $\mathcal U_q'$ be an independent copy of $\mathcal U_q$, independent
of all the original blocks. Holding $\mathcal U_1,\ldots,\mathcal U_{q-1}$
fixed, replace $\mathcal U_q$ by $\mathcal U_q'$ and integrate out all later
blocks $\mathcal U_{q+1},\ldots,\mathcal U_K$. Denote the resulting
conditional expectation of $Z_i$ by
\begin{equation}
M_{q,i}^{(q)}
=
\E\left[
Z_i(\mathcal U_1,\ldots,\mathcal U_{q-1},\mathcal U_q',
\mathcal U_{q+1},\ldots,\mathcal U_K)
\mid \mathcal H_{q-1}^J,\mathcal U_q'
\right].
\label{eq:primitive-block-independent-copy}
\end{equation}
There is a constant independent of $N,J,q,i$, and $\alpha$ such that,
almost surely,
\begin{equation}
\left\{
\E\left[(M_{q,i}-M_{q,i}^{(q)})^2
\mid\mathcal H_{q-1}^J\right]
\right\}^{1/2}
\leq
\frac{CG'(\theta)}{J(1-\alpha)}.
\label{eq:finite-particle-continuation-stability}
\end{equation}
\end{assumptionA6}

Assumption {\refAssumpPrimitiveInnovation} concerns the finite-$J$, $O(J)$
interacting genealogy and separates the two sources of randomness within
each filtering time: the
independent propagation variables and the independent multinomial-resampling
uniforms. In particular, it does not treat the after-resampling particles as
conditionally independent before the shared random prediction cloud has
been revealed.
Lemmas~\ref{lem:derivative-measure-forgetting}--
\ref{lem:discounted-dmop-particle-bias} derive deterministic forgetting,
full-genealogy weak bias, and discounted particle bias under Assumptions
{\refAssumpBoundedProcess}, {\refAssumpBoundedMeasurement}, and
{\refAssumpDifferentiableSimulator}. The time-uniform results of
\citet{delMoral15derivative,tadic18} establish error and stability bounds for
their $O(J^2)$ backward derivative estimator; they do not imply Assumption
{\refAssumpPrimitiveInnovation} for DMOP. Assumption
{\refAssumpPrimitiveInnovation} is used only for the variance bound below.

\begin{lem}[Variance of the discounted genealogical score]
\label{lem:dmop-alpha-variance}
Suppose $\theta=\phi$, $\alpha\in[0,1)$, Assumptions
{\refAssumpBoundedProcess}, {\refAssumpBoundedMeasurement},
{\refAssumpDifferentiableSimulator}, and
{\refAssumpPrimitiveInnovation} hold, and multinomial resampling is used.
Then
\begin{equation}
\Var\left(\nabla_\theta\hat\ell^\alpha(\theta)\right)
\lesssim
\frac{NpG'(\theta)^2}{J(1-\alpha)^2},
\label{eq:refined-dmop-alpha-variance}
\end{equation}
where $\Var(Z)=\E\|Z-\E Z\|_2^2$ for a random vector $Z$.
\end{lem}

\begin{proof}[Proof.]
We prove the result coordinatewise. Fix $i$ and use the primitive blocks,
filtration, and conditional expectations in Assumption
{\refAssumpPrimitiveInnovation}. Equations~
(\ref{eq:conditional-score-path-decomposition}) and
(\ref{eq:bounded-realized-measurement-mark}) give
\begin{align}
|Z_i|
&\leq
\sum_{n=1}^N
\left\{
G'(\theta)
+2G'(\theta)\sum_{r=1}^{n-1}\alpha^r
\right\}                                                       \notag\\
&\leq
NG'(\theta)
\left\{1+\frac{2}{1-\alpha}\right\}
<\infty.
\label{eq:dmop-coordinate-square-integrability}
\end{align}
Thus $Z_i$ is square integrable. Define the Doob martingale differences
\begin{equation}
D_{q,i}
=
M_{q,i}-M_{q-1,i},
\qquad q=1,\ldots,K.
\label{eq:dmop-doob-increments}
\end{equation}
The sigma-field $\mathcal H_K^J$ contains every primitive random variable
used to construct $Z_i$. Hence
\begin{equation}
M_{K,i}=\E(Z_i\mid\mathcal H_K^J)=Z_i,
\qquad
M_{0,i}=\E Z_i.
\label{eq:dmop-martingale-endpoints}
\end{equation}
Summing Equation~(\ref{eq:dmop-doob-increments}) and using Equation~
(\ref{eq:dmop-martingale-endpoints}) give
\begin{align}
\sum_{q=1}^{K}D_{q,i}
&=
\sum_{q=1}^{K}(M_{q,i}-M_{q-1,i})                              \notag\\
&=
Z_i-\E Z_i.
\label{eq:dmop-doob-telescope}
\end{align}
The tower property gives
\begin{align}
\E(D_{q,i}\mid\mathcal H_{q-1}^J)
&=
\E(M_{q,i}\mid\mathcal H_{q-1}^J)-M_{q-1,i}                  \notag\\
&=
\E\{\E(Z_i\mid\mathcal H_q^J)\mid\mathcal H_{q-1}^J\}
-\E(Z_i\mid\mathcal H_{q-1}^J)                               \notag\\
&=0.
\label{eq:dmop-martingale-increment-centering}
\end{align}
If $l<q$, then $D_{l,i}$ is $\mathcal H_{q-1}^J$-measurable. Therefore
\begin{align}
\E(D_{l,i}D_{q,i})
&=
\E\left[
D_{l,i}\E(D_{q,i}\mid\mathcal H_{q-1}^J)
\right]                                                       \notag\\
&=0.
\label{eq:dmop-doob-orthogonality}
\end{align}
Squaring Equation~(\ref{eq:dmop-doob-telescope}), taking expectations,
and using Equation~(\ref{eq:dmop-doob-orthogonality}) give
\begin{equation}
\Var(Z_i)=\sum_{q=1}^{K}\E(D_{q,i}^2).
\label{eq:dmop-doob-variance}
\end{equation}

We next relate each increment to the replacement in Assumption
{\refAssumpPrimitiveInnovation}.
Conditional on $\mathcal H_{q-1}^J$, the original block $\mathcal U_q$ and
its copy $\mathcal U_q'$ are independent and have the same distribution.
Since all later blocks are integrated out in the definitions of
$M_{q,i}$ and $M_{q,i}^{(q)}$, these two conditional expectations are
conditionally independent and identically distributed. Equation~
(\ref{eq:dmop-martingale-increment-centering}) and the same calculation for
$M_{q,i}^{(q)}$ therefore give
\begin{align}
&\E(M_{q,i}\mid\mathcal H_{q-1}^J)
=
\E(M_{q,i}^{(q)}\mid\mathcal H_{q-1}^J)
=M_{q-1,i},
\label{eq:replacement-conditional-means}
\\
&\E\left[
(M_{q,i}-M_{q-1,i})(M_{q,i}^{(q)}-M_{q-1,i})
\mid\mathcal H_{q-1}^J
\right]
=0,
\label{eq:replacement-cross-term-zero}
\\
&\E\left[
(M_{q,i}^{(q)}-M_{q-1,i})^2
\mid\mathcal H_{q-1}^J
\right]
=
\E\left[
(M_{q,i}-M_{q-1,i})^2
\mid\mathcal H_{q-1}^J
\right].
\label{eq:replacement-second-moments}
\end{align}
Expanding the square of the replacement difference and applying
Equations~(\ref{eq:replacement-cross-term-zero})--
(\ref{eq:replacement-second-moments}) give the exact identity
\begin{align}
&\E\left[
(M_{q,i}-M_{q,i}^{(q)})^2
\mid\mathcal H_{q-1}^J
\right]                                                       \notag\\
&\quad=
\E\left[
\{(M_{q,i}-M_{q-1,i})-(M_{q,i}^{(q)}-M_{q-1,i})\}^2
\mid\mathcal H_{q-1}^J
\right]                                                       \notag\\
&\quad=
2\E\left[
(M_{q,i}-M_{q-1,i})^2
\mid\mathcal H_{q-1}^J
\right]                                                       \notag\\
&\quad=
2\E(D_{q,i}^2\mid\mathcal H_{q-1}^J).
\label{eq:replacement-doob-identity}
\end{align}
Assumption {\refAssumpPrimitiveInnovation} and
Equation~(\ref{eq:replacement-doob-identity}) therefore
imply, almost surely,
\begin{align}
\E(D_{q,i}^2\mid\mathcal H_{q-1}^J)
&=
\frac12
\E\left[
(M_{q,i}-M_{q,i}^{(q)})^2
\mid\mathcal H_{q-1}^J
\right]                                                       \notag\\
&\leq
\frac{C^2G'(\theta)^2}{2J^2(1-\alpha)^2}                     \notag\\
&\leq
\frac{CG'(\theta)^2}{J^2(1-\alpha)^2},
\label{eq:dmop-one-block-variance}
\end{align}
where the last line enlarges $C$. Taking expectations, substituting
Equation~(\ref{eq:dmop-one-block-variance}) into Equation~
(\ref{eq:dmop-doob-variance}), and using
$K=J(2N+1)\leq3JN$ for $N\geq1$ give
\begin{align}
\Var(Z_i)
&\leq
\sum_{q=1}^{K}
\frac{CG'(\theta)^2}{J^2(1-\alpha)^2}                        \notag\\
&=
\frac{CKG'(\theta)^2}{J^2(1-\alpha)^2}                      \notag\\
&\leq
\frac{CNG'(\theta)^2}{J(1-\alpha)^2}.
\label{eq:dmop-coordinate-variance}
\end{align}
Finally,
\begin{align}
\Var\{\nabla_\theta\hat\ell^\alpha(\theta)\}
&=
\E\left[
\sum_{i=1}^p(Z_i-\E Z_i)^2
\right]                                                       \notag\\
&=
\sum_{i=1}^p\Var(Z_i)                                        \notag\\
&\leq
\frac{CNpG'(\theta)^2}{J(1-\alpha)^2},
\end{align}
which proves Equation~(\ref{eq:refined-dmop-alpha-variance}).
\end{proof}
\fi

\begin{claim}[Conditional empirical moments]
\label{claim:conditional-empirical-moments}
Let $\mathcal F$ be a sigma-field and let $\mu$ be an
$\mathcal F$-measurable probability measure.  Suppose that,
conditionally on $\mathcal F$, $X_1,\ldots,X_J$ are independent with
common law $\mu$.  The following bounds hold for bounded
$\mathcal F$-measurable real functions $f,f_1,f_2$:
\begin{align}
\E\left\{
\left.
\frac1J\sum_{j=1}^J\{f(X_j)-\mu(f)\}
\right|\mathcal F
\right\}
&=0,
\label{eq:conditional-empirical-mean-zero}
\\
\E\left\{
\left.
\left[
\frac1J\sum_{j=1}^J\{f(X_j)-\mu(f)\}
\right]^2
\right|\mathcal F
\right\}
&=
\frac{\mu\{(f-\mu f)^2\}}J
\leq
\frac{\operatorname{osc}(f)^2}{4J},
\label{eq:conditional-empirical-second-moment}
\\
\E\left\{
\left.
\left[
\frac1J\sum_{j=1}^J\{f(X_j)-\mu(f)\}
\right]^4
\right|\mathcal F
\right\}
&=
\frac{\mu\{(f-\mu f)^4\}}{J^3}
+\frac{3(J-1)}{J^3}\mu\{(f-\mu f)^2\}^2                 \notag\\
&\leq
\frac{\mu\{(f-\mu f)^4\}}{J^3}
+\frac{3}{J^2}\mu\{(f-\mu f)^2\}^2                    \notag\\
&\leq
\frac{3\operatorname{osc}(f)^4}{J^2}.
\label{eq:conditional-empirical-fourth-moment}
\end{align}
Consequently, for bounded $f_1$ and $f_2$,
\begin{align}
&\left\|
\left[
\frac1J\sum_{j=1}^J\{f_1(X_j)-\mu(f_1)\}
\right]
\left[
\frac1J\sum_{j=1}^J\{f_2(X_j)-\mu(f_2)\}
\right]
\right\|_{L^2\mid\mathcal F}                               \notag\\
&\qquad\leq
\frac{\sqrt3}{J}
\operatorname{osc}(f_1)\operatorname{osc}(f_2).
\label{eq:conditional-empirical-product-moment}
\end{align}
\end{claim}

\begin{proof}[Proof.]
Put $Y_j=f(X_j)-\mu(f)$.  Conditional independence and
$\E(Y_j\mid\mathcal F)=0$ give Equations~
(\ref{eq:conditional-empirical-mean-zero}) and
(\ref{eq:conditional-empirical-second-moment}).  They also give
\begin{align}
&\E\left\{
\left.
\left(\frac1J\sum_{j=1}^JY_j\right)^4
\right|\mathcal F
\right\}                                                     \notag\\
&\quad=
\frac1{J^4}
\left\{
J\mu\{(f-\mu f)^4\}
+3J(J-1)\mu\{(f-\mu f)^2\}^2
\right\}                                                    \notag\\
&\quad=
\frac{\mu\{(f-\mu f)^4\}}{J^3}
+\frac{3(J-1)}{J^3}\mu\{(f-\mu f)^2\}^2                 \notag\\
&\quad\leq
\frac{3}{J^2}\operatorname{osc}(f)^4,
\end{align}
which is Equation~(\ref{eq:conditional-empirical-fourth-moment}).
Conditional Cauchy--Schwarz applied to the two fourth moments proves
Equation~(\ref{eq:conditional-empirical-product-moment}).
\end{proof}

\begin{claim}[Normalized-ratio perturbations]
\label{claim:normalized-ratio-perturbations}
Let $\mu$ and $\nu$ be probability measures.  For $l\in\{0,1\}$,
let $a_l>0$ and $b_l$ be bounded functions such that $\mu(a_l)=1$, and
put
\begin{equation}
\mathcal R_l(\lambda)=\frac{\lambda(b_l)}{\lambda(a_l)},
\qquad
D_\mu\mathcal R_l=b_l-\mathcal R_l(\mu)a_l.
\label{eq:auxiliary-normalized-ratio-definition}
\end{equation}
Then
\begin{align}
\mathcal R_l(\nu)-\mathcal R_l(\mu)
&=(\nu-\mu)D_\mu\mathcal R_l
-\frac{(\nu-\mu)(a_l)}{\nu(a_l)}
(\nu-\mu)D_\mu\mathcal R_l,
\label{eq:auxiliary-exact-ratio-expansion}
\end{align}
and hence
\begin{align}
&\{\mathcal R_1(\nu)-\mathcal R_0(\nu)\}
-\{\mathcal R_1(\mu)-\mathcal R_0(\mu)\}                  \notag\\
&\quad-(\nu-\mu)
\{D_\mu\mathcal R_1-D_\mu\mathcal R_0\}                  \notag\\
&=-\frac{(\nu-\mu)(a_1-a_0)}{\nu(a_0)\nu(a_1)}
(\nu-\mu)D_\mu\mathcal R_1                                \notag\\
&\quad-
\frac{(\nu-\mu)(a_0)}{\nu(a_0)}
(\nu-\mu)
\{D_\mu\mathcal R_1-D_\mu\mathcal R_0\}.
\label{eq:auxiliary-paired-ratio-remainder}
\end{align}
If $\inf_{l\in\{0,1\}}\nu(a_l)\geq C^{-1}$, then the absolute
value of the left side of
Equation~(\ref{eq:auxiliary-paired-ratio-remainder}) is at most
\begin{align}
&C^2\left|(\nu-\mu)(a_1-a_0)\right|
\left|(\nu-\mu)D_\mu\mathcal R_1\right|                    \notag\\
&\quad+
C\left|(\nu-\mu)(a_0)\right|
\left|(\nu-\mu)
\{D_\mu\mathcal R_1-D_\mu\mathcal R_0\}\right|.
\label{eq:auxiliary-paired-ratio-bound}
\end{align}
\end{claim}

\begin{proof}[Proof.]
Since $\mu(a_l)=1$,
\begin{align}
\mathcal R_l(\nu)-\mathcal R_l(\mu)
&=
\frac{\nu(b_l)-\mathcal R_l(\mu)\nu(a_l)}{\nu(a_l)}       \notag\\
&=
\frac{(\nu-\mu)D_\mu\mathcal R_l}{\nu(a_l)}              \notag\\
&=(\nu-\mu)D_\mu\mathcal R_l
-\frac{(\nu-\mu)(a_l)}{\nu(a_l)}
(\nu-\mu)D_\mu\mathcal R_l,
\end{align}
which proves Equation~(\ref{eq:auxiliary-exact-ratio-expansion}).
Moreover,
\begin{align}
&\frac{(\nu-\mu)(a_1)}{\nu(a_1)}
-\frac{(\nu-\mu)(a_0)}{\nu(a_0)}                         \notag\\
&\quad=
\left(1-\frac1{\nu(a_1)}\right)
-\left(1-\frac1{\nu(a_0)}\right)                         \notag\\
&\quad=
\frac{\nu(a_1)-\nu(a_0)}{\nu(a_0)\nu(a_1)}              \notag\\
&\quad=
\frac{(\nu-\mu)(a_1-a_0)}{\nu(a_0)\nu(a_1)}.
\end{align}
Subtracting the cases $l=0$ and $l=1$ in
Equation~(\ref{eq:auxiliary-exact-ratio-expansion}) now proves
Equation~(\ref{eq:auxiliary-paired-ratio-remainder}).  Taking absolute
values there and using
$\nu(a_0)^{-1}\leq C$ and $\nu(a_1)^{-1}\leq C$ proves
Equation~(\ref{eq:auxiliary-paired-ratio-bound}).
This exact identity is the paired specialization of the normalized
Feynman--Kac perturbation calculation in Equations~(3.1)--(3.4) and
Appendix~A.3 of \citet{delMoral11}.
\end{proof}

\begin{lem}[Particle error and variance of the DMOP-$\alpha$ score]
\label{lem:dmop-alpha-variance}
Suppose $J\geq2$, $\theta=\phi$, $\alpha\in[0,1]$, Assumptions
{\refAssumpBoundedProcess}, {\refAssumpBoundedMeasurement}, and
{\refAssumpDifferentiableSimulator} hold, and multinomial resampling is
used.  Assume additionally that the bounded measurement-gradient input
is the current function $h_n(X_n)$ in
Equation~(\ref{eq:location-weight-gradient-pair-recursion}). Then
\begin{align}
&\max\left\{
\E\left\|
\nabla_\theta\hat\ell^\alpha(\theta)
-\nabla_\theta\ell^\alpha(\theta)
\right\|_2^2,\,
\Var\left(\nabla_\theta\hat\ell^\alpha(\theta)\right)
\right\}                                                     \notag\\
&\quad\lesssim
pG'(\theta)^2
\begin{aligned}[t]
\Bigg[&
\frac1J\sum_{t=1}^{N}
\sum_{r=0}^{t-1}\sum_{u=0}^{t-1}
\alpha^{r+u}\varrho^{|r-u|}                                  \\
&+
\frac1{J^2}
\left\{
\sum_{t=1}^{N}
\left(
\sum_{r=0}^{t-1}\sum_{u=0}^{t-1}
\alpha^{r+u}\varrho^{|r-u|}
\right)^{1/2}
\right\}^2
\Bigg]
\end{aligned}                                                \notag\\
&\quad\lesssim
pG'(\theta)^2
\left(\frac NJ+\frac{N^2}{J^2}\right)
\left(\sum_{r=0}^{N-1}\alpha^r\right)^2.
\label{eq:refined-dmop-alpha-direct-particle-error}
\end{align}
Here the multiplicative constants suppressed by $\lesssim$ depend on the
fixed model bounds, but are independent of $N,J$, and $\alpha$, and
$\Var(Z)=\E\|Z-\E Z\|_2^2$.
For $\alpha<1$, the first upper bound gives
\begin{align}
&\max\left\{
\E\left\|
\nabla_\theta\hat\ell^\alpha(\theta)
-\nabla_\theta\ell^\alpha(\theta)
\right\|_2^2,\,
\Var\left(\nabla_\theta\hat\ell^\alpha(\theta)\right)
\right\}                                                     \notag\\
&\quad\lesssim
pG'(\theta)^2
\left(\frac NJ+\frac{N^2}{J^2}\right)
\frac{1+\alpha\varrho}
{(1-\alpha^2)(1-\alpha\varrho)}.
\label{eq:refined-dmop-alpha-lag-particle-error}
\end{align}
At $\alpha=1$, it gives
\begin{equation}
\max\left\{
\E\left\|
\nabla_\theta\hat\ell^1(\theta)
-\nabla_\theta\ell^1(\theta)
\right\|_2^2,\,
\Var\left(\nabla_\theta\hat\ell^1(\theta)\right)
\right\}
\lesssim
pG'(\theta)^2
\min\left\{
N^2,\,
\frac{N^2}{J}+\frac{N^3}{J^2}
\right\}.
\label{eq:refined-dmop-one-lag-particle-error}
\end{equation}
\end{lem}

\begin{proof}[Proof.]
The proof keeps three time indices explicit: $s$ is the time at which
$h_{s,i}$ is created, $t$ is the local particle-sampling time, and $n$ is
the conditional-score time.  We first separate terminal resampling, then
control predictable contributions for $s\leq t$ and $t<s$, and only then
sum the local particle errors over $t$.

\medskip\noindent\emph{Step 1: terminal multinomial resampling.}\quad
Fix a coordinate $i$ and write
\begin{equation}
Z_i^J=\partial_{\theta_i}\hat\ell^\alpha(\theta),
\qquad
z_i^\alpha=\partial_{\theta_i}\ell^\alpha(\theta).
\label{eq:dmop-direct-error-coordinate-definitions}
\end{equation}
Equation~(\ref{eq:conditional-score-path-decomposition}) and
Equation~(\ref{eq:bounded-realized-measurement-score}) give
\begin{align}
|Z_i^J|
&\leq
\sum_{n=1}^N
\left\{
G'(\theta)+2G'(\theta)\sum_{r=1}^{n-1}\alpha^r
\right\}                                                   \notag\\
&\leq
NG'(\theta)\left\{1+2\sum_{r=1}^{N-1}\alpha^r\right\}
\leq2N^2G'(\theta)<\infty.
\label{eq:dmop-direct-error-square-integrability}
\end{align}
Thus all random variables used below are square integrable.

We first separate the error created by the time-$n$ multinomial
resampling.  For the prediction paths at time $n$, write
\begin{equation}
p_{n,a}
=
\frac{g_{n,a}^{\phi}}{\sum_{b=1}^Jg_{n,b}^{\phi}},
\qquad a=1,\ldots,J,
\label{eq:dmop-direct-time-n-resampling-probability}
\end{equation}
and put
\begin{align}
R_{n,i}^J
&=
\frac1J\sum_{j=1}^J
\left[
\sum_{s=1}^n\alpha^{n-s}h_{s,i}(\xi_{n,k_j}^P)
-\sum_{a=1}^Jp_{n,a}
\sum_{s=1}^n\alpha^{n-s}h_{s,i}(\xi_{n,a}^P)
\right].
\label{eq:aggregated-terminal-resampling-error}
\end{align}
Conditionally on the prediction paths, the $J$ summands in
Equation~(\ref{eq:aggregated-terminal-resampling-error}) are independent
and centered.  Moreover,
\begin{align}
\operatorname{osc}\left(
\sum_{s=1}^n\alpha^{n-s}h_{s,i}
\right)
&\leq
2G'(\theta)\sum_{s=1}^n\alpha^{n-s}                         \notag\\
&\leq
2G'(\theta)\sum_{r=0}^{N-1}\alpha^r.
\label{eq:aggregated-terminal-function-oscillation}
\end{align}
Claim~\ref{claim:conditional-empirical-moments} gives the exact
conditional variance
\begin{align}
\E\left\{(R_{n,i}^J)^2\mid\xi_{n,1:J}^P\right\}
&=
\frac1J\Psi_n\{m(\xi_n^P)\}
\left[
\left\{
\sum_{s=1}^n\alpha^{n-s}h_{s,i}
-\Psi_n\{m(\xi_n^P)\}
\left(\sum_{s=1}^n\alpha^{n-s}h_{s,i}\right)
\right\}^2
\right].
\label{eq:aggregated-terminal-resampling-second-moment}
\end{align}
Taking expectations and applying the genealogical lag bound in
Equation~(\ref{eq:dmop-genealogical-lag-moments}), proved in Step~5,
gives
\begin{equation}
\E(R_{n,i}^J)^2
\leq
\frac{CG'(\theta)^2}{J}
\sum_{r=0}^{n-1}\sum_{u=0}^{n-1}
\alpha^{r+u}\varrho^{|r-u|}.
\label{eq:aggregated-terminal-resampling-lag-bound}
\end{equation}

\medskip\noindent\emph{Step 2: exact predictable-score decomposition.}\quad
We next derive the local perturbation bound for the predictable part.
Fix $s\leq t\leq n$ and retain the manuscript's $h_{s,i}$.  Define
\begin{align}
q_{t,n}^{1}
&=Q_{t,n}(1),                                                \notag\\
q_{t,n}^{0}
&=
\begin{cases}
Q_{t,n-1}(1),&t<n,\\
1,&t=n,
\end{cases}
&
F_{t,n}
&=\frac{q_{t,n}^{1}}{q_{t,n}^{0}},
\label{eq:dmop-direct-local-future-functions}
\end{align}
and, for a probability measure $\mu$ on prediction paths through time
$t$,
\begin{equation}
C_{t,n}(\mu)
=
\frac{\mu(h_{s,i}q_{t,n}^{1})}{\mu(q_{t,n}^{1})}
-
\frac{\mu(h_{s,i}q_{t,n}^{0})}{\mu(q_{t,n}^{0})}.
\label{eq:dmop-direct-continuation-functional}
\end{equation}
At $t=n$, this is precisely the predictable part of the one-lag
difference:
\begin{align}
C_{n,n}\{m(\xi_n^P)\}
&=
\sum_{a=1}^Jp_{n,a}h_{s,i}(\xi_{n,a}^P)
-m(\xi_n^P)(h_{s,i}),                                      \notag\\
C_{n,n}(\eta_n)
&=
\begin{cases}
\rho_n(h_{s,i})-\rho_{n-1}(h_{s,i}),&s<n,\\
\rho_n(h_{n,i})-\eta_n(h_{n,i}),&s=n.
\end{cases}
\label{eq:dmop-direct-continuation-endpoints}
\end{align}
When several values of $s$ occur in the same display below, write
$C_{t,n}^{(s)}$ for the same functional with the displayed $h_{s,i}$;
the superscript records no additional object.  The conditional-score
decomposition gives the exact identities
\begin{align}
Z_i^J
&=
\sum_{n=1}^{N}R_{n,i}^J
+\sum_{n=1}^{N}m(\xi_n^P)(h_{n,i})
+\sum_{n=1}^{N}\sum_{s=1}^{n}
\alpha^{n-s}C_{n,n}^{(s)}\{m(\xi_n^P)\},
\label{eq:dmop-direct-exact-predictor-decomposition}
\\
z_i^\alpha
&=
\sum_{n=1}^{N}\eta_n(h_{n,i})
+\sum_{n=1}^{N}\sum_{s=1}^{n}
\alpha^{n-s}C_{n,n}^{(s)}(\eta_n).
\label{eq:dmop-direct-exact-population-decomposition}
\end{align}
Indeed, for $s<n$, the $s$th summand of $R_{n,i}^J$ plus
$\alpha^{n-s}C_{n,n}^{(s)}\{m(\xi_n^P)\}$ is
\begin{align}
\alpha^{n-s}
\left\{
m(\xi_n)(h_{s,i})-m(\xi_{n-1})(h_{s,i})
\right\},
\end{align}
while for $s=n$ the three terms are
\begin{align}
&\left[
m(\xi_n)(h_{n,i})
-\sum_{a=1}^Jp_{n,a}h_{n,i}(\xi_{n,a}^P)
\right]
+m(\xi_n^P)(h_{n,i})                                      \notag\\
&\qquad+
\left[
\sum_{a=1}^Jp_{n,a}h_{n,i}(\xi_{n,a}^P)
-m(\xi_n^P)(h_{n,i})
\right]
=m(\xi_n)(h_{n,i}).
\label{eq:dmop-direct-current-predictor-cancellation}
\end{align}
This proves Equation~
(\ref{eq:dmop-direct-exact-predictor-decomposition}); the population
identity follows by the same cancellation.

\medskip\noindent\emph{Step 3: continuation when $s\leq t$.}\quad
The recursion for $Q_{t,n}$ and preservation of $h_{s,i}$ after time
$s$ give, for $s<t\leq n$,
\begin{equation}
C_{t,n}\{\Phi_t(\mu)\}=C_{t-1,n}(\mu).
\label{eq:dmop-direct-continuation-consistency}
\end{equation}
Indeed, for $l\in\{0,1\}$,
\begin{align}
\frac{\Phi_t(\mu)(h_{s,i}q_{t,n}^{l})}
{\Phi_t(\mu)(q_{t,n}^{l})}
&=
\frac{\mu\{G_{t-1}M_t(h_{s,i}q_{t,n}^{l})\}}
{\mu\{G_{t-1}M_t(q_{t,n}^{l})\}}                          \notag\\
&=
\frac{\mu\{h_{s,i}G_{t-1}M_t(q_{t,n}^{l})\}}
{\mu\{G_{t-1}M_t(q_{t,n}^{l})\}}                          \notag\\
&=
\frac{\mu(h_{s,i}q_{t-1,n}^{l})}
{\mu(q_{t-1,n}^{l})}.
\end{align}
Subtracting the cases $l=1$ and $l=0$ proves
Equation~(\ref{eq:dmop-direct-continuation-consistency}).

Assumptions {\refAssumpBoundedProcess} and
{\refAssumpBoundedMeasurement} imply, exactly as in Equations~
(\ref{eq:weak-bias-uniform-normalizer-ratio}) and
(\ref{eq:weak-bias-normalized-semigroup-contraction}), that
\begin{align}
\sup_{x,x'}\frac{q_{t,n}^{0}(x)}{q_{t,n}^{0}(x')}
&\leq C,                                                     \notag\\
\underbar G\leq F_{t,n}(x)&\leq\bar G,                    \notag\\
\operatorname{osc}(F_{t,n})
&\leq C\varrho^{n-t}.
\label{eq:dmop-direct-future-function-contraction}
\end{align}
Normalize $q_{t,n}^{0}$ so that $\mu(q_{t,n}^{0})=1$ and write
\begin{equation}
\bar\mu(f)=\mu(q_{t,n}^{0}f).
\end{equation}
Then
\begin{equation}
C_{t,n}(\mu)
=
\frac{\bar\mu(h_{s,i}F_{t,n})}{\bar\mu(F_{t,n})}
-\bar\mu(h_{s,i}).
\label{eq:dmop-direct-continuation-covariance-form}
\end{equation}
For constants $h_0$ and $F_0$ in the respective ranges of $h_{s,i}$
and $F_{t,n}$,
\begin{align}
&\bar\mu(h_{s,i}F_{t,n})
-\bar\mu(h_{s,i})\bar\mu(F_{t,n})                         \notag\\
&\quad=
\bar\mu\{(h_{s,i}-h_0)(F_{t,n}-F_0)\}
-\bar\mu(h_{s,i}-h_0)\bar\mu(F_{t,n}-F_0).
\label{eq:dmop-direct-local-covariance-identity}
\end{align}
Equations~(\ref{eq:bounded-realized-measurement-score}) and
(\ref{eq:dmop-direct-future-function-contraction}) therefore give
\begin{equation}
|C_{t,n}(\mu)|
\leq
CG'(\theta)\varrho^{n-t}.
\label{eq:dmop-direct-local-continuation-size}
\end{equation}

To control the particle perturbation, put
\begin{equation}
\mathcal R_l(\lambda)
=\frac{\lambda(h_{s,i}q_{t,n}^{l})}{\lambda(q_{t,n}^{l})},
\qquad
a_l=\frac{q_{t,n}^{l}}{\mu(q_{t,n}^{l})},
\qquad l\in\{0,1\}.
\label{eq:dmop-direct-local-ratios}
\end{equation}
Thus $C_{t,n}=\mathcal R_1-\mathcal R_0$.  With the normalization
$\mu(q_{t,n}^{0})=1$, direct differentiation gives
\begin{align}
a_0&=q_{t,n}^{0},
&
a_1&=\frac{q_{t,n}^{0}F_{t,n}}{\bar\mu(F_{t,n})},          \notag\\
D_\mu\mathcal R_0
&=q_{t,n}^{0}\{h_{s,i}-\bar\mu(h_{s,i})\},
&
D_\mu\mathcal R_1
&=a_1\left\{
h_{s,i}-\frac{\bar\mu(h_{s,i}F_{t,n})}{\bar\mu(F_{t,n})}
\right\}.
\label{eq:dmop-direct-local-ratio-derivatives}
\end{align}
Subtracting the two expressions in
Equation~(\ref{eq:dmop-direct-local-ratio-derivatives}) gives the exact
first variation
\begin{align}
D_\mu C_{t,n}
&=D_\mu\mathcal R_1-D_\mu\mathcal R_0                       \notag\\
&=(a_1-a_0)\{h_{s,i}-\mathcal R_1(\mu)\}
+a_0\{\mathcal R_0(\mu)-\mathcal R_1(\mu)\}.
\label{eq:dmop-direct-local-derivative-formula}
\end{align}
Equations~(\ref{eq:dmop-direct-future-function-contraction}),
(\ref{eq:bounded-realized-measurement-score}), and
(\ref{eq:dmop-direct-local-continuation-size}) therefore imply
\begin{align}
\operatorname{osc}(a_0)&\leq C,                            \notag\\
\operatorname{osc}(a_1-a_0)&\leq C\varrho^{n-t},           \notag\\
\operatorname{osc}(D_\mu\mathcal R_1)&\leq CG'(\theta),    \notag\\
\operatorname{osc}(D_\mu C_{t,n})
&\leq CG'(\theta)\varrho^{n-t}.
\label{eq:dmop-direct-local-derivative-oscillation}
\end{align}
The uniform ratio bounds also give
$\inf_l\nu(a_l)\geq C^{-1}$.  Apply Claim~
\ref{claim:normalized-ratio-perturbations} with
$b_l=a_lh_{s,i}$.  Equations~
(\ref{eq:auxiliary-paired-ratio-bound}) and
(\ref{eq:dmop-direct-local-derivative-oscillation}) give
\begin{align}
&\left|
C_{t,n}(\nu)-C_{t,n}(\mu)-(\nu-\mu)D_\mu C_{t,n}
\right|                                                     \notag\\
&\quad\leq
C^2\left|(\nu-\mu)(a_1-a_0)\right|
\left|(\nu-\mu)D_\mu\mathcal R_1\right|                    \notag\\
&\qquad+
C\left|(\nu-\mu)(a_0)\right|
\left|(\nu-\mu)D_\mu C_{t,n}\right|.
\label{eq:dmop-direct-local-quadratic-remainder}
\end{align}
In each product on the right, one oscillation is bounded by
$C\varrho^{n-t}$ and the other by $CG'(\theta)$.  Thus the factor
$\varrho^{n-t}$ follows from the displayed two-ratio cancellation,
rather than from a mixing assertion for the historical function
$h_{s,i}$.

\medskip\noindent\emph{Step 4: telescope over particle updates.}\quad
We now write the full local telescope before summing any norms.  Put
\begin{equation}
\mu_t^J=
\begin{cases}
\eta_1,&t=1,\\
\Phi_t\{m(\xi_{t-1}^P)\},&t\geq2.
\end{cases}
\label{eq:dmop-direct-local-predictor-law}
\end{equation}
In this display, $\Phi_t$ denotes the canonical path-space lift of the
same predictor update: the potential is evaluated at the last location,
multinomial selection chooses a complete path, and $M_t$ appends the new
location.  Its last-coordinate marginal is the predictor update already
defined in Section~\ref{appendix:feynman}.
When $H_{t,n}$ or $C_{t,n}^{(s)}$ is applied to $\eta_t$ below,
$\eta_t$ is read through the same canonical path-space lift.
Thus, conditionally on the preceding prediction cloud, the $J$ paths
whose empirical measure is $m(\xi_t^P)$ are independent with common law
$\mu_t^J$.  For the
current predictor term in
Equation~(\ref{eq:dmop-direct-exact-predictor-decomposition}), define
\begin{equation}
H_{t,n}(\mu)=\Phi_{t,n}(\mu)(h_{n,i}),
\qquad 1\leq t\leq n,
\label{eq:dmop-direct-current-predictor-continuation}
\end{equation}
where $\Phi_{n,n}$ is the identity.  For $t<s$, extend the already
defined continuation functional by
\begin{equation}
C_{t,n}^{(s)}(\mu)
=C_{s,n}^{(s)}\{\Phi_{t,s}(\mu)\}.
\label{eq:dmop-direct-backward-continuation-definition}
\end{equation}
Equations~(\ref{eq:dmop-direct-continuation-consistency}) and
(\ref{eq:weak-bias-composition-identity}) then give the exact telescopes
\begin{align}
&H_{n,n}\{m(\xi_n^P)\}-H_{n,n}(\eta_n)                    \notag\\
&\quad=
\sum_{t=1}^{n}
\left[
H_{t,n}\{m(\xi_t^P)\}-H_{t,n}(\mu_t^J)
\right],
\label{eq:dmop-direct-current-predictor-telescope}
\\
&C_{n,n}^{(s)}\{m(\xi_n^P)\}-C_{n,n}^{(s)}(\eta_n)       \notag\\
&\quad=
\sum_{t=1}^{n}
\left[
C_{t,n}^{(s)}\{m(\xi_t^P)\}-C_{t,n}^{(s)}(\mu_t^J)
\right].
\label{eq:dmop-direct-continuation-full-telescope}
\end{align}
For example, the second term in the $t$th summand of
Equation~(\ref{eq:dmop-direct-continuation-full-telescope}) is
$C_{t-1,n}^{(s)}\{m(\xi_{t-1}^P)\}$ for $t\geq2$, so consecutive terms
cancel.  At $t=1$, $\mu_1^J=\eta_1$, and the remaining endpoints are
exactly those displayed on the left.  The same verification proves
Equation~(\ref{eq:dmop-direct-current-predictor-telescope}).

\medskip\noindent\emph{Step 5: lag moments along a sampled genealogy.}\quad
We first prove the particle-genealogy estimate used below.  For
$q\in\{2,4\}$ and every $t\leq N$,
\begin{align}
&\max\Bigg\{
\E\,\Psi_t\{m(\xi_t^P)\}
\left[
\left|
\sum_{s=1}^{t}\alpha^{t-s}h_{s,i}
-\Psi_t\{m(\xi_t^P)\}
\left(\sum_{s=1}^{t}\alpha^{t-s}h_{s,i}\right)
\right|^q
\right],                                                   \notag\\
&\hspace{31mm}
\E\,\mu_t^J
\left[
\left|
\sum_{s=1}^{t}\alpha^{t-s}h_{s,i}
-\mu_t^J
\left(\sum_{s=1}^{t}\alpha^{t-s}h_{s,i}\right)
\right|^q
\right]
\Bigg\}                                                    \notag\\
&\qquad\leq
C G'(\theta)^q
\left\{
\sum_{r=0}^{t-1}\sum_{u=0}^{t-1}
\alpha^{r+u}\varrho^{|r-u|}
\right\}^{q/2}.
\label{eq:dmop-genealogical-lag-moments}
\end{align}

To verify Equation~(\ref{eq:dmop-genealogical-lag-moments}), condition
on the arrays of particle locations, but not on their ancestral indices.
The exact backward representation of a randomly selected ancestral line
in Equation~(3.3) of \citet{chanDelMoral18} has, from time $s$ to time
$s-1$, the transition
\begin{align}
&\sum_{j=1}^{J}
\frac{
g_{s-1,j}^{\phi}
f_{X_s\mid X_{s-1}}
(x_s\mid x_{s-1,j}^{P,\phi};\theta)
}{
\sum_{a=1}^{J}
g_{s-1,a}^{\phi}
f_{X_s\mid X_{s-1}}
(x_s\mid x_{s-1,a}^{P,\phi};\theta)
}
\delta_{x_{s-1,j}^{P,\phi}}(dx_{s-1}).
\label{eq:dmop-particle-backward-kernel}
\end{align}
The terminal distribution is the normalized measurement-weighted
prediction cloud for $\Psi_t\{m(\xi_t^P)\}$.  For $\mu_t^J$, the same
formula applies after conditioning additionally on the newly propagated
location at time $t$; its terminal distribution is otherwise immaterial
for the bounds below.

For every measurable set $A$, Assumption
{\refAssumpBoundedProcess} gives the pointwise minorization
\begin{align}
&\sum_{j=1}^{J}
\frac{
g_{s-1,j}^{\phi}
f_{X_s\mid X_{s-1}}
(x_s\mid x_{s-1,j}^{P,\phi};\theta)
}{
\sum_{a=1}^{J}
g_{s-1,a}^{\phi}
f_{X_s\mid X_{s-1}}
(x_s\mid x_{s-1,a}^{P,\phi};\theta)
}
\1\{x_{s-1,j}^{P,\phi}\in A\}                          \notag\\
&\quad\geq
\frac{\underbar M}{\bar M}
\frac{
\sum_{j=1}^{J}g_{s-1,j}^{\phi}
\1\{x_{s-1,j}^{P,\phi}\in A\}
}{
\sum_{a=1}^{J}g_{s-1,a}^{\phi}
}.
\label{eq:dmop-particle-backward-minorization}
\end{align}
Consequently, the Dobrushin coefficient of every transition in
Equation~(\ref{eq:dmop-particle-backward-kernel}) is at most
$1-\underbar M/\bar M\leq\varrho$.  If $1\leq s\leq u\leq t$,
composition of these backward transitions and
Equation~(\ref{eq:bounded-realized-measurement-score}) therefore give
\begin{equation}
\operatorname{osc}\left\{
\E(h_{s,i}\mid X_u,\hbox{ location arrays})
\right\}
\leq
2G'(\theta)\varrho^{u-s}.
\label{eq:dmop-backward-conditional-score-contraction}
\end{equation}
For real random variables $U,V$,
\begin{equation}
|\operatorname{Cov}(U,V)|
\leq\frac14\operatorname{osc}(U)\operatorname{osc}(V).
\end{equation}
Using the backward Markov property, followed by
Equation~(\ref{eq:dmop-backward-conditional-score-contraction}), yields
\begin{align}
&\left|
\operatorname{Cov}
\{h_{s,i}(X_s),h_{u,i}(X_u)\mid\hbox{ location arrays}\}
\right|                                                     \notag\\
&\quad=
\left|
\operatorname{Cov}
\{\E(h_{s,i}\mid X_u,\hbox{ location arrays}),
h_{u,i}(X_u)\mid\hbox{ location arrays}\}
\right|                                                     \notag\\
&\quad\leq
G'(\theta)^2\varrho^{u-s}.
\label{eq:dmop-backward-lag-covariance}
\end{align}
Expanding the conditional variance and putting $r=t-s$ and $v=t-u$
now gives
\begin{align}
&\Var\left(
\left.
\sum_{s=1}^{t}\alpha^{t-s}h_{s,i}(X_s)
\right|\hbox{ location arrays}
\right)                                                     \notag\\
&\quad\leq
G'(\theta)^2
\sum_{r=0}^{t-1}\sum_{v=0}^{t-1}
\alpha^{r+v}\varrho^{|r-v|}.
\label{eq:dmop-backward-additive-variance}
\end{align}

For completeness, the fourth moment follows from the same backward
transitions.  Put $c_s=\alpha^{t-s}$ and expose the selected path in the
order $X_t,X_{t-1},\ldots,X_1$.  At the step revealing $X_u$, the
oscillation of the corresponding Doob martingale difference is at most
\begin{equation}
2G'(\theta)\sum_{s=1}^{u}c_s\varrho^{u-s}.
\label{eq:dmop-backward-doob-difference-bound}
\end{equation}
Indeed, conditional expectations of the terms with $s<u$ are obtained by
the backward transitions from $u$ to $s$, and each has oscillation at
most $2G'(\theta)c_s\varrho^{u-s}$; terms with $s>u$ have already been
revealed and cancel from that martingale difference.

Conditional Hoeffding's lemma applied successively to these centered
differences gives, for every $z>0$,
\begin{align}
&\Pr\left\{
\left|
\sum_{s=1}^{t}c_sh_{s,i}(X_s)
-\E\left(\left.\sum_{s=1}^{t}c_sh_{s,i}(X_s)
\right|\hbox{ location arrays}\right)
\right|\geq z
\,\middle|\,\hbox{ location arrays}
\right\}                                                    \notag\\
&\quad\leq
2\exp\left[
-\frac{z^2}{
2G'(\theta)^2
\sum_{u=1}^{t}
\left\{\sum_{s=1}^{u}c_s\varrho^{u-s}\right\}^2}
\right].
\label{eq:dmop-backward-additive-tail}
\end{align}
Young's convolution inequality gives
\begin{align}
\sum_{u=1}^{t}
\left\{\sum_{s=1}^{u}c_s\varrho^{u-s}\right\}^2
&\leq
\left(\sum_{d=0}^{\infty}\varrho^d\right)^2
\sum_{s=1}^{t}c_s^2                                      \notag\\
&\leq
\frac1{(1-\varrho)^2}
\sum_{r=0}^{t-1}\sum_{v=0}^{t-1}
\alpha^{r+v}\varrho^{|r-v|}.
\label{eq:dmop-backward-convolution-square}
\end{align}
Using
$\E|U|^4=4\int_0^\infty z^3\Pr(|U|\geq z)\,dz$ in
Equations~(\ref{eq:dmop-backward-additive-tail}) and
(\ref{eq:dmop-backward-convolution-square}) proves
\begin{align}
&\E\left[
\left.
\left|
