\sum_{r=1}^{\infty}(1-\alpha^r)\varrho^r                    \notag\\
&=
C\sqrt p\,G'(\theta)
\left\{
\sum_{r=1}^{\infty}\varrho^r
-\sum_{r=1}^{\infty}(\alpha\varrho)^r
\right\}                                                     \notag\\
&=
C\sqrt p\,G'(\theta)
\left\{
\frac{\varrho}{1-\varrho}
-\frac{\alpha\varrho}{1-\alpha\varrho}
\right\}                                                     \notag\\
&=
C\sqrt p\,G'(\theta)
\frac{\varrho(1-\alpha)}
{(1-\varrho)(1-\alpha\varrho)}.
\end{align}
\end{proof}

\iffalse
\begin{thm}[Refined MSE bound for DMOP-$\alpha$]
\label{thm:refined-dmop-alpha-mse}
Suppose the observations are fixed, $\theta=\phi$, $\alpha\in[0,1)$,
Assumptions {\refAssumpBoundedProcess},
{\refAssumpBoundedMeasurement}, {\refAssumpDifferentiableSimulator}, and
{\refAssumpPrimitiveInnovation} hold, and multinomial resampling is used.
Then there are
\(\varrho\in(0,1)\) and \(C<\infty\), independent of \(N,J\), and
\(\alpha\), such that
\begin{align}
&\E\left\|
\nabla_\theta\hat\ell^\alpha(\theta)
-\nabla_\theta\ell(\theta)
\right\|_2^2                                                   \notag\\
&\quad\leq C
pG'(\theta)^2
\left[
\frac{N}{J(1-\alpha)^2}
+N^2
\left\{
\frac{\varrho(1-\alpha)}
{(1-\varrho)(1-\alpha\varrho)}
+\frac{N}{J(1-\alpha)}
\right\}^2
\right].
\label{eq:refined-dmop-alpha-mse}
\end{align}
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}[Proof.]
For a square-integrable random vector $Z$ and a deterministic vector $z$,
\begin{equation}
\E\|Z-z\|_2^2
=
\E\|Z-\E Z\|_2^2+\|\E Z-z\|_2^2.
\label{eq:vector-bias-variance-identity}
\end{equation}
The first term is bounded by
Lemma~\ref{lem:dmop-alpha-variance}. For the second term, use
\begin{equation}
\nabla_\theta\hat\ell^\alpha(\theta)
=\sum_{n=1}^N\nabla_\theta\hat\ell_n^\alpha(\theta),
\qquad
\nabla_\theta\ell(\theta)
=\sum_{n=1}^N\nabla_\theta\ell_n(\theta),
\label{eq:full-score-conditional-sums}
\end{equation}
add and subtract each population DMOP-$\alpha$ conditional score, and
apply the triangle inequality. Lemmas~
\ref{lem:discounted-dmop-particle-bias} and
\ref{lem:dmop-population-discount-bias} then give
\begin{align}
&\left\|
\E\nabla_\theta\hat\ell^\alpha(\theta)
-\nabla_\theta\ell(\theta)
\right\|_2                                                   \notag\\
&\quad=
\left\|
\sum_{n=1}^N
\left\{
\E\nabla_\theta\hat\ell_n^\alpha(\theta)
-\nabla_\theta\ell_n^\alpha(\theta)
\right\}
+
\sum_{n=1}^N
\left\{
\nabla_\theta\ell_n^\alpha(\theta)
-\nabla_\theta\ell_n(\theta)
\right\}
\right\|_2                                                   \notag\\
&\quad\leq
\sum_{n=1}^{N}
\left\|
\E\nabla_\theta\hat\ell_n^\alpha(\theta)
-\nabla_\theta\ell_n^\alpha(\theta)
\right\|_2                                                   \notag\\
&\qquad+
\sum_{n=1}^{N}
\left\|
\nabla_\theta\ell_n^\alpha(\theta)
-\nabla_\theta\ell_n(\theta)
\right\|_2                                                   \notag\\
&\quad\leq
\frac{C\sqrt p\,G'(\theta)}{J(1-\alpha)}
\sum_{n=1}^{N}n
+CN\sqrt p\,G'(\theta)
\frac{\varrho(1-\alpha)}
{(1-\varrho)(1-\alpha\varrho)}                              \notag\\
&\quad=
\frac{C\sqrt p\,G'(\theta)}{J(1-\alpha)}
\frac{N(N+1)}2
+CN\sqrt p\,G'(\theta)
\frac{\varrho(1-\alpha)}
{(1-\varrho)(1-\alpha\varrho)}                              \notag\\
&\quad\leq
CN\sqrt p\,G'(\theta)
\left\{
\frac{N}{J(1-\alpha)}
+\frac{\varrho(1-\alpha)}
{(1-\varrho)(1-\alpha\varrho)}
\right\},
\end{align}
where the equality uses
$\sum_{n=1}^Nn=N(N+1)/2$, and the last line uses $N+1\leq2N$.
Squaring this bound and using
Equation~(\ref{eq:vector-bias-variance-identity}) proves the theorem.
\end{proof}

To make the resulting tradeoff explicit, first observe that
$1-\alpha\varrho\geq1-\varrho$. Therefore
\begin{equation}
\frac{\varrho(1-\alpha)}
{(1-\varrho)(1-\alpha\varrho)}
\leq
\frac{\varrho}{(1-\varrho)^2}(1-\alpha)
\lesssim
1-\alpha.
\label{eq:population-factor-delta-bound}
\end{equation}
Theorem~\ref{thm:refined-dmop-alpha-mse} and Equation~
(\ref{eq:population-factor-delta-bound}) therefore give
\begin{align}
\operatorname{MSE}_{\alpha}
&\lesssim
pG'(\theta)^2
\left[
\frac{N}{J(1-\alpha)^2}
+N^2\left\{(1-\alpha)+\frac{N}{J(1-\alpha)}\right\}^2
\right]                                                       \notag\\
&\leq
CpG'(\theta)^2
\left\{
\frac{N}{J(1-\alpha)^2}
+N^2(1-\alpha)^2
+\frac{N^4}{J^2(1-\alpha)^2}
\right\}                                                     \notag\\
&=
CpG'(\theta)^2
\left\{
N^2(1-\alpha)^2
+\frac{N/J+N^4/J^2}{(1-\alpha)^2}
\right\},
\label{eq:dmop-leading-tradeoff}
\end{align}
where the last inequality uses $(a+b)^2\leq2a^2+2b^2$. Here
$\operatorname{MSE}_{\alpha}$ denotes the expectation on the left of
Equation~(\ref{eq:refined-dmop-alpha-mse}). The right side of Equation~
(\ref{eq:dmop-leading-tradeoff}) is minimized over $1-\alpha>0$ when
\begin{equation}
1-\alpha
=
\left(
\frac{1}{NJ}+\frac{N^2}{J^2}
\right)^{1/4},
\label{eq:dmop-interior-alpha}
\end{equation}
provided that the right side of Equation~
(\ref{eq:dmop-interior-alpha}) is at most one. Indeed, differentiating the
two terms inside braces in the final line of Equation~
(\ref{eq:dmop-leading-tradeoff}) with respect to $1-\alpha$ gives
\begin{align}
&2N^2(1-\alpha)
-2\left(\frac{N}{J}+\frac{N^4}{J^2}\right)(1-\alpha)^{-3}=0,
\notag\\
&\hspace{15mm}(1-\alpha)^4
=
\frac{N/J+N^4/J^2}{N^2}
=
\frac{1}{NJ}+\frac{N^2}{J^2}.
\end{align}
Substitution into Equation~(\ref{eq:dmop-leading-tradeoff}) gives
\begin{align}
&N^2(1-\alpha)^2
+\frac{N/J+N^4/J^2}{(1-\alpha)^2}                             \notag\\
&\qquad=
2N\left(\frac{N}{J}+\frac{N^4}{J^2}\right)^{1/2}             \notag\\
&\qquad\leq
2\left(\frac{N^{3/2}}{\sqrt J}+\frac{N^3}{J}\right),
\label{eq:dmop-optimized-upper-calculation}
\end{align}
where the final inequality uses $\sqrt{a+b}\leq\sqrt a+\sqrt b$.
Consequently,
\begin{equation}
\operatorname{MSE}_{\alpha}
\lesssim
pG'(\theta)^2
\left(
\frac{N^{3/2}}{\sqrt J}
+\frac{N^3}{J}
\right).
\label{eq:dmop-interior-mse}
\end{equation}
The earlier choice $1-\alpha=(N/J)^{1/2}$ gives the valid but not fully optimized bound
$1+N^3/J$. It has the same leading order as Equation~
(\ref{eq:dmop-interior-mse}) when $N\lesssim J\lesssim N^3$, but is loose
when $J\gg N^3$. The term $N^3/J$ in Equation~
(\ref{eq:dmop-interior-mse}) comes from the particle-bias term, not the
variance in Lemma~\ref{lem:dmop-alpha-variance}: Lemma~
\ref{lem:discounted-dmop-particle-bias} gives conditional-score bias
$O\{n/[J(1-\alpha)]\}$, summation over $n=1,\ldots,N$ gives
$O\{N^2/[J(1-\alpha)]\}$, and squaring gives
$O\{N^4/[J^2(1-\alpha)^2]\}$.

\begin{remark}[What a uniform weak-bias estimate would change]
Suppose, in addition to the conditions of
Theorem~\ref{thm:refined-dmop-alpha-mse}, that the integrated particle
genealogy satisfies
\begin{equation}
\left\|
\E\Delta_{n,r}^J-\Delta_{n,r}
\right\|_2
\leq
\frac{C\sqrt p\,G'(\theta)}{J}\varrho^r
\label{eq:conditional-integrated-genealogy-bound}
\end{equation}
uniformly in $n,r,J$, with the same $\varrho$ as in Lemma~
\ref{lem:derivative-measure-forgetting}. Equation~
(\ref{eq:conditional-integrated-genealogy-bound}) and the lag
decomposition in Equation~(\ref{eq:discounted-bias-lag-decomposition})
give
\begin{align}
&\left\|
\E\nabla_\theta\hat\ell_n^\alpha(\theta)
-\nabla_\theta\ell_n^\alpha(\theta)
\right\|_2                                                     \notag\\
&\qquad\leq
\frac{C\sqrt p\,G'(\theta)}{J}
\sum_{r=0}^{n-1}(\alpha\varrho)^r
\leq
\frac{C\sqrt p\,G'(\theta)}{J(1-\varrho)}.
\end{align}
Summing this inequality over $n=1,\ldots,N$ gives
\begin{equation}
\left\|
\sum_{n=1}^N
\left\{
\E\nabla_\theta\hat\ell_n^\alpha(\theta)
-\nabla_\theta\ell_n^\alpha(\theta)
\right\}
\right\|_2
\leq
\frac{CN\sqrt p\,G'(\theta)}{J(1-\varrho)}.
\label{eq:conditional-full-particle-bias}
\end{equation}
Lemma~\ref{lem:dmop-population-discount-bias}, Equation~
(\ref{eq:population-factor-delta-bound}), and the triangle inequality give
\begin{align}
&\left\|
\sum_{n=1}^N
\left\{
\nabla_\theta\ell_n^\alpha(\theta)
-\nabla_\theta\ell_n(\theta)
\right\}
\right\|_2                                                     \notag\\
&\qquad\leq
\sum_{n=1}^N
\left\|
\nabla_\theta\ell_n^\alpha(\theta)
-\nabla_\theta\ell_n(\theta)
\right\|_2                                                     \notag\\
&\qquad\leq
CN\sqrt p\,G'(\theta)(1-\alpha).
\label{eq:conditional-full-population-bias}
\end{align}
Adding Equations~(\ref{eq:conditional-full-particle-bias}) and
(\ref{eq:conditional-full-population-bias}), then using
$(a+b)^2\leq2a^2+2b^2$, yields
\begin{align}
&\left\|
\E\nabla_\theta\hat\ell^\alpha(\theta)
-\nabla_\theta\ell(\theta)
\right\|_2^2                                                   \notag\\
&\qquad\leq
CpG'(\theta)^2
\left\{
N^2(1-\alpha)^2+\frac{N^2}{J^2}
\right\},
\label{eq:conditional-full-bias-squared}
\end{align}
where the fixed factor $(1-\varrho)^{-2}$ is absorbed into $C$.
Finally, the bias--variance identity in Equation~
(\ref{eq:vector-bias-variance-identity}), Lemma~
\ref{lem:dmop-alpha-variance}, and Equation~
(\ref{eq:conditional-full-bias-squared}) give
\begin{equation}
\operatorname{MSE}_{\alpha}
\lesssim
pG'(\theta)^2
\left\{
\frac{N}{J(1-\alpha)^2}
+N^2(1-\alpha)^2
+\frac{N^2}{J^2}
\right\}.
\label{eq:conditional-sharp-tradeoff}
\end{equation}
With $1-\alpha=(NJ)^{-1/4}$, the right side of
Equation~(\ref{eq:conditional-sharp-tradeoff}) is at most
\begin{equation}
CpG'(\theta)^2
\left(
\frac{N^{3/2}}{\sqrt J}+\frac{N^2}{J^2}
\right).
\label{eq:conditional-sharp-interior-mse}
\end{equation}
This is only a conditional upper bound. In particular, it does not imply
that DMOP-$\alpha$ has smaller MSE than either endpoint.

Equation~(\ref{eq:conditional-integrated-genealogy-bound}) is not a
consequence of Assumptions {\refAssumpBoundedMeasurement} and
{\refAssumpDifferentiableSimulator}. The deterministic filter-derivative
stability theorem of \citet{tadicHigher20} and the
time-uniform results for the $O(J^2)$ backward derivative estimator
\citep{delMoral15derivative,tadic18} do not establish it for the $O(J)$
DMOP genealogy. The finite-horizon result of \citet{chanDelMoral18} gives
the factor $n/J$ in Lemma~\ref{lem:lagwise-weak-error}. It cannot be applied
only to the last $r+1$ steps of the actual DMOP coefficient because the
total reparameterized-simulator derivative carried at time $n-r$ already
depends on the earlier genealogy. In particular, that result does not give
the geometrically decreasing factor in
Equation~(\ref{eq:conditional-integrated-genealogy-bound}). Consequently,
the sharper bound records precisely the additional particle-level estimate
that would be needed; it is not claimed under Assumptions
{\refAssumpBoundedProcess}--{\refAssumpPrimitiveInnovation} alone.
\end{remark}
\fi

\iffalse
\begin{thm}[MSE bound for DMOP-$\alpha$]
\label{thm:refined-dmop-alpha-mse}
Suppose the observations are fixed, $J\geq2$, $\theta=\phi$,
$\alpha\in[0,1]$,
Assumptions {\refAssumpBoundedProcess},
{\refAssumpBoundedMeasurement}, and
{\refAssumpDifferentiableSimulator} hold, and multinomial resampling is
used.  Then there are $C<\infty$ and $\varrho\in(0,1)$, independent of
$N,J$, and $\alpha$, such that
\begin{align}
&\E\left\|
\nabla_\theta\hat\ell^\alpha(\theta)
-\nabla_\theta\ell(\theta)
\right\|_2^2                                                   \notag\\
&\quad\lesssim
pG'(\theta)^2
\left[
\frac1J\left\{
\sum_{t=1}^{N-1}(1-\alpha^t)^2
+\left(\sum_{r=0}^{N-1}\alpha^r\right)^2
\right\}
+N^2\left\{
\frac{\varrho(1-\alpha)}
{(1-\varrho)(1-\alpha\varrho)}
\right\}^2
+\frac{N^2}{J^2}
\right].
\label{eq:refined-dmop-alpha-mse}
\end{align}
Consequently, for $0\leq\alpha<1$,
\begin{align}
&\E\left\|
\nabla_\theta\hat\ell^\alpha(\theta)
-\nabla_\theta\ell(\theta)
\right\|_2^2                                                   \notag\\
&\quad\lesssim
pG'(\theta)^2
\left\{
\frac{N}{J}
+\frac{1}{J(1-\alpha)^2}
+N^2(1-\alpha)^2
+\frac{N^2}{J^2}
\right\}.
\label{eq:refined-dmop-alpha-mse-simplified}
\end{align}
The finite-sum display in Equation~
(\ref{eq:refined-dmop-alpha-mse}) is defined directly at $\alpha=1$;
its three terms then have
orders $N^2/J$, $0$, and $N^2/J^2$, respectively.
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}[Proof.]
For a square-integrable random vector $Z$ and a deterministic vector $z$,
expansion of the square gives
\begin{align}
\E\|Z-z\|_2^2
&=
\E\|Z-\E Z+\E Z-z\|_2^2                                    \notag\\
&=
\E\|Z-\E Z\|_2^2
+2\E\{(Z-\E Z)^\mathsf T(\E Z-z)\}
+\|\E Z-z\|_2^2                                             \notag\\
&=
\Var(Z)+\|\E Z-z\|_2^2,
\label{eq:vector-bias-variance-identity}
\end{align}
because $\E(Z-\E Z)=0$.

Apply Equation~(\ref{eq:vector-bias-variance-identity}) with
$Z=\nabla_\theta\hat\ell^\alpha(\theta)$ and
$z=\nabla_\theta\ell(\theta)$. Lemma~
\ref{lem:dmop-alpha-variance} gives
\begin{equation}
\Var\left(\nabla_\theta\hat\ell^\alpha(\theta)\right)
\lesssim
\frac{pG'(\theta)^2}{J}
\left\{
\sum_{t=1}^{N-1}(1-\alpha^t)^2
+\left(\sum_{r=0}^{N-1}\alpha^r\right)^2
\right\}.
\label{eq:refined-theorem-variance-term}
\end{equation}
For the bias, add and subtract the full population DMOP-$\alpha$ score.
The triangle inequality, Lemma~
\ref{lem:dmop-full-particle-bias}, and Lemma~
\ref{lem:dmop-population-discount-bias} give
\begin{align}
&\left\|
\E\nabla_\theta\hat\ell^\alpha(\theta)
-\nabla_\theta\ell(\theta)
\right\|_2                                                    \notag\\
&\quad\leq
\left\|
\E\nabla_\theta\hat\ell^\alpha(\theta)
-\nabla_\theta\ell^\alpha(\theta)
\right\|_2                                                    \notag\\
&\qquad+
\left\|
\sum_{n=1}^{N}
\{\nabla_\theta\ell_n^\alpha(\theta)
-\nabla_\theta\ell_n(\theta)\}
\right\|_2                                                    \notag\\
&\quad\leq
\frac{CN\sqrt p\,G'(\theta)}{J}
+CN\sqrt p\,G'(\theta)
\frac{\varrho(1-\alpha)}
{(1-\varrho)(1-\alpha\varrho)}.
\label{eq:refined-theorem-full-bias}
\end{align}
Using $(a+b)^2\leq2a^2+2b^2$ in
Equation~(\ref{eq:refined-theorem-full-bias}) gives
\begin{align}
&\left\|
\E\nabla_\theta\hat\ell^\alpha(\theta)
-\nabla_\theta\ell(\theta)
\right\|_2^2                                                  \notag\\
&\quad\leq
CpG'(\theta)^2
\left[
\frac{N^2}{J^2}
+N^2\left\{
\frac{\varrho(1-\alpha)}
{(1-\varrho)(1-\alpha\varrho)}
\right\}^2
\right].
\label{eq:refined-theorem-bias-squared}
\end{align}
Substitution of Equations~(\ref{eq:refined-theorem-variance-term}) and
(\ref{eq:refined-theorem-bias-squared}) into
Equation~(\ref{eq:vector-bias-variance-identity}) proves
Equation~(\ref{eq:refined-dmop-alpha-mse}). For $\alpha<1$,
\begin{align}
\sum_{t=1}^{N-1}(1-\alpha^t)^2
&\leq N,                                                     \notag\\
\sum_{r=0}^{N-1}\alpha^r
&\leq\frac1{1-\alpha},                                      \notag\\
\frac{\varrho(1-\alpha)}
{(1-\varrho)(1-\alpha\varrho)}
&\leq
\frac{\varrho}{(1-\varrho)^2}(1-\alpha).
\label{eq:refined-mse-geometric-simplifications}
\end{align}
Substitution of Equation~
(\ref{eq:refined-mse-geometric-simplifications}) into Equation~
(\ref{eq:refined-dmop-alpha-mse}), with the fixed factors in $\varrho$
absorbed by $\lesssim$, proves Equation~
(\ref{eq:refined-dmop-alpha-mse-simplified}).
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Choice of $\alpha$ within the proved MSE upper bound]
\label{rem:alpha-upper-bound-choice}
The finite-sum bound in Equation~
(\ref{eq:refined-dmop-alpha-mse}) can be minimized numerically over the
finite interval $\alpha\in[0,1]$ for given $N,J$, and $\varrho$.  For an
analytic rate calculation, Equation~
(\ref{eq:refined-dmop-alpha-mse-simplified}) separates the common terms
$N/J+N^2/J^2$ from the $\alpha$-dependent terms
\begin{equation}
\frac1{J(1-\alpha)^2}+N^2(1-\alpha)^2.
\label{eq:simplified-alpha-upper-bound}
\end{equation}
This replacement of the finite geometric sum by $(1-\alpha)^{-1}$ is
appropriate only before the sum saturates at the horizon.  Differentiating
Equation~(\ref{eq:simplified-alpha-upper-bound}) gives
\begin{align}
0
&=
\frac{d}{d(1-\alpha)}
\left\{
\frac1{J(1-\alpha)^2}+N^2(1-\alpha)^2
\right\}                                                     \notag\\
&=
-\frac2{J(1-\alpha)^3}+2N^2(1-\alpha),                     \notag\\
(1-\alpha)^4
&=\frac1{N^2J}.
\label{eq:rate-balancing-alpha-calculation}
\end{align}
Thus, with the fixed factors in $\varrho$ suppressed,
\begin{equation}
1-\alpha\asymp N^{-1/2}J^{-1/4}.
\label{eq:rate-balancing-alpha-choice}
\end{equation}
At this choice the two terms in Equation~
(\ref{eq:simplified-alpha-upper-bound}) are each of order $N/\sqrt J$.
The full bound is therefore
\begin{equation}
\operatorname{MSE}
\lesssim
pG'(\theta)^2
\left\{
\frac{N}{J}+\frac{N}{\sqrt J}+\frac{N^2}{J^2}
\right\}.
\label{eq:rate-balanced-mse-upper-bound}
\end{equation}
At Equation~(\ref{eq:rate-balancing-alpha-choice}),
$N(1-\alpha)\asymp N^{1/2}J^{-1/4}$.  Hence the unsaturated calculation
applies, up to the fixed factors in $\varrho$, when $J\lesssim N^2$.

When $N(1-\alpha)$ is small, the exact terminal geometric sum instead
satisfies
\begin{equation}
\sum_{r=0}^{N-1}\alpha^r
=
N-\frac{N(N-1)}2(1-\alpha)
+O\{N^3(1-\alpha)^2\}.
\label{eq:finite-horizon-geometric-expansion}
\end{equation}
Its squared contribution to the variance decreases to first order by
$N^2(N-1)(1-\alpha)/J$, while the displayed population-bias bound
increases as $N^2(1-\alpha)^2$.  Balancing these changes gives
\begin{equation}
1-\alpha\asymp\frac{N}{J},
\qquad
\operatorname{MSE}\lesssim
\frac{pG'(\theta)^2N^2}{J}
\quad\text{when }J\gtrsim N^2,
\label{eq:finite-horizon-alpha-choice}
\end{equation}
again with fixed $\varrho$ factors suppressed.  The leading rate in this
regime is the finite-horizon terminal variance at $\alpha=1$; moving left
changes only lower-order terms.

The $N/\sqrt J$ term in Equation~
(\ref{eq:rate-balanced-mse-upper-bound}) is a property of the proved uniform
upper bound, not an actual-MSE lower bound.  Indeed,
\begin{equation}
\nabla_\theta\ell(\theta)-\nabla_\theta\ell^\alpha(\theta)
=
\sum_{n=1}^{N}\sum_{r=1}^{n-1}
(1-\alpha^r)\Delta_{n,r},
\label{eq:global-population-discount-bias}
\end{equation}
and, for each fixed $r$,
\begin{equation}
1-\alpha^r
=r(1-\alpha)+O\{r^2(1-\alpha)^2\}.
\label{eq:discount-weight-endpoint-expansion}
\end{equation}
Lemma~\ref{lem:derivative-measure-forgetting} and the triangle inequality
bound Equation~(\ref{eq:global-population-discount-bias}) by a constant
times $N(1-\alpha)$.  They do not prove that the coefficient of
$1-\alpha$ is nonzero: the signed vector
$\sum_{n=1}^{N}\sum_{r=1}^{n-1}r\Delta_{n,r}$ can cancel.  Therefore the
actual population bias, and hence the actual optimally tuned MSE, can be
smaller than this upper bound.  No such cancellation is assumed in
Theorem~\ref{thm:refined-dmop-alpha-mse}.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[Fixed lag and effective memory]
Fix $0\leq k<N$ and retain only the coefficients
$\Delta_{n,r}^J$ with $0\leq r\leq\min(k,n-1)$ in Equation~
(\ref{eq:conditional-score-path-decomposition}). Reindexing the finite
sums by $s=n-r$ and telescoping over $n$ give
\begin{align}
&\sum_{n=1}^{N}\sum_{r=0}^{\min(k,n-1)}\Delta_{n,r}^J          \notag\\
&\quad=
\sum_{s=1}^{N-k-1}m(\xi_{s+k})(h_s)
+m(\xi_N)\left(\sum_{s=N-k}^{N}h_s\right).
\label{eq:fixed-lag-full-score-telescope}
\end{align}
Indeed, for each fixed $s$,
\begin{align}
&m(\xi_s)(h_s)
+\sum_{n=s+1}^{\min(N,s+k)}
\{m(\xi_n)(h_s)-m(\xi_{n-1})(h_s)\}                         \notag\\
&\quad=
m(\xi_{\min(N,s+k)})(h_s),
\end{align}
which yields Equation~(\ref{eq:fixed-lag-full-score-telescope}) after the
time-$N$ terms are combined. The identical population calculation gives
\begin{equation}
\sum_{s=1}^{N-k-1}\rho_{s+k}(h_s)
+\rho_N\left(\sum_{s=N-k}^{N}h_s\right).
\label{eq:fixed-lag-population-telescope}
\end{equation}

For the population truncation error,
Equations~(\ref{eq:full-population-score-path-average}) and
(\ref{eq:fixed-lag-population-telescope}) give
\begin{align}
&\nabla_\theta\ell(\theta)
-\left\{
\sum_{s=1}^{N-k-1}\rho_{s+k}(h_s)
+\rho_N\left(\sum_{s=N-k}^{N}h_s\right)
\right\}                                                     \notag\\
&\quad=
\sum_{s=1}^{N-k-1}\{\rho_N(h_s)-\rho_{s+k}(h_s)\}           \notag\\
&\quad=
\sum_{s=1}^{N-k-1}\sum_{u=s+k+1}^{N}\Delta_{u,u-s}.
\label{eq:fixed-lag-population-bias-telescope}
\end{align}
Lemma~\ref{lem:derivative-measure-forgetting} therefore gives
\begin{align}
&\left\|
\sum_{s=1}^{N-k-1}\sum_{u=s+k+1}^{N}\Delta_{u,u-s}
\right\|_2                                                   \notag\\
&\quad\leq
C\sqrt p\,G'(\theta)
\sum_{s=1}^{N-k-1}\sum_{r=k+1}^{\infty}\varrho^r            \notag\\
&\quad\leq
CN\sqrt p\,G'(\theta)
\frac{\varrho^{k+1}}{1-\varrho}.
\label{eq:fixed-lag-full-population-bias}
\end{align}
Equation~(\ref{eq:fixed-lag-full-population-bias}) is a population result
and contains no particle approximation.  We now add the particle error.
The particle in this manuscript is differentiable, so its location,
simulator gradient, and weight gradient are all functionals of the current
particle.  Hence $m(\xi_{s+k})(h_s)$ and
$m(\xi_N)(\sum_{s=N-k}^{N}h_s)$ are current differentiable-particle cloud
averages to which Lemma~\ref{lem:lagwise-weak-error} and the current-cloud
$L^4$ calculation in Lemma~\ref{lem:dmop-alpha-variance} apply.

Applying Lemma~\ref{lem:lagwise-weak-error} coordinatewise to Equation~
(\ref{eq:fixed-lag-full-score-telescope}) and its population counterpart
gives
\begin{align}
&\left\|
\E\left\{
\sum_{s=1}^{N-k-1}m(\xi_{s+k})(h_s)
+m(\xi_N)\left(\sum_{s=N-k}^{N}h_s\right)
\right\}
-\left\{
\sum_{s=1}^{N-k-1}\rho_{s+k}(h_s)
+\rho_N\left(\sum_{s=N-k}^{N}h_s\right)
\right\}
\right\|_2                                                   \notag\\
&\quad\leq
\frac{C\sqrt p\,G'(\theta)}{J}
\{N-k-1+(k+1)\}                                             \notag\\
&\quad\leq
\frac{CN\sqrt p\,G'(\theta)}{J}.
\label{eq:fixed-lag-full-particle-bias}
\end{align}
The first term in braces counts the $N-k-1$ separate averages, and the
second uses
\begin{equation}
\operatorname{osc}\left(\sum_{s=N-k}^{N}h_{s,i}\right)
\leq2(k+1)G'(\theta)
\end{equation}
for every coordinate $i$.

For the variance calculation, fix a coordinate $i$ and center every
average.  Equations~(\ref{eq:filtered-particle-l4}) and
(\ref{eq:l4-centering-bridge}) give
\begin{align}
\left\|
m(\xi_{s+k})(h_{s,i})-\E m(\xi_{s+k})(h_{s,i})
\right\|_{L^4}
&\leq\frac{CG'(\theta)}{\sqrt J},
\label{eq:fixed-lag-one-time-l4}
\\
\left\|
m(\xi_N)\left(\sum_{s=N-k}^{N}h_{s,i}\right)
-\E m(\xi_N)\left(\sum_{s=N-k}^{N}h_{s,i}\right)
\right\|_{L^4}
&\leq\frac{C(k+1)G'(\theta)}{\sqrt J}.
\label{eq:fixed-lag-terminal-l4}
\end{align}
Let $a(l)$ be the current-cloud mixing coefficient in Equation~
(\ref{eq:conditional-score-alpha-mixing}).  Davydov's inequality and
Equation~(\ref{eq:fixed-lag-one-time-l4}) give, for $s<t<N-k$,
the following bound.  Indeed, the sigma-field generated by the variables
through index $s$ is contained in the current-cloud sigma-field through
time $s+k$, while the variables from index $t$ onward are measurable with
respect to the cloud tail from time $t+k$ onward.  Their separation is
therefore $t-s$, even though the bounded current-particle functional
$h_s$ changes with $s$.
\begin{align}
&\left|
\Cov\{m(\xi_{s+k})(h_{s,i}),m(\xi_{t+k})(h_{t,i})\}
\right|                                                       \notag\\
&\quad\leq
\frac{CG'(\theta)^2}{J}a(t-s)^{1/2}.
\label{eq:fixed-lag-davydov-covariance}
\end{align}
Therefore, using Equation~(\ref{eq:conditional-score-mixing-sum}),
\begin{align}
&\Var\left\{\sum_{s=1}^{N-k-1}m(\xi_{s+k})(h_{s,i})\right\}  \notag\\
&\quad\leq
\frac{CG'(\theta)^2}{J}
\left\{
N-k-1
+2\sum_{l=1}^{N-k-2}(N-k-1-l)a(l)^{1/2}
\right\}                                                     \notag\\
&\quad\lesssim
\frac{NG'(\theta)^2}{J}.
\label{eq:fixed-lag-interior-variance}
\end{align}
Equation~(\ref{eq:fixed-lag-terminal-l4}) also gives
\begin{equation}
\Var\left\{
m(\xi_N)\left(\sum_{s=N-k}^{N}h_{s,i}\right)
\right\}
\leq
\frac{C(k+1)^2G'(\theta)^2}{J}.
\label{eq:fixed-lag-terminal-variance}
\end{equation}
All sums in this calculation are finite, and an empty sum is zero; thus
the same displays cover the boundary case $k=N-1$.
Applying Equation~(\ref{eq:two-term-variance-bound}) to the two terms in
Equation~(\ref{eq:fixed-lag-full-score-telescope}), summing over
$i=1,\ldots,p$, and using the manuscript's convention that $\lesssim$
suppresses the current-cloud mixing-time factor prove
\begin{equation}
\Var\left\{
\sum_{n=1}^{N}\sum_{r=0}^{\min(k,n-1)}\Delta_{n,r}^J
\right\}
\lesssim
\frac{pG'(\theta)^2}{J}\{N+(k+1)^2\}.
\label{eq:fixed-lag-full-variance}
\end{equation}

Combining Equations~(\ref{eq:fixed-lag-full-population-bias}),
(\ref{eq:fixed-lag-full-particle-bias}), and
(\ref{eq:fixed-lag-full-variance}) with the vector bias--variance
identity gives
\begin{align}
&\E\left\|
\sum_{n=1}^{N}\sum_{r=0}^{\min(k,n-1)}\Delta_{n,r}^J
-\nabla_\theta\ell(\theta)
\right\|_2^2                                                 \notag\\
&\quad\lesssim
pG'(\theta)^2
\left\{
\frac{N+(k+1)^2}{J}
+\frac{N^2}{J^2}
+\frac{N^2\varrho^{2(k+1)}}{(1-\varrho)^2}
\right\}.
\label{eq:fixed-lag-full-mse}
\end{align}
Thus the factor $\varrho$ is independent of $k$: the $k$ dependence is
the explicit power $\varrho^{k+1}$ and the explicit terminal-cloud term
$(k+1)^2/J$.

The natural effective-memory comparison is
\begin{equation}
k+1\asymp\sum_{r=0}^{N-1}\alpha^r
\label{eq:fixed-lag-effective-memory-comparison}
\end{equation}
because both sides count the number of appreciably retained lags. It does
match the particle-memory terms in the proved upper bounds:
$(k+1)^2/J$ in Equation~(\ref{eq:fixed-lag-full-mse}) and
$(\sum_{r=0}^{N-1}\alpha^r)^2/J$ in Theorem~
\ref{thm:refined-dmop-alpha-mse}.  It does not match the population-bias
terms. A hard cutoff leaves every lag through $k$ unchanged and has the
tail bound $\varrho^{k+1}$ in Equation~
(\ref{eq:fixed-lag-full-population-bias}), whereas exponential
discounting changes every positive lag and has the bound of order
$1-\alpha$ in Lemma~\ref{lem:dmop-population-discount-bias}.

For example, provided $\log(NJ)=o(N)$, as when $J$ is polynomial in $N$,
one may choose $k<N$ so that
\begin{equation}
\varrho^{2(k+1)}\leq\frac1{NJ}
\end{equation}
with $k+1\asymp\log(NJ)$.  This turns Equation~
(\ref{eq:fixed-lag-full-mse}) into
\begin{equation}
\operatorname{MSE}_{\mathrm{fixed\ lag}}
\lesssim
pG'(\theta)^2
\left\{
\frac{N}{J}+\frac{\log(NJ)^2}{J}+\frac{N^2}{J^2}
\right\}.
\label{eq:fixed-lag-logarithmic-choice}
\end{equation}
This upper bound can therefore be sharper than the tuned
DMOP-$\alpha$ upper bound.  The reason is not an extra factor of $N$ in the
discounted particle variance: the effective-memory terms agree after
$k+1\asymp\sum_r\alpha^r$.  The difference is that a hard cutoff has
geometric population truncation bias, whereas discounting perturbs every
positive lag and is bounded only linearly in $1-\alpha$ under the stated
assumptions.  This comparison concerns the proved upper bounds and
does not order the actual MSEs.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[Endpoint rates and oscillation normalization]
Lemma~\ref{lem:dmop-alpha-variance} gives
\begin{equation}
\Var\left(\nabla_\theta\hat\ell^\alpha(\theta)\right)
\lesssim
\frac{pG'(\theta)^2}{J}
\left\{
\sum_{t=1}^{N-1}(1-\alpha^t)^2
+\left(\sum_{r=0}^{N-1}\alpha^r\right)^2
\right\}.
\label{eq:current-particle-variance-summary}
\end{equation}
Thus the variance orders are $N/J$ at $\alpha=0$,
$N/J$ for every fixed $\alpha<1$, and
\begin{equation}
\frac{N^2}{J}
\label{eq:dmop-one-exact-squared-sum}
\end{equation}
at $\alpha=1$, up to the common factor $pG'(\theta)^2$ and the
mixing-time factor suppressed by $\lesssim$.

This resolves the normalization question for the present proof. At
$\alpha=1$, Equation~(\ref{eq:full-score-summation-by-parts}) contains
only the terminal current-particle average of
$\sum_{k=1}^{N}h_k$. Its coordinatewise oscillation is
$O\{NG'(\theta)\}$. A current-particle result normalized by
$\operatorname{osc}(f)\leq1$ has one-time MSE order $J^{-1}$; rescaling
therefore gives $N^2G'(\theta)^2/J$, not $N^3/J$.

Larger powers can still arise if one begins from a less specialized
path-space theorem. A norm-one historical estimate with MSE order $N/J$,
such as the finite-horizon estimate of \citet{chanDelMoral18}, becomes
$N^3G'(\theta)^2/J$ after the additional $N^2G'(\theta)^2$ rescaling. A
generic norm-one RMSE bound of order $N/\sqrt J$, as in the direct
path-space bound of \citet{delMoral03}, similarly gives
\begin{equation}
\operatorname{RMSE}
=
O\left(\frac{N^2G'(\theta)}{\sqrt J}\right),
\qquad
\operatorname{MSE}
=
O\left(\frac{N^4G'(\theta)^2}{J}\right).
\end{equation}
Thus $N^3/J$ or $N^4/J$ can arise from generic normalized
path-functional estimates, but neither is the current-particle rate
proved in Lemma~\ref{lem:dmop-alpha-variance}.

The lower-bound result of \citet{poyiadjis11} concerns their path-space
score estimator and does not apply to DMOP-$0$. At $\alpha=1$, DMOP has
the same undiscounted path-space structure, but its reparameterization
score contributions are not asserted to be particlewise identical to the
likelihood-ratio score contributions of \citet{poyiadjis11}.
More precisely, in the present notation, Theorem~1 of
\citet{poyiadjis11} gives a lower bound of order $N^2$ for the asymptotic
variance of $\sqrt J$ times the error of their $O(J)$ path-space
estimator.  Its leading Monte Carlo MSE in the particle central-limit
regime is therefore at least of order $N^2/J$.  The generic path-space
$L^2$ estimate quoted there from \citet{delMoral03} gives the distinct
upper bound $O(N^4/J)$.  These two results do not identify a single sharp
horizon order for the MSE of that estimator; in particular, the
$N^4/J$ quantity is an upper bound, not the Poyiadjis lower-bound rate.
Their separate $O(J^2)$ marginal algorithm is not the path-space
estimator covered by Theorem~1.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[Scope of the proved $\alpha$ dependence]
The singular dependence on $\alpha$ is confined to the terminal term
$J^{-1}(\sum_{r=0}^{N-1}\alpha^r)^2$ in Equation~
(\ref{eq:current-particle-variance-summary}); the remaining time sum is
$O(N/J)$. Thus the proved horizon order is $N/J$ for each fixed
$\alpha<1$.  The differentiated log-space recursion is analyzed through
the pair consisting of the particle location and its log-weight gradient;
no separate gradient-stability assumption is used.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[Ridge-type tuning and endpoint derivatives]
At $\theta=\phi$, the particle genealogy in
Equation~(\ref{eq:conditional-score-path-decomposition}) does not depend
on $\alpha$. Hence
\begin{equation}
\operatorname{MSE}_\alpha
=
\E\left\|
\sum_{n=1}^{N}\sum_{r=0}^{n-1}\alpha^r\Delta_{n,r}^J
-\nabla_\theta\ell(\theta)
\right\|_2^2
\label{eq:actual-mse-finite-polynomial}
\end{equation}
is a polynomial in $\alpha$ and has no singularity at $\alpha=1$.
Differentiating the finite sum gives
\begin{align}
\frac{d}{d\alpha}\operatorname{MSE}_\alpha
&=
2\E\left[
\left\{
\sum_{n=1}^{N}\sum_{r=0}^{n-1}\alpha^r\Delta_{n,r}^J
-\nabla_\theta\ell(\theta)
\right\}^{\!\mathsf T}
\left\{
\sum_{n=1}^{N}\sum_{r=1}^{n-1}
r\alpha^{r-1}\Delta_{n,r}^J
\right\}
\right].
\label{eq:actual-mse-alpha-derivative}
\end{align}
The second brace in Equation~
(\ref{eq:actual-mse-alpha-derivative}) is the derivative of a finite
polynomial.  At $\alpha=0$ it is evaluated directly as
\begin{equation}
\sum_{n=2}^{N}\Delta_{n,1}^J,
\end{equation}
so no convention involving an indeterminate power is needed.
Therefore moving right from DMOP-$0$ lowers the actual MSE precisely when
the right derivative in Equation~
(\ref{eq:actual-mse-alpha-derivative}) at zero is negative; moving left
from DMOP-$1$ lowers it precisely when the left derivative at one is
positive. If both inequalities are strict, continuity gives an interior
minimizer whose MSE is smaller than the MSE at either endpoint. The
assumptions used here do not determine these signed covariances, so they
do not prove either endpoint sign.

Remark~\ref{rem:alpha-upper-bound-choice} gives the corresponding calculation
for the proved upper bound.  It identifies rate-balancing interior
choices in two horizon regimes, but it does not determine either endpoint
derivative of the actual MSE in Equation~
(\ref{eq:actual-mse-alpha-derivative}).
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[Endpoint interpretation]
There is no DMOP-$0$ MSE lower bound under the present assumptions:
conditional population biases can cancel and can vanish in special
models. Consequently, upper bounds alone cannot prove that an interior
choice of $\alpha$ improves on DMOP-$0$.

For every fixed $N$, the finite-sum bound at $\alpha=1$ has the ordinary
Monte Carlo variance order $J^{-1}$; its dependence on the horizon is
$N^2/J$. The squared particle-bias term is $N^2/J^2$ and is lower order
as $J\to\infty$ with $N$ fixed. These fixed-$N$ statements should not be
confused with joint limits in which $N$ and $J$ grow together.

The quadratic asymptotic-variance lower bound of
\citet{poyiadjis11} is already stated for an unnormalized additive
functional. It must not be multiplied by another $N^2$ normalization
factor. Moreover, the DMOP-$1$ reparameterization score contributions are
not generally particlewise identical to their likelihood-ratio score
contributions, so their lower
bound is not automatically a DMOP-$1$ lower bound.
\end{remark}

\fi

\begin{thm}[MSE bounds for DMOP-$\alpha$]
\label{thm:refined-dmop-alpha-mse}
Under the conditions of Lemmas~\ref{lem:dmop-alpha-variance} and
\ref{lem:dmop-population-discount-bias}, for $\alpha\in[0,1]$,
\begin{align}
\E\left\|
\nabla_\theta\hat\ell^\alpha(\theta)
-\nabla_\theta\ell(\theta)
\right\|_2^2
&\lesssim
pG'(\theta)^2
\begin{aligned}[t]
\Bigg[&
\frac1J\sum_{t=1}^{N}
\sum_{r=0}^{t-1}\sum_{u=0}^{t-1}
\alpha^{r+u}\varrho^{|r-u|}                                  \\
&+
\frac1{J^2}
\left\{
\sum_{t=1}^{N}
\left(
\sum_{r=0}^{t-1}\sum_{u=0}^{t-1}
\alpha^{r+u}\varrho^{|r-u|}
\right)^{1/2}
\right\}^2                                                    \\
&+
\left\{
\sum_{r=1}^{N-1}(N-r)(1-\alpha^r)\varrho^r
\right\}^2
\Bigg].
\end{aligned}
\label{eq:verified-dmop-alpha-mse-finite-sum}
\end{align}
For $\alpha\in[0,1)$, this implies
\begin{align}
&\E\left\|
\nabla_\theta\hat\ell^\alpha(\theta)
-\nabla_\theta\ell(\theta)
\right\|_2^2                                                \notag\\
&\lesssim
pG'(\theta)^2
\left[
\left(\frac NJ+\frac{N^2}{J^2}\right)
\frac{1+\alpha\varrho}
{(1-\alpha^2)(1-\alpha\varrho)}
+N^2
\left\{
\frac{\varrho(1-\alpha)}
{(1-\varrho)(1-\alpha\varrho)}
\right\}^2
\right].
\label{eq:verified-dmop-alpha-mse-bound}
\end{align}
At $\alpha=1$,
\begin{equation}
\E\left\|
\nabla_\theta\hat\ell^1(\theta)
-\nabla_\theta\ell(\theta)
\right\|_2^2
\lesssim
pG'(\theta)^2
\min\left\{
N^2,\,
\frac{N^2}{J}+\frac{N^3}{J^2}
\right\}.
\label{eq:verified-dmop-one-mse-bound}
\end{equation}
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}[Proof.]
For reference, direct expansion gives the exact bias--variance identity
\begin{equation}
\E\left\|
\nabla_\theta\hat\ell^\alpha(\theta)
-\nabla_\theta\ell(\theta)
\right\|_2^2
=
\Var\left\{\nabla_\theta\hat\ell^\alpha(\theta)\right\}
+\left\|
\E\nabla_\theta\hat\ell^\alpha(\theta)
-\nabla_\theta\ell(\theta)
\right\|_2^2,
\label{eq:direct-vector-bias-variance-identity}
\end{equation}
because
\begin{equation}
\E\left[
\left\{\nabla_\theta\hat\ell^\alpha(\theta)
-\E\nabla_\theta\hat\ell^\alpha(\theta)\right\}^{\mathsf T}
\left\{\E\nabla_\theta\hat\ell^\alpha(\theta)
-\nabla_\theta\ell(\theta)\right\}
\right]
=0.
\end{equation}
For the MSE bound itself, add and subtract
$\nabla_\theta\ell^\alpha(\theta)$ before squaring.  The inequality
$\|a+b\|_2^2\leq2\|a\|_2^2+2\|b\|_2^2$ gives directly
\begin{align}
&\E\left\|
\nabla_\theta\hat\ell^\alpha(\theta)
-\nabla_\theta\ell(\theta)
\right\|_2^2                                                \notag\\
&\quad\leq
2\E\left\|
\nabla_\theta\hat\ell^\alpha(\theta)
-\nabla_\theta\ell^\alpha(\theta)
\right\|_2^2
+2\left\|
\nabla_\theta\ell^\alpha(\theta)
-\nabla_\theta\ell(\theta)
\right\|_2^2.
\label{eq:mse-proof-combined-particle-population-error}
\end{align}

\medskip\noindent\emph{Particle error.}\quad
Lemma~\ref{lem:dmop-alpha-variance} gives the direct particle error
\begin{align}
&\E\left\|
\nabla_\theta\hat\ell^\alpha(\theta)
-\nabla_\theta\ell^\alpha(\theta)
\right\|_2^2                                                \notag\\
&\quad\lesssim
pG'(\theta)^2
\begin{aligned}[t]
\Bigg[&
\frac1J\sum_{t=1}^{N}
\sum_{r=0}^{t-1}\sum_{u=0}^{t-1}
\alpha^{r+u}\varrho^{|r-u|}                                  \\
&+
\frac1{J^2}
\left\{
\sum_{t=1}^{N}
\left(
\sum_{r=0}^{t-1}\sum_{u=0}^{t-1}
\alpha^{r+u}\varrho^{|r-u|}
\right)^{1/2}
\right\}^2
\Bigg]
\end{aligned}.
\label{eq:mse-proof-direct-particle-error}
\end{align}
\medskip\noindent\emph{Population discounting error.}\quad
Summing the first bound in Lemma~
\ref{lem:dmop-population-discount-bias} over $n=1,\ldots,N$, using
the triangle inequality, and interchanging the two finite sums give
\begin{align}
\left\|
\nabla_\theta\ell^\alpha(\theta)
-\nabla_\theta\ell(\theta)
\right\|_2
&\leq
C\sqrt p\,G'(\theta)
\sum_{n=1}^{N}\sum_{r=1}^{n-1}(1-\alpha^r)\varrho^r       \notag\\
&=
C\sqrt p\,G'(\theta)
\sum_{r=1}^{N-1}(N-r)(1-\alpha^r)\varrho^r                \notag\\
&\leq
CN\sqrt p\,G'(\theta)
\frac{\varrho(1-\alpha)}
{(1-\varrho)(1-\alpha\varrho)}.
\label{eq:verified-full-population-discount-bias}
\end{align}

Insert Equation~(\ref{eq:mse-proof-direct-particle-error}) and the first
two lines of Equation~
(\ref{eq:verified-full-population-discount-bias}) into Equation~
(\ref{eq:mse-proof-combined-particle-population-error}).  Absorbing the
fixed factor $2$ into $\lesssim$ gives
\begin{align}
&\E\left\|
\nabla_\theta\hat\ell^\alpha(\theta)
-\nabla_\theta\ell(\theta)
\right\|_2^2                                                \notag\\
&\quad\lesssim
pG'(\theta)^2
\begin{aligned}[t]
\Bigg[&
\frac1J\sum_{t=1}^{N}
\sum_{r=0}^{t-1}\sum_{u=0}^{t-1}
\alpha^{r+u}\varrho^{|r-u|}                                  \\
&+
\frac1{J^2}
\left\{
\sum_{t=1}^{N}
\left(
\sum_{r=0}^{t-1}\sum_{u=0}^{t-1}
\alpha^{r+u}\varrho^{|r-u|}
\right)^{1/2}
\right\}^2                                                    \\
&+
\left\{
\sum_{r=1}^{N-1}(N-r)(1-\alpha^r)\varrho^r
\right\}^2
\Bigg],
\end{aligned}
\end{align}
which proves Equation~
(\ref{eq:verified-dmop-alpha-mse-finite-sum}).  For $\alpha<1$, Equation~
(\ref{eq:dmop-infinite-lag-double-sum}) bounds every finite double sum
by
\begin{equation}
\frac{1+\alpha\varrho}
{(1-\alpha^2)(1-\alpha\varrho)}.
\end{equation}
Therefore the first two particle terms are bounded by
\begin{equation}
\left(\frac NJ+\frac{N^2}{J^2}\right)
\frac{1+\alpha\varrho}
{(1-\alpha^2)(1-\alpha\varrho)}.
\end{equation}
Combining this inequality with the last line of
Equation~(\ref{eq:verified-full-population-discount-bias}) proves the
bound in Equation~(\ref{eq:verified-dmop-alpha-mse-bound}).  The
exact bias--variance identity does not create an additional power of
$N$.
Finally, at $\alpha=1$,
$\nabla_\theta\ell^1(\theta)=\nabla_\theta\ell(\theta)$.
Equation~(\ref{eq:refined-dmop-one-lag-particle-error}) therefore proves
Equation~(\ref{eq:verified-dmop-one-mse-bound}).
\end{proof}

\iffalse
Equation~(\ref{eq:dmop-alpha-coordinate-pathwise-bound}) gives
\begin{equation}
\left\|\nabla_\theta\hat\ell^\alpha(\theta)\right\|_2
\leq
N\sqrt p\,G'(\theta).
\label{eq:verified-particle-score-pathwise-bound}
\end{equation}
Equation~(\ref{eq:full-population-score-path-average}) and
Equation~(\ref{eq:bounded-realized-measurement-score}) likewise give
\begin{align}
\left\|\nabla_\theta\ell(\theta)\right\|_2
&=
\left\|\rho_N\left(\sum_{s=1}^{N}h_s\right)\right\|_2
                                                               \notag\\
&\leq
\rho_N\left(\left\|\sum_{s=1}^{N}h_s\right\|_2\right)
                                                               \notag\\
&\leq
N\sqrt p\,G'(\theta).
\label{eq:verified-exact-score-pathwise-bound}
\end{align}
The triangle inequality and Equations~
(\ref{eq:verified-particle-score-pathwise-bound})--
(\ref{eq:verified-exact-score-pathwise-bound}) imply, pathwise,
\begin{equation}
\left\|
\nabla_\theta\hat\ell^\alpha(\theta)
-\nabla_\theta\ell(\theta)
\right\|_2^2
\leq
4N^2pG'(\theta)^2.
\end{equation}
Taking expectations proves Equation~
(\ref{eq:verified-dmop-alpha-mse-bound}).

For a square-integrable random vector $Z$ and a deterministic vector
$z$, direct expansion gives
\begin{align}
\E\|Z-z\|_2^2
&=
\E\|Z-\E Z\|_2^2
+\|\E Z-z\|_2^2,
\end{align}
because the cross term has expectation zero.  Apply this identity with
$Z=\nabla_\theta\hat\ell^\alpha(\theta)$ and
$z=\nabla_\theta\ell(\theta)$, and add and subtract
$\nabla_\theta\ell^\alpha(\theta)$ inside the deterministic norm.  This
proves Equation~
(\ref{eq:verified-dmop-alpha-bias-variance-decomposition}).  Finally,
sum the bound in Lemma~\ref{lem:dmop-population-discount-bias} over
$n=1,\ldots,N$ and use the triangle inequality.  The result is
Equation~(\ref{eq:verified-full-population-discount-bias}).
\end{proof}
\fi

\begin{remark}[Neutral-model check]
The dependence on $\alpha$ in Lemma~\ref{lem:dmop-alpha-variance} is
conservative but cannot be replaced by a bound involving only the current
particle cloud.  For example, take iid ${\rm Uniform}[0,1]$ states,
constant weights at $\theta=\phi=0$, and
\begin{equation}
\Pr_\theta(Y_n=1\mid X_n=x)
=\frac12+\frac\theta4(2x-1),
\qquad y_n^*=1.
\end{equation}
Then $h_n=(2X_n-1)/2$ has variance $\sigma^2=1/12$, and direct
conditional multinomial calculation gives
\begin{equation}
\Var\left\{\nabla_\theta\hat\ell^\alpha(\theta)\right\}
=
\frac{\sigma^2}{J}
\left[
N(2-J^{-1})
+\sum_{r=1}^{N-1}(N-r)\alpha^{2r}(1-J^{-1})^{r+1}
\right].
\label{eq:neutral-dmop-alpha-exact-variance}
\end{equation}
Thus the variance has order $N/J$ for every fixed $\alpha<1$, and its
genealogical contribution has order $N/\{J(1-\alpha^2)\}$ before horizon
saturation.  At $\alpha=0$, this agrees with the DMOP-$0$ bound preceding
Theorem~\ref{thm:dmop-alpha-truncation-mse}.  The example is consistent
with the lag-moment bound proved in Lemma~
\ref{lem:dmop-alpha-variance}.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[Choice of $\alpha$ in the fixed-observation upper bound]
The identity $1-\alpha^r=(1-\alpha)\sum_{q=0}^{r-1}\alpha^q$
and $1-\alpha^q\leq q(1-\alpha)$ imply, uniformly in $N$,
\begin{align}
0
&\leq
(1-\alpha)\sum_{r=1}^{N-1}(N-r)r\varrho^r
-\sum_{r=1}^{N-1}(N-r)(1-\alpha^r)\varrho^r              \notag\\
&\leq
N(1-\alpha)^2\frac{\varrho^2}{(1-\varrho)^3},            \notag\\
\sum_{r=1}^{N-1}(N-r)(1-\alpha^r)\varrho^r
&\leq
N(1-\alpha)\frac{\varrho}{(1-\varrho)^2}.
\label{eq:finite-population-bias-first-order-bound}
\end{align}
No horizon-saturation argument is used for this population term.
Combining the sharper particle bound in Equation~
(\ref{eq:refined-dmop-alpha-lag-particle-error}) with the exact
$\varrho$-dependent population term in Theorem~
\ref{thm:refined-dmop-alpha-mse} gives, with fixed model constants and
before the geometric memory reaches the horizon,
\begin{align}
&\E\left\|
\nabla_\theta\hat\ell^\alpha(\theta)
-\nabla_\theta\ell(\theta)
\right\|_2^2                                                \notag\\
&\quad\lesssim
pG'(\theta)^2
\left[
\left(\frac NJ+\frac{N^2}{J^2}\right)\frac1{1-\alpha}
+N^2(1-\alpha)^2
\right].
\label{eq:direct-alpha-tuning-upper-bound}
\end{align}
The factor $(1-\alpha)^{-1}$ here is obtained from the exact factor
\begin{equation}
\frac{1+\alpha\varrho}
{(1-\alpha^2)(1-\alpha\varrho)},
\end{equation}
and the population term before suppressing fixed model constants is
\begin{equation}
N^2
\left\{
\frac{\varrho(1-\alpha)}
{(1-\varrho)(1-\alpha\varrho)}
\right\}^2.
\end{equation}

Balancing the two terms in
Equation~(\ref{eq:direct-alpha-tuning-upper-bound}) gives
\begin{equation}
1-\alpha
\asymp
\left(\frac1{NJ}+\frac1{J^2}\right)^{1/3}
\end{equation}
until $1-\alpha$ reaches order $N^{-1}$.  Written by regime, choices
attaining the resulting upper bounds are
\begin{equation}
\begin{cases}
1-\alpha\asymp J^{-2/3},&J\lesssim N,\\
1-\alpha\asymp(NJ)^{-1/3},&N\lesssim J\lesssim N^2,\\
1-\alpha\asymp N/J,&J\gtrsim N^2.
\end{cases}
\label{eq:direct-alpha-tuning-rate}
\end{equation}
and
\begin{align}
\E\left\|
\nabla_\theta\hat\ell^\alpha(\theta)
-\nabla_\theta\ell(\theta)
\right\|_2^2
&\lesssim
pG'(\theta)^2
\begin{cases}
N^2/J^{4/3},&J\lesssim N,\\
N^{4/3}/J^{2/3},&N\lesssim J\lesssim N^2,\\
N^2/J,&J\gtrsim N^2.
\end{cases}
\label{eq:direct-alpha-tuned-mse-rate}
\end{align}
In the last line, the endpoint $\alpha=1$ is already rate-optimal:
Equation~(\ref{eq:refined-dmop-one-lag-particle-error}) gives
\begin{equation}
pG'(\theta)^2
\left(\frac{N^2}{J}+\frac{N^3}{J^2}\right)
\lesssim
\frac{N^2pG'(\theta)^2}{J},
\qquad J\gtrsim N^2.
\end{equation}
Every $1-\alpha\lesssim J^{-1/2}$ also attains the same order: the
nonnegative finite lag sums in Equation~
(\ref{eq:verified-dmop-alpha-mse-finite-sum}) are nondecreasing in
$\alpha$ and hence are bounded by their values at $\alpha=1$, while
Equation~(\ref{eq:finite-population-bias-first-order-bound}) bounds the
population term by $CN^2(1-\alpha)^2\lesssim N^2/J$.

The explicit finite-sum right side is nevertheless strictly decreased
by moving slightly to the left of $\alpha=1$.  Indeed, for $N\geq2$,
\begin{align}
&\left.
\frac{\partial}{\partial\alpha}
\sum_{t=1}^{N}\sum_{r=0}^{t-1}\sum_{u=0}^{t-1}
\alpha^{r+u}\varrho^{|r-u|}
\right|_{\alpha=1}                                          \notag\\
&\qquad=
\sum_{t=1}^{N}\sum_{r=0}^{t-1}\sum_{u=0}^{t-1}
(r+u)\varrho^{|r-u|}
>0,
\label{eq:finite-particle-bound-endpoint-derivative}
\end{align}
and the other particle term is also strictly increasing because it is
the square of a sum of square roots of these positive increasing sums.
In contrast, the squared population term has derivative zero at
$\alpha=1$, because its unsquared sum vanishes there.

For completeness, the minimizer of the displayed finite upper bound can
be localized when $J\gg N^2$.  The $r=1$ population summand gives
\begin{align}
\left\{
\sum_{r=1}^{N-1}(N-r)(1-\alpha^r)\varrho^r
\right\}^2
&\geq
(N-1)^2\varrho^2(1-\alpha)^2.
\label{eq:finite-population-bound-localization}
\end{align}
At $\alpha=1$, the same right side has order
$N^2/J+N^3/J^2$.  Hence, if $\alpha$ minimizes that right side and
$J\gg N^2$, Equation~
(\ref{eq:finite-population-bound-localization}) implies
\begin{equation}
1-\alpha=O(J^{-1/2}),
\qquad
N(1-\alpha)=o(1).
\label{eq:finite-alpha-minimizer-localization}
\end{equation}
Uniformly in this localized interval, direct differentiation of the
finite sums gives
\begin{align}
-\frac{\partial}{\partial(1-\alpha)}
\{\text{two particle terms}\}
&\asymp\frac{N^3}{J}+\frac{N^4}{J^2},                         \notag\\
\frac{\partial}{\partial(1-\alpha)}
\{\text{squared population term}\}
&\asymp N^2(1-\alpha).
\label{eq:finite-alpha-local-derivative-orders}
\end{align}
The population curvature has order $N^2$, while the particle curvature
is $O(N^4/J)=o(N^2)$.  Thus the localized minimizer is interior and
Equation~(\ref{eq:finite-alpha-local-derivative-orders}) gives
\begin{equation}
1-\alpha\asymp
\frac{N^3/J+N^4/J^2}{N^2}
\asymp\frac NJ.
\label{eq:finite-alpha-high-particle-choice}
\end{equation}
The improvement over the endpoint is of lower order; both the interior
choice and $\alpha=1$ have the $N^2/J$ upper-bound rate.

In particular, when $N>J^3$ the first regime applies and the tuned
DMOP-$\alpha$ upper bound is $N^2/J^{4/3}$, not $N^2/J$.
Moreover, Equation~(\ref{eq:refined-dmop-one-lag-particle-error}) and
the pathwise bound in Equation~
(\ref{eq:dmop-one-pathwise-particle-error}) give at $\alpha=1$
\begin{equation}
pG'(\theta)^2
\min\left\{
N^2,\,
\frac{N^2}{J}+\frac{N^3}{J^2}
\right\}
\asymp N^2pG'(\theta)^2,
\qquad N>J^3.
\end{equation}
Within the regime $N>J^3$, the ratio of the two numerical upper-bound
expressions is $J^{4/3}$.  This is a regime-dependent comparison of upper
bounds, not a ratio of the actual MSEs.

The power $J^{-4/3}$ is likewise not a fixed-$N$ or superparametric
Monte Carlo rate.  It occurs only in the joint regime $J\lesssim N$,
where the $N^2/J^2$ quadratic particle-remainder term dominates the
$N/J$ particle-variance term before tuning.  For fixed $N$, increasing
$J$ eventually enters the last regime, where the displayed rate is the
ordinary $N^2/J$ particle rate.

Oscillation normalization is a separate issue.  If a particle theorem
is stated for $\operatorname{osc}(f)\leq1$ and is applied to a full
unnormalized score with oscillation of order $N$, its RMSE bound must be
multiplied by $N$, and its MSE bound by $N^2$; in particular, a norm-one
MSE bound of order $N^2/J$ would become $N^4/J$.  That rescaling is not
used in Lemma~\ref{lem:dmop-alpha-variance}, which bounds each $h_s$
before summing its lag covariances.  It also must not be applied again
to the quadratic asymptotic-variance lower bound of
\citet{poyiadjis11}, which is already stated for an unnormalized
additive functional.

This calculation gives rate-balancing choices for the displayed
fixed-observation norm upper bound, not the actual MSE.  The actual
population discount bias is the signed finite sum
\begin{equation}
\nabla_\theta\ell(\theta)-\nabla_\theta\ell^\alpha(\theta)
=
\sum_{n=1}^{N}\sum_{r=1}^{n-1}
(1-\alpha^r)\Delta_{n,r},
\end{equation}
whose first-order coefficient can cancel.  Consequently,
Equation~(\ref{eq:direct-alpha-tuned-mse-rate}) consists only of upper
bounds.  It neither identifies the actual MSE rates nor proves equality
with the DMOP-$1$ endpoint.  A comparison with the quadratic lower bound
of \citet{poyiadjis11} additionally requires the nondegeneracy conditions
of that result and the identification of its likelihood-ratio estimator
with the DMOP-$1$ estimator in the model under study.

\end{remark}

\iffalse
\begin{remark}[The location--log-weight-gradient pair and a sharper rate]
At $\theta=\phi$, the gradient of the log weight obeys the existing
recursions
\begin{align}
\left.\partial_{\theta_i}\log w_{n,j}^{P,\theta}
\right|_{\theta=\phi}
&=
\alpha
\left.\partial_{\theta_i}\log w_{n-1,j}^{F,\theta}
\right|_{\theta=\phi},                                     \notag\\
\left.\partial_{\theta_i}\log w_{n,j}^{F,\theta}
\right|_{\theta=\phi}
&=
\left.\partial_{\theta_i}\log w_{n,k_j}^{P,\theta}
\right|_{\theta=\phi}
+h_{n,i}(x_{n,k_j}^{P,\phi}).
\label{eq:separate-weight-gradient-recursion}
\end{align}
The current object is therefore the pair consisting of the particle
location and the gradient of its log weight.  Only the location is
required to mix in total variation under Assumption~
{\refAssumpBoundedProcess}; the affine recursion for the second component
is treated directly.  Two different common shifts in the second component
need not mix in total variation, but a common shift cancels from the score
difference in Equation~
(\ref{eq:conditional-score-weight-difference}).

Equations~(\ref{eq:dmop-direct-location-coupling})--
(\ref{eq:dmop-direct-pair-full-score-continuation-bound}) give the precise
calculation.  A previously accumulated difference is multiplied by
$\alpha$ at each step.  A new difference in $h_{t+q}(X_{t+q})$ occurs
only while the coupled locations differ, an event having probability at
most $\varrho^q$.  Finally,
\begin{equation}
(1-\alpha)\sum_{r=q}^{L-1}\alpha^{r-q}+\alpha^{L-q}=1
\end{equation}
shows that full-score summation by parts counts each such difference once,
rather than adding another factor $(1-\alpha)^{-1}$.  Consequently, the
memory cost is $(1-\alpha)^{-1}$ in local root mean square and
$(1-\alpha)^{-2}$ in the displayed MSE.  The factors
$(1-\alpha\varrho)^{-1}$ in Equations~
(\ref{eq:dmop-direct-past-score-coefficient-sum}) and
(\ref{eq:dmop-direct-future-score-coefficient-sum}) retain the interaction
between location mixing and discounting; there is no second independent
mixing penalty.

The $N^2/\{J^2(1-\alpha)^2\}$ term in
Equation~(\ref{eq:refined-dmop-alpha-direct-particle-error}) comes from
quadratic self-normalized-ratio remainders.  It is a direct-MSE term and
may represent coherent $J^{-1}$ particle bias across observation times;
it is not an additional oscillation normalization of the unnormalized
score.  A sharper general finite-$J$ variance bound with the squared-weight
behavior of the constant-weight calculation would follow from the centered
estimate
\begin{align}
&\left\|
\sum_{r=0}^{n-1}\alpha^r
\left\{\left(\Delta_{n,r}^J\right)_i-
\E\left(\Delta_{n,r}^J\right)_i\right\}
\right\|_{L^4}                                               \notag\\
&\qquad\leq
\frac{CG'(\theta)}{\sqrt J}
\left\{
\sum_{r=0}^{n-1}\sum_{s=0}^{n-1}
\alpha^{r+s}\varrho^{|r-s|}
\right\}^{1/2}
\label{eq:needed-centered-genealogy-estimate}
\end{align}
together with its joint-time analogue.  If those estimates hold, direct
covariance summation gives
\begin{align}
\Var\left\{\nabla_\theta\hat\ell^\alpha(\theta)\right\}
&\lesssim
\frac{pG'(\theta)^2}{J}
\sum_{n=1}^{N}
\sum_{r=0}^{n-1}\sum_{s=0}^{n-1}
\alpha^{r+s}\varrho^{|r-s|}.
\label{eq:conditional-squared-weight-variance}
\end{align}
For $0\leq\alpha<1$, every inner double sum satisfies
\begin{align}
&\sum_{r=0}^{n-1}\sum_{s=0}^{n-1}
\alpha^{r+s}\varrho^{|r-s|}                                \notag\\
&\quad=
\sum_{r=0}^{n-1}\alpha^{2r}
+2\sum_{d=1}^{n-1}(\alpha\varrho)^d
\sum_{r=0}^{n-1-d}\alpha^{2r}                              \notag\\
&\quad\leq
\frac{1+\alpha\varrho}{1-\alpha\varrho}
\sum_{r=0}^{n-1}\alpha^{2r}.
\label{eq:conditional-squared-weight-geometric-sum}
\end{align}
Reversing the remaining finite sums gives
\begin{align}
\sum_{n=1}^{N}\sum_{r=0}^{n-1}\alpha^{2r}
&=
\sum_{r=0}^{N-1}(N-r)\alpha^{2r}                           \notag\\
&=
\begin{cases}
\dfrac{N-(N+1)\alpha^2+\alpha^{2N+2}}
{(1-\alpha^2)^2},&0\leq\alpha<1,\\[8pt]
\dfrac{N(N+1)}2,&\alpha=1.
\end{cases}
\label{eq:conditional-squared-weight-finite-sum}
\end{align}
Thus this further refinement would have order
$N/\{J(1-\alpha^2)\}$ before horizon saturation and $N^2/J$ at
$\alpha=1$.  Equations~
(\ref{eq:needed-centered-genealogy-estimate})--
(\ref{eq:conditional-squared-weight-variance}) are not claimed as
consequences of Assumptions {\refAssumpBoundedProcess}--
{\refAssumpDifferentiableSimulator}: they are the precise
variance-sensitive two- and four-lineage estimates still requiring a
proof.  They are not needed for the direct bound in Lemma~
\ref{lem:dmop-alpha-variance}.  The current-cloud oscillation result of
\citet{karjalainen23} does not supply the joint-lineage estimate, and the
first-order path-space estimates of \citet{chanDelMoral18} do not by
themselves control the required joint-time centered covariance.
\end{remark}
\fi

\iffalse
\begin{remark}[Fixed-lag audit]
For $0\leq k<N$, the finite telescope for the fixed-lag score is
\begin{equation}
\sum_{s=1}^{N-k-1}m(\xi_{s+k})(h_s)
+m(\xi_N)\left(\sum_{s=N-k}^{N}h_s\right).
\label{eq:verified-fixed-lag-telescope}
\end{equation}
The corresponding population truncation error is
\begin{equation}
\sum_{s=1}^{N-k-1}\sum_{u=s+k+1}^{N}\Delta_{u,u-s}.
\end{equation}
Lemma~\ref{lem:derivative-measure-forgetting} and the triangle inequality
therefore give
\begin{equation}
\left\|\operatorname{Bias}^{\rm pop}_k\right\|_2
\leq
C(N-k-1)\sqrt p\,G'(\theta)
\frac{\varrho^{k+1}}{1-\varrho}.
\label{eq:verified-fixed-lag-population-bias}
\end{equation}
The sum is empty, and the population truncation bias is exactly zero, at
$k=N-1$.

The former fixed-lag particle-variance proof is not valid: it applied a
current-filter $L^4$ estimate to the historical quantity
$m(\xi_{s+k})(h_s)$ with a constant independent of its genealogical age.
In the constant-weight model used in Lemma~
\ref{lem:dmop-alpha-variance}, direct use of Equation~
(\ref{eq:neutral-selection-variance-recursion}) gives instead
\begin{align}
&\Var\left\{
\sum_{s=1}^{N-k-1}m(\xi_{s+k})(h_s)
+m(\xi_N)\left(\sum_{s=N-k}^{N}h_s\right)
\right\}                                                    \notag\\
&\quad=
\sigma^2\left[
(N-k-1)\{1-(1-J^{-1})^{k+2}\}
+\sum_{a=0}^{k}\{1-(1-J^{-1})^{a+2}\}
\right].
\label{eq:neutral-fixed-lag-exact-variance}
\end{align}
For $1\ll k\ll\min(N,J)$ this has order $N(k+1)/J$; at
$k=N-1$ it has order $N^2/J$ before coalescence saturation.  This exact
calculation disproves the earlier $\{N+(k+1)^2\}/J$ upper bound.  A general
upper bound of order $N(k+1)/J$ requires the fixed-lag version of
Equation~(\ref{eq:needed-centered-genealogy-estimate}) and is not
asserted here.

The same distinction applies to particle bias.  The active proof of
Lemma~\ref{lem:lagwise-weak-error} establishes a uniform $J^{-1}$ bias
for a current filtering functional, but its one-sided forward-semigroup
argument does not establish the same bound for the historical
$h_s$ in Equation~(\ref{eq:verified-fixed-lag-telescope}).  Generic
path-space weak-error results give the conservative full-score order
$N(k+1)/J$.  A uniform $N/J$ result may still follow from a two-sided
backward-smoothing argument, but it is not used in the verified theorem
above.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[Why geometric discounting does not have fixed-lag bias]
The exact finite-horizon scalar sum in the upper bound of Theorem~
\ref{thm:refined-dmop-alpha-mse} is
\begin{equation}
\sum_{r=1}^{N-1}(N-r)(1-\alpha^r)\varrho^r.
\label{eq:exact-full-population-discount-sum}
\end{equation}
The simpler bound in Lemma~\ref{lem:dmop-population-discount-bias}
uses
\begin{align}
\sum_{r=1}^{\infty}(1-\alpha^r)\varrho^r
&=
\frac{\varrho(1-\alpha)}
{(1-\varrho)(1-\alpha\varrho)}.
\label{eq:infinite-population-discount-sum}
\end{align}
This identity already contains the location-forgetting factor
$\varrho^r$.  Its order in $1-\alpha$ is not caused by discarding that
factor.

Indeed, for $0\leq\alpha<1$ and every $r\geq1$,
\begin{align}
\alpha^r
&=\sum_{k=r}^{\infty}(1-\alpha)\alpha^k,                  \notag\\
1-\alpha^r
&=\sum_{k=0}^{r-1}(1-\alpha)\alpha^k.
\label{eq:geometric-discount-cutoff-identity}
\end{align}
Therefore, by Tonelli's theorem for nonnegative series,
\begin{align}
\sum_{r=1}^{\infty}(1-\alpha^r)\varrho^r
&=
\sum_{r=1}^{\infty}\sum_{k=0}^{r-1}
(1-\alpha)\alpha^k\varrho^r                              \notag\\
&=
\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}(1-\alpha)\alpha^k
\sum_{r=k+1}^{\infty}\varrho^r                           \notag\\
&=
\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}(1-\alpha)\alpha^k
\frac{\varrho^{k+1}}{1-\varrho}                          \notag\\
&=
\frac{\varrho(1-\alpha)}
{(1-\varrho)(1-\alpha\varrho)}.
\label{eq:geometric-mixture-of-fixed-lag-bounds}
\end{align}
Thus exponential discounting is a geometric mixture of fixed-lag
cutoffs.  The mixing weights $(1-\alpha)\alpha^k$ have mean
$\alpha/(1-\alpha)$, but the cutoff is zero with probability
$1-\alpha$.  This mass on short cutoffs is why the mixture in Equation~
(\ref{eq:geometric-mixture-of-fixed-lag-bounds}) is of order
$1-\alpha$ rather than $\varrho^{\alpha/(1-\alpha)}$.  Since
\begin{equation}
\frac{\alpha}{1-\alpha}
=\frac{1}{1-\alpha}-1,
\end{equation}
using $1/(1-\alpha)$ instead as the effective horizon changes the proposed
exponential rate only by the fixed factor $\varrho$.

The first-order behavior is also visible without taking norms.  Since all
sums are finite and
\begin{equation}
\frac{1-\alpha^r}{1-\alpha}
=\sum_{q=0}^{r-1}\alpha^q\longrightarrow r
\qquad\text{as }\alpha\uparrow1,
\end{equation}
Equation~(\ref{eq:population-discount-lag-difference}) gives
\begin{align}
&\lim_{\alpha\uparrow1}
\frac{\nabla_\theta\ell(\theta)
-\nabla_\theta\ell^\alpha(\theta)}{1-\alpha}
\notag\\
&\qquad=
\sum_{n=1}^{N}\sum_{r=1}^{n-1}r\Delta_{n,r}.
\label{eq:first-order-population-discount-bias}
\end{align}
The assumptions do not force the vector on the right to vanish.
For example, take $N=2$, state space $\{0,1\}$,
\begin{align}
\Pr(X_1=1)&=\frac12,
&
\Pr(X_2=X_1\mid X_1)&=\frac34,
\end{align}
observe $Y_1=Y_2=1$, and, in a neighborhood of $\theta=\phi=0$, let
\begin{align}
\Pr_\theta(Y_1=1\mid X_1=x)
&=\frac12+\theta\left(x-\frac12\right),                  \notag\\
\Pr_\theta(Y_2=1\mid X_2=x)
&=\frac14+\frac{x}{2}.
\label{eq:two-state-first-order-bias-example}
\end{align}
The probabilities of the complementary observations are one minus the
displayed probabilities.  On $|\theta|\leq1/2$, all transition and
measurement probabilities lie between $1/4$ and $3/4$, and the required
derivatives are bounded.  At $\theta=0$,
\begin{align}
h_1(x)&=2x-1,
&h_2(x)&=0,                                                \notag\\
\rho_1(h_1)&=0,
&\rho_2(h_1)&=\frac14.
\end{align}
Indeed,
\begin{align}
\Pr(Y_2=1\mid X_1=1)&=\frac58,
&
\Pr(Y_2=1\mid X_1=0)&=\frac38,
\end{align}
so $\Pr(X_1=1\mid Y_1=Y_2=1)=5/8$.  Consequently,
\begin{equation}
\nabla_\theta\ell(0)-\nabla_\theta\ell^\alpha(0)
=(1-\alpha)\Delta_{2,1}
=\frac{1-\alpha}{4}.
\label{eq:two-state-first-order-bias-value}
\end{equation}
Hence no bound of order
$C\varrho^{\alpha/(1-\alpha)}$, with $C$ independent of $\alpha$, can
hold for all $\alpha$ sufficiently close to one under the present
assumptions.  Particular models may nevertheless have a vanishing
first-order coefficient in Equation~
(\ref{eq:first-order-population-discount-bias}).

The contraction of the location--log-weight-gradient pair does not change
this conclusion.  It compares two initial conditions under the same
discounted recursion.  By contrast, write $w_n^\alpha$ and $w_n^1$ only
in the next display for the gradients of the corresponding log weights,
evaluated along the same location path.  Comparing the discounted and
undiscounted recursions gives at every time
\begin{equation}
w_n^\alpha-w_n^1
=\alpha(w_{n-1}^\alpha-w_{n-1}^1)
-(1-\alpha)w_{n-1}^1,
\label{eq:discount-comparison-persistent-forcing}
\end{equation}
so the discount introduces a new forcing term at every step.

The finite-polynomial structure makes the necessary cancellation precise.
The binomial theorem and the exact signed full-score representation give
\begin{align}
&\nabla_\theta\ell(\theta)-\nabla_\theta\ell^\alpha(\theta)
                                                               \notag\\
&\quad=
\sum_{q=1}^{N-1}(-1)^{q+1}(1-\alpha)^q
\sum_{n=1}^{N}\sum_{r=q}^{n-1}
{r\choose q}\Delta_{n,r}.
\label{eq:population-discount-finite-polynomial}
\end{align}
Vanishing of the first signed sum in Equation~
(\ref{eq:population-discount-finite-polynomial}) improves the order from
$1-\alpha$ to $(1-\alpha)^2$; successively higher polynomial orders require
the corresponding successive signed sums to vanish.  For every $c>0$,
$\varrho^{c/(1-\alpha)}=o\{(1-\alpha)^q\}$ for every fixed $q$ as
$\alpha\uparrow1$.  Hence, for fixed $N$, a bound of order
$\varrho^{c/(1-\alpha)}$ forces every coefficient in Equation~
(\ref{eq:population-discount-finite-polynomial}) to vanish.  The left side
is then identically zero as a polynomial in $\alpha$.  Equivalently, linear
independence of the powers of $\alpha$ gives
\begin{equation}
\sum_{n=r+1}^{N}\Delta_{n,r}=0,
\qquad 1\leq r<N.
\label{eq:all-order-population-cancellation}
\end{equation}
Thus exponential decay in the mean geometric memory would assume exact
all-order cancellation, rather than follow from stronger smoothness or
mixing alone.

An exact summation-by-parts identity explains both the bound and the useful
regime of the geometric rule.  For each original time $s$, its contribution
to the DMOP-$\alpha$ full score is
\begin{align}
&\rho_s(h_s)
+\sum_{q=1}^{N-s}\alpha^q
\{\rho_{s+q}(h_s)-\rho_{s+q-1}(h_s)\}                       \notag\\
&\quad=
(1-\alpha)\sum_{q=0}^{N-s-1}\alpha^q\rho_{s+q}(h_s)
+\alpha^{N-s}\rho_N(h_s).
\label{eq:geometric-partial-smoother-average}
\end{align}
Its exact-score contribution is $\rho_N(h_s)$, so their difference is
\begin{equation}
(1-\alpha)\sum_{q=0}^{N-s-1}\alpha^q
\{\rho_N(h_s)-\rho_{s+q}(h_s)\}.
\label{eq:geometric-partial-smoother-error}
\end{equation}
Thus geometric discounting averages partial smoothers with a geometrically
distributed smoothing time capped at $N-s$, whereas fixed lag uses one
smoothing time.

For a quantitative comparison, suppose $0\leq\alpha<1$, take
$k=\lfloor\alpha/(1-\alpha)\rfloor<N$, and compare the uncapped scalar
upper bounds.  Here $k$ is the integer part of the mean geometric time.
The ratio of the geometric scalar upper bound to the corresponding fixed-lag
scalar upper bound is
\begin{align}
\frac{
\dfrac{\varrho(1-\alpha)}
{(1-\varrho)(1-\alpha\varrho)}
}{
\dfrac{\varrho^{k+1}}{1-\varrho}
}
&=
\frac{(1-\alpha)\varrho^{-k}}{1-\alpha\varrho}
\leq
\varrho^{-k}.
\label{eq:mixing-calibrated-geometric-fixed-lag-comparison}
\end{align}
The same ratio also satisfies
\begin{align}
\frac{(1-\alpha)\varrho^{-k}}{1-\alpha\varrho}
&=
\sum_{q=0}^{\infty}(1-\alpha)\alpha^q\varrho^{q-k}          \notag\\
&\geq
\sum_{q=0}^{k}(1-\alpha)\alpha^q
=1-\alpha^{k+1}                                             \notag\\
&>
1-\left(\frac{k+1}{k+2}\right)^{k+1}
\geq\frac12.
\label{eq:mixing-calibrated-geometric-fixed-lag-lower-comparison}
\end{align}
Indeed, $k\leq\alpha/(1-\alpha)<k+1$ implies
$\alpha<(k+1)/(k+2)$, and
$\{(k+1)/(k+2)\}^{k+1}\leq1/2$.  Consequently the two uncapped scalar
upper bounds are comparable up to a fixed factor whenever
$k|\log\varrho|$ is bounded, namely when the discount memory spans at most
a fixed number of mixing e-folding times.  This gives a finite-regime
explanation consistent with comparable empirical behavior.  The asymptotic
gap appears when $\alpha\uparrow1$ while $\varrho$ is fixed, so that the
discount memory spans arbitrarily many mixing times.
\end{remark}
\fi

\begin{remark}[Fixed lag and geometric memory]
For $0\leq k<N$, the fixed-lag score and its population truncation error
are, respectively,
\begin{align}
&\sum_{s=1}^{N-k-1}m(\xi_{s+k})(h_s)
+m(\xi_N)\left(\sum_{s=N-k}^{N}h_s\right),
\label{eq:verified-fixed-lag-telescope}
\\
&\sum_{s=1}^{N-k-1}\sum_{u=s+k+1}^{N}\Delta_{u,u-s}.
\label{eq:verified-fixed-lag-population-error}
\end{align}
Lemma~\ref{lem:derivative-measure-forgetting} therefore gives
\begin{equation}
\left\|\operatorname{Bias}^{\rm pop}_k\right\|_2
\leq
C(N-k-1)\sqrt p\,G'(\theta)\frac{\varrho^{k+1}}{1-\varrho}.
\label{eq:verified-fixed-lag-population-bias}
\end{equation}

The genealogy calculation in Lemma~\ref{lem:dmop-alpha-variance} also
gives the general particle bound
\begin{align}
&\max\Bigg\{
\E\left\|
\sum_{n=1}^{N}\sum_{r=0}^{\min(k,n-1)}
\left(\Delta_{n,r}^J-\Delta_{n,r}\right)
\right\|_2^2,                                                \notag\\
&\hspace{27mm}
\Var\left(
\sum_{n=1}^{N}\sum_{r=0}^{\min(k,n-1)}
\Delta_{n,r}^J
\right)
\Bigg\}                                                       \notag\\
&\qquad\lesssim
pG'(\theta)^2
\left\{
\frac{N(k+1)}J+\frac{N^2(k+1)}{J^2}
\right\}.
\label{eq:verified-fixed-lag-particle-bound}
\end{align}
Here is the cutoff-specific step needed to obtain this consequence.
For $q\in\{2,4\}$, the backward-genealogy calculation in Step 5 of
Lemma~\ref{lem:dmop-alpha-variance} remains valid when the historical
sum is restricted to $a\leq s\leq b$.  For either conditional path law
$\mu$ appearing there, it gives
\begin{align}
&\E\,\mu\left[
\left|
\sum_{s=a}^{b}h_{s,i}
-\mu\left(\sum_{s=a}^{b}h_{s,i}\right)
\right|^q
\right]                                                       \notag\\
&\quad\leq
CG'(\theta)^q
\left\{
\sum_{r=0}^{b-a}\sum_{u=0}^{b-a}
\varrho^{|r-u|}
\right\}^{q/2}                                                \notag\\
&\quad\leq
CG'(\theta)^q(b-a+1)^{q/2},
\label{eq:fixed-lag-block-moments}
\end{align}
because, for every integer $L\geq1$,
\begin{align}
\sum_{r=0}^{L-1}\sum_{u=0}^{L-1}\varrho^{|r-u|}
&=
L+2\sum_{d=1}^{L-1}(L-d)\varrho^d                            \notag\\
&\leq
L\frac{1+\varrho}{1-\varrho}.
\label{eq:fixed-lag-block-double-sum}
\end{align}

Let $R_{n,i}^{J,k}$, $\Gamma_{t,i}^{J,k}$, and $B_{t,i}^{J,k}$
denote, respectively, the terminal-resampling, first-order local, and
quadratic local terms obtained from Equations~
(\ref{eq:dmop-direct-centered-local-field-definition})--
(\ref{eq:dmop-direct-local-error-decomposition}) after every coefficient
$\alpha^{n-s}$ is replaced by
$\mathbf 1\{0\leq n-s\leq k\}$.  Coefficient-by-coefficient
substitution in that exact decomposition gives
\begin{align}
&\sum_{n=1}^{N}\sum_{r=0}^{\min(k,n-1)}
\left\{(\Delta_{n,r}^J)_i-(\Delta_{n,r})_i\right\}            \notag\\
&\qquad=
\sum_{n=1}^{N}R_{n,i}^{J,k}
+\sum_{t=1}^{N}\Gamma_{t,i}^{J,k}
+\sum_{t=1}^{N}B_{t,i}^{J,k}.
\label{eq:fixed-lag-exact-local-decomposition}
\end{align}

For terminal resampling at time $n$, the retained historical block is
$\sum_{s=\max(1,n-k)}^nh_{s,i}$ and has at most $k+1$ terms.  The
conditional empirical second-moment identity and Equation~
(\ref{eq:fixed-lag-block-moments}) therefore give
\begin{equation}
\E\left[
\left.
\left|
m(\xi_n)\left(\sum_{s=\max(1,n-k)}^nh_{s,i}\right)
-\Psi_n\{m(\xi_n^P)\}
\left(\sum_{s=\max(1,n-k)}^nh_{s,i}\right)
\right|^2
\right|\xi_n^P
\right]
\leq\frac{CG'(\theta)^2(k+1)}J.
\label{eq:fixed-lag-terminal-local-variance}
\end{equation}
The expression inside the absolute value is $R_{n,i}^{J,k}$.
These terminal terms are martingale differences in $n$.  At a local
particle update time $t$, put $d=n-t$.  If $d>k$, the past block is
empty.  If $0\leq d\leq k$, linearity of the continuation functional
gives
\begin{align}
&\sum_{s=\max(1,n-k)}^{t}
D_{\mu_t^J}C_{t,n}^{(s)}
=
D_{\mu_t^J}C_{t,n}^{
\left(\sum_{s=\max(1,n-k)}^{t}h_{s,i}\right)}.
\label{eq:fixed-lag-local-past-block}
\end{align}
This block has at most $k-d+1$ terms.  Equations~
(\ref{eq:dmop-local-past-field-l2})--
(\ref{eq:dmop-local-past-field-l4}) and
(\ref{eq:fixed-lag-block-moments}) consequently give, for $q=2,4$, the
corresponding norm bound
\begin{equation}
CG'(\theta)\varrho^d(k-d+1)^{1/2}.
\label{eq:fixed-lag-local-past-field-bound}
\end{equation}
Summing over the possible values of $d$ yields
\begin{align}
\sum_{d=0}^{k}\varrho^d(k-d+1)^{1/2}
&\leq
(k+1)^{1/2}\sum_{d=0}^{\infty}\varrho^d
=\frac{(k+1)^{1/2}}{1-\varrho}.
\label{eq:fixed-lag-local-past-field-sum}
\end{align}
The contributions created after time $t$ and the current-predictor
contributions do not carry the historical block.  Their coefficient
sums satisfy
\begin{align}
\sum_{s=t+1}^{N}\sum_{n=s}^{\min(N,s+k)}
\varrho^{n-t}
&\leq
\sum_{a=1}^{\infty}\sum_{d=0}^{k}\varrho^{a+d}
\leq\frac{\varrho}{(1-\varrho)^2},                           \notag\\
\sum_{n=t}^{N}\varrho^{n-t}
&\leq\frac1{1-\varrho}.
\label{eq:fixed-lag-future-field-sums}
\end{align}
Thus the centered local field at time $t$ has second moment at most
$CG'(\theta)^2(k+1)/J$; explicitly,
\begin{equation}
\E(\Gamma_{t,i}^{J,k})^2
\leq\frac{CG'(\theta)^2(k+1)}J.
\label{eq:fixed-lag-centered-local-variance}
\end{equation}
Repeating the paired-ratio
fourth-moment calculation in Equations~
(\ref{eq:dmop-direct-empirical-product-fourth-moment})--
(\ref{eq:dmop-direct-past-remainder-lag-bound}), with Equation~
(\ref{eq:fixed-lag-local-past-field-sum}) in place of the geometric
discount sum, gives
\begin{equation}
\|B_{t,i}^{J,k}\|_{L^2}
\leq\frac{CG'(\theta)(k+1)^{1/2}}J.
\label{eq:fixed-lag-local-remainder-bound}
\end{equation}
Martingale orthogonality for the terminal and centered local terms, and
Minkowski's inequality for the remainders, now give
\begin{align}
&\E\left|
\sum_{n=1}^{N}\sum_{r=0}^{\min(k,n-1)}
\left\{(\Delta_{n,r}^J)_i-(\Delta_{n,r})_i\right\}
\right|^2                                                     \notag\\
&\quad\leq
3\E\left|\sum_{n=1}^{N}R_{n,i}^{J,k}\right|^2
+3\E\left|\sum_{t=1}^{N}\Gamma_{t,i}^{J,k}\right|^2
+3\left\|\sum_{t=1}^{N}B_{t,i}^{J,k}\right\|_{L^2}^2         \notag\\
&\quad=
3\sum_{n=1}^{N}\E(R_{n,i}^{J,k})^2
+3\sum_{t=1}^{N}\E(\Gamma_{t,i}^{J,k})^2
+3\left\|\sum_{t=1}^{N}B_{t,i}^{J,k}\right\|_{L^2}^2         \notag\\
&\quad\leq
\frac{CNG'(\theta)^2(k+1)}J
+3\left\{
\sum_{t=1}^{N}
\frac{CG'(\theta)(k+1)^{1/2}}J
\right\}^2                                                    \notag\\
&\quad\leq
CG'(\theta)^2
\left\{\frac{N(k+1)}J+\frac{N^2(k+1)}{J^2}\right\}.
\label{eq:fixed-lag-coordinate-particle-bound}
\end{align}
Summing over $i=1,\ldots,p$ proves Equation~
(\ref{eq:verified-fixed-lag-particle-bound}); its variance assertion
follows because the truncated population target is deterministic.
Combining Equations~(\ref{eq:verified-fixed-lag-population-bias}) and
(\ref{eq:verified-fixed-lag-particle-bound}) gives
\begin{align}
&\E\left\|
\sum_{n=1}^{N}\sum_{r=0}^{\min(k,n-1)}
\Delta_{n,r}^J-\nabla_\theta\ell(\theta)
\right\|_2^2                                                 \notag\\
&\quad\lesssim
pG'(\theta)^2
\left\{
\frac{N(k+1)}J+\frac{N^2(k+1)}{J^2}
+\frac{N^2\varrho^{2(k+1)}}{(1-\varrho)^2}
\right\}.
\label{eq:verified-fixed-lag-mse}
\end{align}

For comparison, in the neutral model of Equation~
(\ref{eq:neutral-dmop-alpha-exact-variance}), direct multinomial
calculation gives
\begin{align}
&\Var\left\{
\sum_{s=1}^{N-k-1}m(\xi_{s+k})(h_s)
+m(\xi_N)\left(\sum_{s=N-k}^{N}h_s\right)
\right\}                                                    \notag\\
&\quad=
\sigma^2\left[
(N-k-1)\{1-(1-J^{-1})^{k+2}\}
+\sum_{a=0}^{k}\{1-(1-J^{-1})^{a+2}\}
\right].
\label{eq:verified-fixed-lag-neutral-variance}
\end{align}
This has order $N(k+1)/J$ when
$1\ll k\ll\min(N,J)$, in agreement with the leading term of
Equation~(\ref{eq:verified-fixed-lag-particle-bound}).

The scalar geometric-discount bias bound is an exact geometric mixture of
the fixed-lag scalar bounds:
\begin{align}
\sum_{r=1}^{\infty}(1-\alpha^r)\varrho^r
&=
\sum_{q=0}^{\infty}(1-\alpha)\alpha^q
\frac{\varrho^{q+1}}{1-\varrho}                            \notag\\
&=
\frac{\varrho(1-\alpha)}
{(1-\varrho)(1-\alpha\varrho)}.
\label{eq:geometric-mixture-of-fixed-lag-bounds}
\end{align}
If $k=\lfloor\alpha/(1-\alpha)\rfloor<N$, the ratio of this bound to
$\varrho^{k+1}/(1-\varrho)$ satisfies
\begin{equation}
\frac12
<
\frac{(1-\alpha)\varrho^{-k}}{1-\alpha\varrho}
\leq
\varrho^{-k}.
\label{eq:mixing-calibrated-geometric-fixed-lag-comparison}
\end{equation}
Thus the two scalar bounds are within fixed factors when
$k|\log\varrho|=O(1)$.  This comparison does not turn geometric
discounting into a hard cutoff: from the exact signed representation,
\begin{equation}
\lim_{\alpha\uparrow1}
\frac{\nabla_\theta\ell(\theta)
-\nabla_\theta\ell^\alpha(\theta)}{1-\alpha}
=
\sum_{n=1}^{N}\sum_{r=1}^{n-1}r\Delta_{n,r},
\label{eq:first-order-population-discount-bias}
\end{equation}
and the assumptions do not force the right side to vanish.  Hence the
generic geometric-discount population bias is first order in
$1-\alpha$, even though its coefficient contains the forgetting powers
$\varrho^r$.
\end{remark}

\iffalse
\bigskip
\noindent{\rmfamily\bfseries DMOP-$\alpha$ as an estimating equation}\par
\medskip

The preceding MSE results condition on a fixed realized data set and
compare DMOP-$\alpha$ with its realized exact likelihood score.  That is
not the only criterion relevant to parameter estimation.  We now take
expectation under the original POMP law at parameter $\theta_0$:
\begin{align}
X_0&\sim f_{X_0}(\mathord\cdot;\theta_0),                    \notag\\
X_n\mid X_{n-1}
&\sim f_{X_n\mid X_{n-1}}(\mathord\cdot\mid X_{n-1};\theta_0),
                                                                    \notag\\
Y_n\mid X_n
&\sim f_{Y_n\mid X_n}(\mathord\cdot\mid X_n;\theta_0).
\label{eq:original-pomp-data-generating-law}
\end{align}
Thus ``taking expectation over the observations'' means integrating over
the measurement density already specified by the POMP, rather than
conditioning on the realized values $y_{1:N}^*$.  It introduces no second
observation model.  We evaluate the population recursion at
$\theta=\phi=\theta_0$.

\begin{proposition}[Conditional unbiasedness]
\label{prop:dmop-alpha-unbiased-estimating-equation}
Suppose the differentiation and domination conditions used in
Equation~(\ref{eq:finite-horizon-differentiation-under-expectation}) hold
also when integrating over the next observation. Then, for every fixed
$\alpha\in[0,1]$,
\begin{equation}
\E_{\theta_0}\left\{
\nabla_\theta\ell_n^\alpha(\theta_0)
\mid Y_{1:n-1}
\right\}
=0.
\label{eq:dmop-alpha-conditional-unbiasedness}
\end{equation}
Consequently,
\begin{equation}
\E_{\theta_0}\left\{
\sum_{n=1}^{N}\nabla_\theta\ell_n^\alpha(\theta_0)
\right\}
=0.
\label{eq:dmop-alpha-unbiased-estimating-equation}
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}[Proof.]
For $1\leq r<n$, the definitions of $\rho_n$ and $h_{n-r}$ give
\begin{align}
\rho_n(h_{n-r})
&=
\E_{\theta_0}(h_{n-r}\mid Y_{1:n}),                         \notag\\
\rho_{n-1}(h_{n-r})
&=
\E_{\theta_0}(h_{n-r}\mid Y_{1:n-1}).
\label{eq:rho-as-observation-conditional-expectation}
\end{align}
Apply conditional expectation given $Y_{1:n-1}$ to the definition of
$\Delta_{n,r}$ in Equation~(\ref{eq:lag-coefficient-definitions}).  The
tower property and Equation~
(\ref{eq:rho-as-observation-conditional-expectation}) give
\begin{align}
\E_{\theta_0}(\Delta_{n,r}\mid Y_{1:n-1})
&=
\E_{\theta_0}\left[
\E_{\theta_0}(h_{n-r}\mid Y_{1:n})
\mid Y_{1:n-1}
\right]                                                       \notag\\
&\qquad-
\E_{\theta_0}(h_{n-r}\mid Y_{1:n-1})                       \notag\\
&=
\E_{\theta_0}(h_{n-r}\mid Y_{1:n-1})
-\E_{\theta_0}(h_{n-r}\mid Y_{1:n-1})                       \notag\\
&=0.
\label{eq:positive-lag-conditional-mean-zero}
\end{align}

It remains to treat $r=0$.  Conditional on $Y_{1:n-1}$, the simulated
$X_n^{\theta_0}$ and its gradient, the next observation has density
$f_{Y_n\mid X_n}(y\mid X_n^{\theta_0};\theta_0)$.  The derivative in
Equation~(\ref{eq:actual-path-score}) acts on both the parameter argument
and the differentiable state argument.  Differentiation under the
observation integral therefore gives
\begin{align}
&\E_{\theta_0}
\left(
h_n
\mid Y_{1:n-1},X_n^{\theta_0},\nabla_\theta X_n^{\theta_0}
\right)                                                       \notag\\
&\quad=
\int
f_{Y_n\mid X_n}(y\mid X_n^{\theta_0};\theta_0)
\left.
\nabla_\theta\log
f_{Y_n\mid X_n}(y\mid X_n^\theta;\theta)
\right|_{\theta=\theta_0}
\,dy                                                          \notag\\
&\quad=
\left.
\nabla_\theta
\int f_{Y_n\mid X_n}(y\mid X_n^\theta;\theta)\,dy
\right|_{\theta=\theta_0}                                   \notag\\
&\quad=
\nabla_\theta 1
=0.
\label{eq:current-score-observation-integral-zero}
\end{align}
Taking conditional expectations of Equation~
(\ref{eq:current-score-observation-integral-zero}) given
$Y_{1:n-1}$ and using the tower property yield
\begin{align}
\rho_n(h_n)
&=
\E_{\theta_0}(h_n\mid Y_{1:n}),                              \notag\\
\E_{\theta_0}(\Delta_{n,0}\mid Y_{1:n-1})
&=
\E_{\theta_0}\{\rho_n(h_n)\mid Y_{1:n-1}\}                 \notag\\
&=
\E_{\theta_0}(h_n\mid Y_{1:n-1})                            \notag\\
&=0.
\label{eq:zero-lag-conditional-mean-zero}
\end{align}
Finally, Equation~
(\ref{eq:population-conditional-score-path-decomposition}) and the
finiteness of the lag sum give
\begin{align}
\E_{\theta_0}
\{\nabla_\theta\ell_n^\alpha(\theta_0)\mid Y_{1:n-1}\}
&=
\sum_{r=0}^{n-1}\alpha^r
\E_{\theta_0}(\Delta_{n,r}\mid Y_{1:n-1})                  \notag\\
&=0
\end{align}
by Equations~(\ref{eq:positive-lag-conditional-mean-zero}) and
(\ref{eq:zero-lag-conditional-mean-zero}).  This proves Equation~
(\ref{eq:dmop-alpha-conditional-unbiasedness}).  Taking expectations and
summing the finite sequence proves Equation~
(\ref{eq:dmop-alpha-unbiased-estimating-equation}).
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Data-averaged population-score discrepancy]
\label{prop:dmop-alpha-data-averaged-score-discrepancy}
Suppose the conditions of Proposition~
\ref{prop:dmop-alpha-unbiased-estimating-equation} hold and the conditional
scores are square integrable.  Then
