\begin{align}
&\E_{\theta_0}\left\|
\nabla_\theta\ell^\alpha(\theta_0)
-\nabla_\theta\ell(\theta_0)
\right\|_2^2                                                \notag\\
&\quad=
\sum_{n=1}^{N}\E_{\theta_0}\left\|
\nabla_\theta\ell_n^\alpha(\theta_0)
-\nabla_\theta\ell_n(\theta_0)
\right\|_2^2.
\label{eq:data-averaged-score-orthogonality}
\end{align}
If, in addition, the lagwise bound in Lemma~
\ref{lem:derivative-measure-forgetting}, including its bound on
$G'(\theta_0)$, holds with the same deterministic constants over the support
of the random observations, then
\begin{align}
&\E_{\theta_0}\left\|
\nabla_\theta\ell^\alpha(\theta_0)
-\nabla_\theta\ell(\theta_0)
\right\|_2^2                                                \notag\\
&\quad\leq
CNpG'(\theta_0)^2
\left\{
\frac{\varrho(1-\alpha)}
{(1-\varrho)(1-\alpha\varrho)}
\right\}^2.
\label{eq:data-averaged-population-score-bound}
\end{align}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}[Proof.]
Proposition~\ref{prop:dmop-alpha-unbiased-estimating-equation}, first at
$\alpha$ and then at $\alpha=1$, gives
\begin{align}
&\E_{\theta_0}\left[
\nabla_\theta\ell_n^\alpha(\theta_0)
-\nabla_\theta\ell_n(\theta_0)
\mid Y_{1:n-1}
\right]
=0.
\label{eq:data-averaged-score-difference-centering}
\end{align}
For $m<n$, the time-$m$ score difference is measurable with respect to
$Y_{1:n-1}$.  Therefore the tower property and Equation~
(\ref{eq:data-averaged-score-difference-centering}) give
\begin{align}
&\E_{\theta_0}\left[
\{\nabla_\theta\ell_m^\alpha(\theta_0)
-\nabla_\theta\ell_m(\theta_0)\}^{\mathsf T}
\{\nabla_\theta\ell_n^\alpha(\theta_0)
-\nabla_\theta\ell_n(\theta_0)\}
\right]                                                     \notag\\
&\quad=
\E_{\theta_0}\left[
\{\nabla_\theta\ell_m^\alpha(\theta_0)
-\nabla_\theta\ell_m(\theta_0)\}^{\mathsf T}
\E_{\theta_0}\left\{
\nabla_\theta\ell_n^\alpha(\theta_0)
-\nabla_\theta\ell_n(\theta_0)
\mid Y_{1:n-1}
\right\}
\right]                                                     \notag\\
&\quad=0.
\label{eq:data-averaged-score-cross-term-zero}
\end{align}
Expanding the squared Euclidean norm of the finite sum of conditional
score differences and applying Equation~
(\ref{eq:data-averaged-score-cross-term-zero}) proves Equation~
(\ref{eq:data-averaged-score-orthogonality}).  Under the additional
uniformity condition, Lemma~\ref{lem:dmop-population-discount-bias} gives,
for every $n$,
\begin{align}
\left\|
\nabla_\theta\ell_n^\alpha(\theta_0)
-\nabla_\theta\ell_n(\theta_0)
\right\|_2^2
&\leq
CpG'(\theta_0)^2
\left\{
\frac{\varrho(1-\alpha)}
{(1-\varrho)(1-\alpha\varrho)}
\right\}^2.
\end{align}
Taking expectations and summing this inequality in Equation~
(\ref{eq:data-averaged-score-orthogonality}) proves Equation~
(\ref{eq:data-averaged-population-score-bound}).
\end{proof}

The uniform-observation condition in Proposition~
\ref{prop:dmop-alpha-data-averaged-score-discrepancy} is needed only for
the last deterministic upper bound.  The orthogonality identity requires
only square integrability, and the uniform condition may be replaced by a
random per-time bound whose second moment is uniformly bounded in $n$.
The same proof applied to the fixed-lag tail gives, for $0\leq k<N$,
\begin{align}
&\E_{\theta_0}\left\|
\sum_{n=1}^{N}\sum_{r=k+1}^{n-1}\Delta_{n,r}
\right\|_2^2
\leq
CNpG'(\theta_0)^2
\left\{\frac{\varrho^{k+1}}{1-\varrho}\right\}^2.
\label{eq:data-averaged-fixed-lag-score-bound}
\end{align}
Thus both discrepancies accumulate linearly in $N$.  Combining Equations~
(\ref{eq:mixing-calibrated-geometric-fixed-lag-comparison}),
(\ref{eq:data-averaged-population-score-bound}), and
(\ref{eq:data-averaged-fixed-lag-score-bound}) shows that their
data-averaged upper bounds are comparable up to a fixed factor in the
mixing-calibrated regime described above; the ratio for the squared bounds
is the square of the ratio in Equations~
(\ref{eq:mixing-calibrated-geometric-fixed-lag-comparison})--
(\ref{eq:mixing-calibrated-geometric-fixed-lag-lower-comparison}).

Proposition~\ref{prop:dmop-alpha-unbiased-estimating-equation} does not
say that a realized DMOP-$\alpha$ score equals the realized likelihood
score when $\alpha<1$.  It says that, under the original POMP law in
Equation~(\ref{eq:original-pomp-data-generating-law}), the population
conditional contributions form a martingale-difference estimating equation.
The usual parametric rate therefore does not require $\alpha\to1$, provided
the estimating equation identifies $\theta_0$ and satisfies the regularity
conditions stated next.

\begin{proposition}[Parametric inference for fixed $\alpha$]
\label{prop:dmop-alpha-parametric-inference}
Fix $\alpha\in[0,1]$. Suppose $\theta_0$ is locally identified by the
limiting population estimating equation, a local uniform law of large
numbers holds, and there is a consistent solution
$\widehat\theta_N^\alpha$ of
\begin{equation}
\sum_{n=1}^{N}\nabla_\theta\ell_n^\alpha
(\widehat\theta_N^\alpha)=0,
\label{eq:dmop-alpha-population-estimating-equation}
\end{equation}
where the reference value in the population recursion is set equal to its
parameter argument.  Suppose also that the following limits hold under the
POMP law in Equation~(\ref{eq:original-pomp-data-generating-law}):
\begin{align}
&\frac1{\sqrt N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}
\nabla_\theta\ell_n^\alpha(\theta_0)
\ \Rightarrow\
\mathcal N(0,B_\alpha),
\label{eq:dmop-alpha-martingale-clt}
\\
&-\frac1N\sum_{n=1}^{N}
\nabla_\theta
\{\nabla_\theta\ell_n^\alpha(\theta)\}^{\mathsf T}
\ \stackrel{p}{\longrightarrow}\ A_\alpha
\label{eq:dmop-alpha-sensitivity-limit}
\end{align}
uniformly on a neighborhood shrinking to $\theta_0$, where
$A_\alpha$ is nonsingular.  Then
\begin{equation}
\sqrt N(\widehat\theta_N^\alpha-\theta_0)
\ \Rightarrow\
\mathcal N\left(
0,A_\alpha^{-1}B_\alpha A_\alpha^{-\mathsf T}
\right).
\label{eq:dmop-alpha-sandwich-limit}
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}[Proof.]
Apply the integral form of the multivariate Taylor expansion to
Equation~(\ref{eq:dmop-alpha-population-estimating-equation}):
\begin{align}
0
&=
\frac1{\sqrt N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}
\nabla_\theta\ell_n^\alpha(\theta_0)                         \notag\\
&\quad+
\left[
\frac1N\int_0^1\sum_{n=1}^{N}
\nabla_\theta
\{\nabla_\theta\ell_n^\alpha(
\theta_0+s(\widehat\theta_N^\alpha-\theta_0))\}^{\mathsf T}
\,ds
\right]
\sqrt N(\widehat\theta_N^\alpha-\theta_0).
\label{eq:dmop-alpha-taylor-expansion}
\end{align}
Consistency of $\widehat\theta_N^\alpha$ and the uniform convergence in
Equation~(\ref{eq:dmop-alpha-sensitivity-limit}) imply that the matrix in
square brackets converges in probability to $-A_\alpha$.  Nonsingularity
and continuity of matrix inversion therefore give
\begin{align}
\sqrt N(\widehat\theta_N^\alpha-\theta_0)
&=
A_\alpha^{-1}
\frac1{\sqrt N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}
\nabla_\theta\ell_n^\alpha(\theta_0)
+o_p(1).
\label{eq:dmop-alpha-asymptotic-linearization}
\end{align}
Equation~(\ref{eq:dmop-alpha-sandwich-limit}) follows from
Equation~(\ref{eq:dmop-alpha-martingale-clt}), Slutsky's theorem, and
Equation~(\ref{eq:dmop-alpha-asymptotic-linearization}).
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Mixing-calibrated discounting and parameter inference]
The preceding results have three distinct consequences.

First, suppose $0\leq\alpha<1$ and
$k=\lfloor\alpha/(1-\alpha)\rfloor<N$.  Equations~
(\ref{eq:mixing-calibrated-geometric-fixed-lag-comparison})--
(\ref{eq:mixing-calibrated-geometric-fixed-lag-lower-comparison}) give
\begin{equation}
\frac12
<
\frac{
\dfrac{\varrho(1-\alpha)}
{(1-\varrho)(1-\alpha\varrho)}
}{
\dfrac{\varrho^{k+1}}{1-\varrho}
}
\leq
\varrho^{-k}
=exp\{k|\log\varrho|\}.
\label{eq:summary-mixing-calibrated-comparison}
\end{equation}
Hence, whenever $k|\log\varrho|=O(1)$, the geometric and fixed-lag
scalar upper bounds are within fixed factors.  Equivalently, discount
memory spans only a fixed number of mixing e-folding times.  The same
comparison for the squared upper bounds has fixed factors obtained by
squaring Equation~(\ref{eq:summary-mixing-calibrated-comparison}).

Second, expectation under Equation~
(\ref{eq:original-pomp-data-generating-law}) and martingale orthogonality
give Equation~(\ref{eq:data-averaged-population-score-bound}):
\begin{align}
&\E_{\theta_0}\left\|
\nabla_\theta\ell^\alpha(\theta_0)
-\nabla_\theta\ell(\theta_0)
\right\|_2^2                                                \notag\\
&\quad\leq
CNpG'(\theta_0)^2
\left\{
\frac{\varrho(1-\alpha)}
{(1-\varrho)(1-\alpha\varrho)}
\right\}^2.
\label{eq:summary-data-averaged-population-score-bound}
\end{align}
Thus, as $\alpha\uparrow1$, this contribution is
$O\{N(1-\alpha)^2\}$ rather than the
$O\{N^2(1-\alpha)^2\}$ fixed-observation upper bound in Theorem~
\ref{thm:refined-dmop-alpha-mse}.  The improvement from $N^2$ to $N$
comes from the exact vanishing of all cross-time inner products in
Equation~(\ref{eq:data-averaged-score-cross-term-zero}); it is not an
additional mixing assumption.

Third, for every fixed $\alpha\in[0,1]$, Proposition~
\ref{prop:dmop-alpha-parametric-inference} gives
\begin{equation}
\sqrt N(\widehat\theta_N^\alpha-\theta_0)
\ \Rightarrow\
\mathcal N\left(
0,A_\alpha^{-1}B_\alpha A_\alpha^{-\mathsf T}
\right).
\label{eq:summary-fixed-alpha-parametric-rate}
\end{equation}
Therefore fixed $\alpha$ retains the parametric $N^{-1/2}$ rate.  When
$\alpha<1$, the sandwich covariance may differ from the likelihood
covariance at $\alpha=1$, so the possible cost is statistical efficiency,
not a slower consistency rate.
\end{remark}

At $\alpha=1$, the estimating equation is the likelihood score and the
usual information equality may reduce the sandwich covariance.  For
$\alpha<1$, the covariance in Equation~
(\ref{eq:dmop-alpha-sandwich-limit}) records a possible efficiency loss,
not a loss of the $N^{-1/2}$ parameter rate.  This population
estimating-equation result is independent of the unresolved finite-$J$
genealogy problem.  In particular, Proposition~
\ref{prop:dmop-alpha-parametric-inference} does not assert an $O(N/J)$
finite-particle score bias or variance bound, nor first-order equivalence
between the particle and population roots.  Such a transfer requires
uniform-in-parameter versions of the additional finite-particle estimates
identified after Theorem~\ref{thm:refined-dmop-alpha-mse}.
\fi

\section{Justification for the Choice of Baseline Comparisons}

The reasoning we use in the main text to support the magnitude of our contribution is as follows: (i) iterated filtering methods are the current state of the art, and have been for a decade; (ii) we have demonstrated a substantial advance over iterated filtering.
The main text focuses on demonstrating (ii), while supporting (i) via references.
Listing a few references amounts only to anecdotal evidence, which is not sufficient to persuade someone properly skeptical about the claim.
Therefore, this supplement provides an additional quantitative evaluation.

We count the number of papers published in three top scientific journals ({\it Science}, {\it Nature}, and {\it Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the USA (PNAS)}) that carry out statistical inference for partially observed nonlinear non-Gaussian stochastic dynamic models to address a scientific question by fitting mechanistic models to time series data.
For all methods, papers in these high-profile journals are expected to represent only be the tip of the iceberg, but this count provides a concrete measure of the capability of the methods to handle world-class scientific inference problems.
We could have considered additional journals, but the three we chose are undoubtedly prestigious and their scope covers the full range of scientific endeavor so there is less room for subfield bias.
For iterated filtering, our high-impact application count gives the following:
\begin{enumerate*}
  % \item \citet{trevisin23} % this uses pomp for a simulator
\item \citet{he23}\inlineItemSep
  % \item \citet{griffiths23} % this uses pfilter but not if2
  % \item \citet{gokhale23} % uses pomp for trajectory matching
\item \citet{fox22}\inlineItemSep
\item \citet{subramanian21}\inlineItemSep
  \item \citet{pei21}\inlineItemSep
  \item \citet{li20}\inlineItemSep
  %  \item \citet{borchering21} % uses pomp for a simulator
  \item \citet{giezendanner20}\inlineItemSep
\item \citet{wale19}\inlineItemSep
  % \item \citet{decelles19} % uses pomp for trajectory matching
\item \citet{pons-salort18}\inlineItemSep
\item \citet{ranjeva17}\inlineItemSep
\item \citet{martinez16-pnas} \inlineItemSep
\item \citet{becker16}\inlineItemSep
\item \citet{bakker16}\inlineItemSep
\item \citet{laneri15}\inlineItemSep
\item \citet{blake14}\inlineItemSep
\item \citet{blackwood13}\inlineItemSep
\item \citet{blackwood13b}\inlineItemSep
\item \citet{king08}\inlineItemSep
\end{enumerate*}

A referee advised consideration of an alternative approach by \citet{gu98} that we found to have a  high-impact application count of zero, via Google Scholar.

A referee also recommended comparison with \citet{lindsten14}, for which the high-impact application count is also zero.
\citet{lindsten14} has been employed once in {\it Nature Communications} \citep{calafat18} though that journal did not quite make it onto our elite list.
We also found five applications of iterated filtering in {\it Nature Communications} \citep{ranjeva19,rodo21,santos22,briga24,perez25}.
There is a paper in the Statistics section of {\it PNAS} by \citet{wang22} that demonstrates the method of \citet{lindsten14}, but here we are counting the demonstrated ability of the methodology to generate high-profile scientific papers, not statistical methodology papers.
Otherwise, we would also count \citet{ionides06-pnas} and \citet{ionides15} for iterated filtering. 

This reasoning supports iterated filtering as an empirically justified point of reference, in preference to the approaches of \citet{lindsten14} and \citet{gu98}.
Further, the method of \citet{lindsten14} does not have the plug-and-play property so it cannot readily be applied to our example.
The method of \citet{gu98} would have to be combined with an approach such as \citet{andrieu10} that has known scalability problems.
Therefore, we focus on comparing with iterated filtering and we do not provide a comparison with \citet{lindsten14} or \citet{gu98}.

Benchmark tasks are invaluable for making progress on challenging problems \citep{donoho17}.
The Dhaka cholera data and model have served as such a benchmark since its introduction by \citet{king08}.
Other papers testing methods on this example include \citet{ionides15,fasiolo16,wycoff26}.
Thus, our test is established as a demanding inference problem that challenges the best available methods.
We note that this benchmark challenge contains various difficulties that arise commonly in scientific inquiry but are absent from simple SIR models such as the simulation study considered by \citet{lindsten14}.
Ability to accommodate such features is critical for the methodology to be effective for scientific inquiry.

Iterated filtering has recently been used as a benchmark for new POMP model methodology by \citet{whitehouse23}.
This analysis presented numerical results that claimed to beat iterated filtering, but was subsequently found to contain numerical errors.
Corrected results are favorable for iterated filtering methods \citep{hao24-arxiv,whitehouse25-correction,he25}.

Recent results of \citet{chen25} have extended the previous theoretical understanding of iterated filtering.
The ongoing study of iterated filtering as a state-of-the-art approach to inference for POMP models gives additional support for our decision to use iterated filtering as a benchmark for our new methodology.

\section{Algorithmic Parameters}
\label{appendix:algparams}

We include the full set of algorithmic parameters used for the Dhaka results in Table~\ref{table:algparams}. 
The warm start iterations are IF2 iterations, which are followed by gradient descent (GD) iterations when using IFAD. 
The IF2 cooling rate is the fractional reduction in the parameter perturbations over 50 iterations, so that a cooling rate of 0.5 corresponds to approximately 1\% cooling per iteration.
The random walk standard deviation (RWSD) scales the noise added at each filtering step to each regular parameter.
Initial value parameters (IVPs), that affect the value of the latent process only at time $t_0$, are treated differently: they are perturbed only at time $t_0$, with a larger RWSD. 
Parameters are transformed to a unit scale, for example by taking logarithms or a logistic transformation, so that a single RWSD value works for all parameters.

\begin{table}[H]
\centering
\begin{tabular}{p{4.5cm}cccc}
\toprule
\textbf{Hyperparameter} & \textbf{Standard IF2} & \textbf{IFAD-0} & \textbf{IFAD-0.97} & \textbf{IFAD-1} \\
\midrule
Number of Particles ($J$) & 5,000 & 5,000 & 5,000 & 5,000 \\
Warm-start Iterations & 650 & 60 & 175 & 60 \\
GD Iterations & --- & 225 & 175 & 225 \\
IF2 Cooling Rate & 0.8 & 0.5 & 0.5 & 0.5 \\
Initial RWSD & 0.02 & 0.02 & 0.02 & 0.02 \\
Initial RWSD (IVPs) & 0.16 & 0.16 & 0.16 & 0.16 \\
Gradient Optimizer & --- & Adam & Adam & Adam \\
Momentum ($\beta_1$) & --- & 0.0 & 0.9 & 0.9 \\
LR Schedule & --- & Cosine Decay & Cosine Decay & Cosine Decay \\
Initial LR ($\eta_0$) & --- & 0.1 & 0.1 & 0.1 \\
Warm-up Steps & --- & 10 & 10 & 10 \\
Eval. Particles / Reps & 5,000 / 36 & 5,000 / 36 & 5,000 / 36 & 5,000 / 36 \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\caption{Algorithmic parameters for the global search benchmarking on the Dhaka cholera model.}
\label{table:algparams}
\end{table}

Following some experimentation, IFAD was implemnenteed by plugging the DMOP gradient estimate into the Adam optimizer \citep{kingma14arxiv}.
The momentum parameter in Adam was turned off for IFAD-0, since this low-variance estimate did not reqquire it.
Decreasing the learning rate (LR) was found to be helpful, and we selected the commonly used cosine decay.
Warm-up steps were taken with Adam to stabilize its initial performance and avoid noisy
